# Consideration of funding proposals - Addendum X

## Funding proposal package for FP268

### Summary

This addendum contains the following seven parts:
- a) Funding proposal titled "Scaling-Up Resilience in Africa's Great Green Wall (SUBRAGGW)";
- b) No-objection letter issued by the national designated authority(ies) or focal point(s);
- c) Environmental and social report(s)/disclosure;
- d) Secretary's assessment;
- e) Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment;
- f) Response from the accredited entity to the independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment; and
- g) Gender documentation.

This document is prepared and transmitted for consideration by the GCF Board at a Board meeting. It is published on the GCF public website in its current, unedited form. The GCF Secretariat will make publicly available in accordance with applicable GCF policies.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3.011 | PAGE 3

## The Least Developed Countries (LDCs) of the Great Green Wall (GGW) are among the lowest income countries and the most vulnerable to climate change. They ranked at the bottom of the Human Development Index in 2020 and a large share of their populations live on less than $1 per day. Climate change is already leading to desertification, land degradation of the natural resources they depend on. Agriculture, livestock and forestry activities are the foundation of their economies and more than 70 per cent of rural communities depend directly on rainfed agriculture. The GGW has been identified as a key strategy for addressing climate change in these countries. However, climate change is hitting the region hard, with higher temperatures and an increased frequency of extreme events, such as prolonged droughts, floods and heatwaves. This has led to reduced agricultural productivity, loss of livestock and crops, and the resulting depletion of vegetation and biodiversity has undermined livelihoods and increased vulnerability. This has severely impacted the semi-arid pastoral landscapes, degrading food and nutrition insecurity and compromising the sustainability of livelihoods.

### Proposed Interventions

#### i. Suragawa Programme
The Africa’s Great Green Wall (SURAGAWA) is a USD 231,960,912 multi-country programme to be funded by a USD 150 million grant from the Green Climate Fund (GCF) and USD 71,960,212 from the African Development Bank (AfDB), the Global Environment Facility (GEF), the World Bank and the national governments of eight countries. The SURAGAWA programme is designed to be a flagship initiative that aims to transform Sahelian landscapes by restoring 150 million hectares of degraded lands, creating 10 million jobs and sequestering 250 Mt CO2eq by 2030. The SURAGAWA programme is designed to work in synergy with the Great Green Wall (GGW) initiative and other initiatives that are addressing desertification, land degradation and climate resilience across eight Sahelian countries that are extremely vulnerable to climate change.

#### ii. GCF Africa Forest Landscape Restoration Programme
The GCF Africa Forest Landscape Restoration Programme will support landscape restoration activities as a major contribution to climate change mitigation through carbon sequestration in restored land. The programme will also help to build the capacity of African countries to implement forest landscape restoration (FLR) and to monitor its impact across the SURAGAWA countries and the Pan-African region itself. SURAGAWA will build on FCLRP’s experience with FLR, which has been implemented in 10 countries since 2015. The programme is initially funded by the European Union and Turkey, and scale-up success of practices from these and other recent initiatives will be used to attract additional funding.

#### iii. SURAGAWA proposes to implement three key interventions that are designed to address the technical, organizational and financial constraints that have been identified as major barriers to sustainable land-based livelihoods:
- **up-scale successful land restoration practices in the eight above-mentioned Sahelian countries, using a diversity of native species to increase livelihood resilience while also sequestering carbon;
- **strengthen the capacity of local communities to engage in land restoration and NTFP value chain activities with a particular focus on women and youth; and
- **strengthen the capacity of the Great Green Wall’s regional and national institutions to coordinate implementation, monitor progress and ensure that resources are effectively used. This will be done through resources including engaging with climate change adaptation and mitigation financing mechanisms.

#### iv. The SURAGAWA programme will work with community organizations to build ownership and sustainability and strengthen community awareness and capacity to engage in land restoration and NTFP value chain activities, with a particular focus on women and youth. It will also support the development of sustainable agroforestry and food security value chains benefiting vulnerable communities (livelihoods and food and nutrition security) in the eight countries involved.

#### v. The current proposal is for a 10-year programme that will be implemented over three phases, with specific bottlenecks faced by the target groups in the area targeted by SURAGAWA. This includes establishing linkage between local communities and finance models of value chain financing and de-risking lending to smallholders and producer organizations in the region.


---
**Page 10**
---

Climate impacts/benefits

* The SURAGAWA programme will make a direct contribution to the African Union’s GGR targets, helping to realize the EU’s commitment to its pledge at the One Planet Summit (January 2021) and catalyse changes needed to achieve these ambitious targets. The proposed programme will restore approximately 1,273 ha of degraded lands, which is equivalent to 5% of the total area covered by the GGR area* (Area 23) while sequestering 65.1 million tons of CO₂ over the programme lifespan of 20 years**.
* The programme is aligned with the African Union Green Wall Initiative strategy and the “leaf” indicator of the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs). It will contribute to SDG 15 (Life on land), SDG 8 (Decent work and economic growth) as well as associated Sustainable Development Goals, including SDG 3 which is aimed at eradicating poverty, SDG 7 (Affordable and clean energy), SDG 9 (Industry, innovation and infrastructure), SDG 10 (Reduced inequalities), SDG 12 (Responsible consumption and production) and SDG 17 (Partnerships for the Goals).
* Investments in improving land and natural resource management get to the heart of what many communities in the Savel view as both their most pressing human security concerns, and the factors that contribute to persistent conflict. The programme will also support the implementation of the African Union’s Agenda 2063. These chains will thus contribute to climate change mitigation and adaptation, but also to community-based peace-building.

* * Areas 23 provides details of the calculation approach for direct and indirect beneficiaries.
* * * This estimate is based on a restoration target of 15% of the total GGR area. As it takes into account a slight increase in biomass emissions due to the fodder production contribution of the restored land, see Annex A: carbon impact potential assessment (April 2020) and the Green Climate Fund Document, AU, Senegal, 31.7.2020.
* * * All countries which are also GGR countries benefiting from SURAGAWA are the following: Burkina Faso, Chad, Mali, Niger, Nigeria, Senegal.

### B.1 Climate context (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages)

#### Climate change problem[1]

The eight countries of the Great Green Wall (GGW) included in the SURAGOWA[2] programme are among the world's most vulnerable to those most vulnerable to climate change.[3] These countries rely on agriculture (including cropping, livestock, forestry and fisheries) as a key source of their food supply, income and employment.[4] The GGW states face increasing temperatures, more frequent and intense droughts, reduced rainfall variability, increased soil erosion, and a considerable share of it has been degraded and continues to lose its potential for providing the many ecosystem services that are essential for human well-being. ## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.311.0 | PAGE 7

### Topography, Vegetation and Economy

24 CREDIS LINNOR: Human Cost of Disasters: An Overview of the Last 5 Years 2000 - 2015 / 2016 - 2015. CREDI (2000). Saving lives and reducing conflict in the Sahel region, RefNet link: https://refnetweb.org/repository/bahina-saving-lives-and-reducing-conflict-in-the-sahel-region/

26 Information on FAO's Climate Adaptation in Rural Development (CARD) Assessment Tool is available from: http://www.fao.org/3/a-i4051e.pdf

* * *

The Sahel is highly exposed to climate change, yet impacts vary across different regions. The Sahel will increasingly become hotter, with some areas experiencing increased, but more erratic rainfall. Extreme weather events, including droughts and floods, are expected to increase in frequency and intensity. These extreme weather events have led to countries with the highest share of the populations affected by natural hazard-related disasters between 2000 and 2015 (including droughts) being located in West Africa. The Sahel is also highly vulnerable to climate change, which will reduce food availability and farmers' incomes while increasing competition over diminishing natural capital leading to conflicts. According to the World Bank's Climate Change Vulnerability Index (CCVI), the GWDW ADA Climate Adaptation in Rural Development Assessment Tool® indicated that the production of the main crops in the targeted countries is expected to decrease by 10 per cent, groundnut by 11 per cent and rice by 8 per cent over the next 20 years. The tool shows that an additional 3 million people will be food insecure by 2050 compared with a baseline scenario.

In Burkina Faso, the yields of ground nut and sugar beet will be the most affected. In Chad, wheat, soy and ground nuts are expected to decrease in yield by more than 10 per cent over the next 20 years while rice is expected to decrease by 10 per cent. In Mauritania, ground nuts, millet, wheat and sugar beet will be the most affected. In Niger, wheat yields are expected to go down significantly every year following by soy and groundnuts. In Nigeria, wheat is also expected to be impacted. The yield of maize in Senegal is expected to decrease by 10 per cent over the next 20 years (it is expected to increase every year: Annex A gives country level analysis). These yield reductions are expected to have negative impacts on food security and nutrition in the Sahel region, with a particular impact on children's health and food insecurity and poverty. According to the Ecological Threat Report 2021, climate change also has far-reaching implications for human security and conflict. The report highlights that climate change is already causing insecurity in the GWDW area, which is increasingly transcending the capacity of each country to manage these issues alone.

The topography of the Sahel is mostly flat. Altitudes mostly range between 200 and 400 m in elevation, while most of Senegal and the coastal areas of Mauritania lie at sea level. Some isolated mountain areas with an elevation between 1,000 and 1,500 m are found in Mali, Niger, Chad, and Sudan. The Sahara Desert is a vast desert region separating the Sahel from the north and tropical savannahs to the south. It comprises several quite diverse agro-ecological zones, which divide the Sahel into ecological sections, ranging from a mainly dry climate in the north to a semi-arid climate in the center, with increasing rainfall towards the south. The vegetation varies according to space and time. At national level, some countries, such as Nigeria, have abundant water resources, while others, such as Burkina Faso, are characterized by scarce water availability.

Nigeria is the most populous country with 271 million inhabitants, followed by Ethiopia (114 million), Chad (19 million), and Niger (18 million). The Sahel lies at the heart of Africa, home to some of the fastest growing societies in the world with high population growth rates between 2% and 3%, which is expected to continue for decades. In addition, the Sahel has a young population structure: 65% are below the age of 15**.

8. The economic growth rate varies widely within the region and GDP per capita ranges from USD 421 in Chad to 787 in Libya. In most countries, agriculture is the most important sector and provides livelihoods for the majority of the population. The agriculture sector's contribution to Gross Domestic Product (GDP) varies from 2.6% in Djibouti to 50% in Chad. Against a recent general economic recession due to the COVID-19 pandemic, the region is marked by significant food insecurity and malnutrition. This situation is exacerbated by climate change, which has led to droughts and is based on the export of primary goods. The region offers great potential considering the abundant natural resources and its strategic location in Africa. However, the economic development of most countries in the Sahel are affected by multiple interrelated crises. According to the World Bank Poverty Headcount Ratio, large parts of the population live below the poverty line. In addition, 40% of the population aged between 15-29 years old lives in extreme poverty and as a consequence, most countries in the Sahel rank among the lowest on the Human Development Index.

Vulnerability of the Livelihoods and Ecosystems to Climate Change

9. The livelihoods and ecosystems in the Sahel are extremely vulnerable to climate change. Agriculture, the dominant economic activity in most countries, is highly dependent on rainfall as it is the only available source of water.[2] Agriculture is further restricted by up to 50% of rainfall evaporating before crops can make use of it.[3] Spots and droughts are the primary reason for crop failures, especially when they occur during the growing season. The frequency and intensity of extreme weather events such as heat waves or heavy rainfalls are increasing, leading to a decrease in agricultural productivity. In addition, the availability of water is decreasing, highlighting an increase in the intensity and/or frequency of agricultural droughts.[4] Use of irrigation systems is also limited by the lack of infrastructure and access to electricity for pumps. This leads to overexploitation from wells or rivers, making irrigation impractical in large areas of the Sahel.[5] This makes agricultural production even more vulnerable as it becomes increasingly difficult to adapt to climate change.[6] The northern Sahel is drier than its southern counterpart due to its proximity to the Sahara biomes, consequently being more vulnerable to climate change. It is thus dominated by transhumant pastoralism and some farming of drought-resistant sorghum and millet.[7]

## Livestock systems in the Sahel

### Figure 2 - Livestock Systems in the Sahel

| System Type | Description |
| --- | --- |
| Nomadic pastoralism | Herds of livestock are moved seasonally to follow pasture and water resources, with herders following a seasonal pattern. |
| Semi-nomadic pastoralism | Herds of livestock are moved seasonally to follow pasture and water resources, but herders do not move as far or as frequently as in nomadic pastoralism. |
| Sedentary pastoralism | Herds of livestock remain within the same area year-round, with herders tending to a fixed herd of animals. |

### 10. Potential for agricultural expansion and intensification is low
Agriculture in the southern Sahel is more diversified, including crops such as groundnuts, cassava, rice, and oilseeds (e.g., cotton) and sugarcane as well as extensively used forest and parkland areas comprised of natural vegetation. Soil quality is a prominent yield-limiting factor in the Southern Sahel. In general, soil has low availability of Nitrogen and Phosphorus, and poor water holding capacity. As yields have stagnated over historically periodic periods of growth, the needs of a rapidly growing urban population and an increased market orientation of agriculture are creating pressure on land use. This is further exacerbated by the fact that many farmers are not well-versed in sustainable agricultural practices such as crop rotation or intercropping, and instead continue to practice short-term monoculture systems. In addition, soil degradation has become a major problem due to previous droughts and trends from anthropogenic factors have found it difficult to separate the two. Overgrazing and land abandonment are also common issues that contribute to soil degradation.

### 11. In the absence of proposed interventions
The vulnerability of the area to both the anthropogenic pressures and climate risks is high, with a significant risk of desertification and land degradation due to droughts and floods which make it increasingly harder for the local population to sustain subsistence agricultural practices. Extreme weather events such as droughts and floods are becoming more frequent and severe, particularly the impact of climate change is straining the relationship of herders and pastoralists and thus also ethnic relations. For cammerals, pastoralists have crossed the Sahel following seasonal patterns, which allowed them to lead their herds.

The sharp increase in population pressure on land resources has led to competition over land and resources between herders and farmers, which in turn further fuel displacement dynamics. Climate variability

### Table A: Summary of observed and projected climate hazards in the eight Sahelian countries and their impacts on livelihoods of rural populations.

#### Climate hazards (statistically significant/observed)
- **Temperature:** Increasing temperatures, particularly extreme heat events.
- **Precipitation - Drought:** High interannual variability, increasing anomalies in distribution, and a shift towards the cropping season.
- **Precipitation - Heavy rainfalls and floods:** Observed and projected heavy rainfall events.

#### Climate impacts (future)
- **Production and harvest:** Future yield projections of crops and pasture production systems due to higher temperatures and more frequent extreme heat events. Reduced annual growth rate, increased competition for water resources, and increased concentration of contaminants in soil.
- **Future climate impacts:**
  - Increased demand for irrigation water.
  - Higher temperatures and dry spells leading to reduced crop damage during the growing season.

### Related Programmes and Project Interventions

#### Investments in improving land and natural resource management
12. Investments in improving land and natural resource management get at the heart of what many communities view as both their most pressing Human security issues, and the factors that contribute to perfect competition and conflict.
The programme's investments in land restoration and support related to smallholder value chains will thus contribute not only to climate mitigation and adaptation, but also to peace building through community-based approaches with a territorial lens.

#### Related Programmes and Project Interventions

There are a large number of programmes and projects designed to address the issues in the Sahel. To address these increasingly complex and interrelated challenges and support the transition towards climate resilient, low emission agriculture and land management practices, the Green Climate Fund (GCF) has been working with a wide range of actors.
The objectives were to address land degradation, climate change adaptation and mitigation, and protect biodiversity and forest ecosystems in the Sahel region. To achieve these goals, the GCF has supported several initiatives aligned with its strategic agenda and a multi-stakeholder dialogue has been established to ensure country ownership. It has also created opportunities for collaboration between countries and stakeholders on the ground.
The One Planet Summit in January 2021, the launch of the Great Green Wall Accelerator was announced to help meet the GCF's objective of mobilizing $5 billion by 2030 to support the implementation of the Great Green Wall initiative. For the Great Green Wall (GGW) with support from UNCCD. It aims to facilitate collaboration among donors and stakeholders in the Sahel region, including countries, civil society organizations, and private sector actors.
The GCF has also supported several initiatives that aim at addressing the impact of land degradation. ### B

A host of international development agencies and bilateral donors also have a long list of projects that are being implemented in the GOW area. SURAGAWA will build on FAD’s long-lasting support to Sahelian countries to achieve the goals set out by the Green Climate Fund (GCF) for West Africa, including Mali, Niger, Senegal, and Turkey; and to scale-up successful practices from the GFWD-World Bank African Development Bank Programme for project design and implementation. The GCF is also supporting a number of projects in the region that are being implemented by FAD for sustainable gum Arabic production and food security. A 2012 evaluation of AID by FAO showed that the project was successful, with an increase in the income of farmers from gum arabic production, as well as efforts to plan land restoration and manage forest and land resources. Livelihood improvements were found in all countries, with a significant increase in the number of people involved in the production and marketing of non-timber forest products.

### GAF’s contributions and Investments in GOW through the GCF

The GCF is currently implementing 10 projects in the region, including the current SURAGAWA project. The FAO GCF-GEAMS project in Burkina (2014) and four Readiness projects developed and implemented respectively in Burkina Faso (2015), Mali (2016), Niger (2017), and Senegal (2018). These projects are designed to ensure coordination and complementarity with other GCF interventions, the collection, exchange and application of lessons learned from these projects will be shared among all GCF members. The Readiness project in Burkina Faso is the overall impact of this GCF transferrable programme and encourage collaboration among the countries in the region at a Cost-Effective Model (CEM) level. The GCF has invested over US$10 million to support the development of new projects for the Sahel region. In response to demands of the countries in the region, the GCF has invested over US$800 million in grants and loans to support the implementation of 25 projects in Burkina Faso, Mali, Niger, Senegal and Chad with bilateral donors for projects in the Sahel region. This financing has helped GWP member countries to boost their efforts to promote sustainable land management practices that will help them to increase their resilience and adaptability to climate change.

16. The Sahel’s Dryland ecosystems consist of forests, woodlands, wetlands, grasslands and parklands which include shrubs and trees. These ecosystems provide a wide range of goods and services to people and animal species that help sustain human livelihoods and enhance their resilience, but a considerable part of it has been degraded due to overgrazing, deforestation, desertification, land degradation and climate change. This has led to biodiversity loss in the region’s ecosystems and biodiversity conservation dependent on them. Land restoration generates major climate change adaptation benefits through the range of ecosystem services (provisioning, regulating, cultural and supporting) they provide. These include: maintaining soil fertility; increasing water holding capacity; improving sources of income, whether seasonal or in agricultural years, thus offering a safety net to sustain livelihoods and food security; reducing vulnerability to drought and climate change; enhancing resilience through adaptation when they are properly managed, restored and reduced pressure on vulnerable natural resources such as forests, wetlands and grasslands. Land restoration also helps to improve soil structure and fertility, preserve soil water holding capacity, reduce run-off, protect land and agricultural production from erosion and desertification, increase carbon sequestration in the soil and vegetation, and enhance biodiversity.

At the same time, restoration of degraded dryland ecosystems generates considerable climate change mitigation benefits through increased carbon storage in the soil and vegetation. This is particularly important for the Sahel region where deforestation has led to a significant loss of forest cover and reduced capacity to store carbon dioxide (CO2) from the atmosphere.


17. The Sahelian region is experiencing the full impact of climate change, which has dramatic consequences on the eco-systems and people’s livelihoods. Temperatures in the region are rising 1.5 times faster than in the rest of Africa. This has led to a significant increase in evaporation rates, leading to a decrease in rainfall and an increased frequency of heavy rains and violent thunderstorms. However, the land is too dry to absorb the additional water, resulting in devastating flash floods and mudslides such as the one observed in Niger and Mali in 2019. These events are degrading the natural resources that underpin the agro-pastoral livelihood systems that underpin the economy of much of the area. Land degradation has adverse impacts on the environment, human health, food security, water availability, biodiversity conservation and other problems. The degradation of ecosystems and consequent depletion of vegetation and biodiversity has also resulted in a significant reduction in the carrying capacity of land for livestock grazing and crop cultivation with consequences for the populations who rely mainly on agriculture and livestock. Insufficient rainfall means that crops are not able to grow properly or at all, while livestock die from lack of water and insufficient amounts of feed. This has aggravated food and nutrition insecurity and compromised the sustainability of livelihoods. The green cover in the Sahel region is estimated to have decreased by 30% since the beginning of the 21st century and by the end of the 21st century in some areas of the Sahel. The competition for the use of scarce natural resources between pastoralists, farmers and herders has increased significantly, leading to conflicts and tension between farmers and herders. In some areas, the scarcity of natural resources further aggravates conflict and is leading to governance mechanisms being struggled to deal with the many challenges that confront these fragile environments.


Key Barriers and Constraints

18. Constraints Faced by Smallholder Farmers and Herders: Livelihoods in the Sahel countries heavily depend on the availability of vegetation resources. The state of these resources has been steadily deteriorating in many areas as a result of expanding human settlement and demand for more food and livestock. Frequent droughts, coupled with climate change (increased frequency and intensity of extreme weather events such as flash floods and groundwater), have, along with natural variability, caused the drying up of national and transboundary rivers and lakes, while wind erosion has diminished valuable topsoil too. As a result, the natural vegetation of most of the Sahel

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3110 | PAGE 15

## Box 6 - Key Barriers and Underlying Factors

### Main barriers

- Lack of incentives for communities to manage natural resources sustainably
- Rural communities have limited organizational capacity, financial and technical capacity to engage in land restoration and associated NTFP value chains
- Women have limited knowledge, decision-making power and capital to enable them to invest in their livelihoods and the associated NTFP value chains

### Underlying factors

- Lack of formal tenure security for over-communal lands leading to their overuse.
- Increasing livestock numbers coupled with a decrease in human and livestock populations, aggravated by climate change and desertification, which can lead to the combination of these two key causes.
- Alternative livelihood opportunities resulting from the above.

### Women's barriers

- Low levels of education and limited access to information in the Sahel due to low levels of literacy among women.
- Limited opportunities for education and employment generation.
- Lack of awareness about climate change and its impacts on their lives, which makes it difficult for them to adapt to climate risks by using more adaptive crop varieties or improved livestock breeds.
- Low levels of technical knowledge and skills in land management practices that would increase their resilience.

### Rural communities' barriers

- Limited access to credit and capital for investment decisions-making, limited asset ownership, low levels of education among women, and lack of awareness about climate change and its impacts on their lives.
- Insufficient consideration of gender in rural development policies and programs.

### Small-scale NTFP producers' barriers

- Weak bargaining and negotiating power of small producers due to the informal nature of their production and aggregation process.
- Limited access to credit for the formal private sector to invest in rural areas of the Sahel due to fragmented land production, limited market access, and lack of infrastructure.
- Low risk appetite for lenders to sectors with high-risk and low-reward potential, which is exacerbated by a poor view of growing climate threats and crop failure and losses.

### Weak institutional capacity of GGGW institutions to monitor, evaluate, scale-up and replicate successful projects

- Fragmented nature of agriculture and NTFP production with high transaction costs due to the large number of unorganized producers
- Lack of appreciation of the market potential to innovate for these products and services in order to achieve higher returns


### Absence of clear policies and limited national/local capacity to engage with carbon finance

- Transforming land degradation into national policies and frameworks: land restoration tends to be framed as an environmental issue rather than a development issue
- Lack of policy instruments that can support the scaling up of rural development
- Technological innovation in using advanced tools for land restoration is a relatively new field for which technical expertise is missing in the public sector


### Constraints Faced by Private Sector: The formal private sector has generally been missing from these degraded landscapes because of the limited demand for inputs and technologies that they can supply and the low returns on investment. In contrast, smallholder farmers, pastoralists, agroforestry/agribusiness/food operations, most conventional restoration projects generate returns that are too low to attract private investors.

- There is currently lack of private sector rental markets for scaling up innovative restoration activities
- The formal private sector has been reluctant to invest in land restoration and rehabilitation of the eco-systems. private sector has been known to establish well-functioning ventures in other sectors, but it has not yet established itself as a major player in land restoration


### Constraints Faced by Public Sector: An overview issue appearing in most GGGW countries is weak governance structures and processes for implementing the GGWP activities. Other challenges include the lack of high-level political support for the environmental policy agenda from the governments of the GGGW member states and weak organizational structures and processes for the implementation of the GGWP activities.

- The public sector has limited ability to finance, implement, monitor, evaluate or scale up successful experiences implemented by development agencies and other stakeholders. This is due to a lack of institutional capacity, insufficient resources and coordination they have not adequately resourced the institutions with staff or funds.
- The lack of managed knowledge sharing and learning among GGGW countries, project developers and donors. This is especially true for the GGWP activities where there are no established mechanisms for knowledge sharing and scaling up could accelerate and ensure an efficient expansion of the GGWP’s activities.
- Another key barrier in the public sector is the absence of a clear policy framework that can provide guidance on how to scale up, which hampers the establishment of proper M&E systems at the project and national levels and creates challenges for the effective implementation of the GGWP activities. This is especially true when lessons learned, which is key to avoiding negative developments and capitalize positive results achieved by the projects under the GGGW.

## Main Barriers and DISABLING Interventions

### Main barriers

- Lack of incentives for local communities to manage natural resources sustainably
- Rural communities have limited capacity to participate in land restoration and NTFP value chains
- Women have limited skills, voice, decision-making power, and capital
- Poor market access and value-chain linkages for small-scale NTFP producers
- Lack of adequate financial products and services for smallholder NTFP producers and SMEs
- Weak institutional capacity of government institutions to support restoration and improve livelihoods
- Absence of clear policies and mechanisms to engage with carbon finance

### DISABLING Interventions

- A consolidated approach that organizes and strengthens local communities' capacity to manage natural resources sustainably, and that creates effective mechanisms for restoring degraded lands and landscapes.
- All programme investments in participatory land restoration and NTFP value chains have a strong capacity-building focus
- Gender issues are mainstreamed in all programme activities

### Through the GCF

- Facilitating direct interaction between NTFP producer groups and buyers, including through the introduction of FAID GREENFIN, the programme will ensure better service delivery to smallholder NTFP producers.
- Introduction of test and tested, user-friendly land restoration technologies and practices that are cost-effective for restoration and improve livelihoods
- Strengthening national and regional climate finance frameworks and carbon in policy frameworks and financing mechanisms

### Key strategies

- Increase sequestration and reduce emissions of GHG through sustainable land use management and agroforestry systems (rehabilitation, agroforestry systems, mitigation), and restore degraded lands and landscapes (adaptation);
- Strengthening the resilience of communities to the effects of a changing climate on productive landscapes, by forming non-timber forestry products low-emission value chains;
- Strengthening national NTFP policies and practices, early climate learning information and lessons learned from up-and-scaling restoration experiences.

23. The Theory of Change (ToC) of SURAGAWA is that the participation of local communities along with strengthened land and forest/natural resources are one set conditions for successful adaptation and mitigation efforts in the Drylands. The design of the SURAGAWA programme builds on the experience of the FAO implemented project in 2013-2015, which demonstrated that when communities are provided with incentives motivated to invest their labour and resources (including local knowledge about appropriate species and ways to ensure their regeneration) to achieve sustainable land restoration. The approach promoted by the programme includes: reforestation and afforestation; rehabilitation of degraded lands through tree planting, soil conservation, rehabilitation, water infiltration and soil improvement techniques, including the production and collection of restoration seeds; and the establishment of agroforestry systems. The project also demonstrated that communities have a strong vested interest in preserving and restoring communal forest and land resources. This is because they are directly dependent on these resources for their livelihoods. Communities can use to enhance their livelihoods through direct consumption and through income generation. Adaptation and mitigation efforts will be more effective if they are integrated into the development of vulnerable households with more sustainable livelihoods. The private sector (including local and international firms) has a role in providing technical assistance, financing and marketing for the production and sale of restored land products, thus creating market both locally and regionally. The theory also contends that with targeted support to the Pan-African Seed Network, they can play a more effective role in coordination, resource mobilisation and monitoring and evaluation.

24. The Theory of change of SURAGAWA is based on the following postulate: If the capacity of smallholder farmers and communities to manage their land resources increases through improved access to degraded lands, and improve market linkages and access to financial services; along with strengthened GG institutional capacities, THEN rural households and ecosystems will become more resilient to climate change while reducing CO2 emissions. This will be achieved by increasing the adoption of sustainable land management practices, sustainable production and marketing of NTFPs, along with increased capacity of GG institutions and private sector actors to provide technical assistance, financing and market linkages for the sale of restored lands, thus meeting both climate change adaptation and mitigation objectives over the long term.

25. The concerted impact of the investments that are being proposed under the SURAGAWA programme will be to increase the adaptive capacity of rural households in the Drylands through improved access by governments and communities to become aware of the potential of the investments to protect their natural environment, improve livelihoods and reduce poverty, while also increasing the opportunities for sustainable employment. The pathways being designed under SURAGAWA form the core of its ToC and are outlined below:

26. Pathways to Eco-System and Landscape Restoration: Local governments and communities do not have the resources or capacity to restore degraded lands on a large scale. However, they can contribute by providing a combination of climate change impacts and poor land use practices. The farming and herding communities are scattered and not well organized to develop and sustainably use the common lands. There is limited financial and technical assistance available for them to do so. The smallholders are also not able to use their resources in an efficient manner due to lack of access to information, extension services, credit facilities, the harm climate factors and limited resources. However, the pathway to rehabilitation of the natural resource base through community participation will be more effective if it is supported by the availability of technology and technical solutions which can act as restoration at the land scale.

27. The experience of AAD and a range of other investments in large scale land restoration has shown that the local communities are not able to restore degraded lands on their own, but they have the potential and capacity to do so on a sustainable basis. Furthermore, the local authorities can work with local communities in a participatory way to develop and implement strategies for restoring degraded lands. The pathway to rehabilitation of the land through the local authorities and communities to modern equipment for mechanical ploughing on the vast lands; training farmers and herders in soil conservation, soil management and afforestation techniques. The communities can also be organized to collect the seeds of appropriate species that can preserve or enhance the biodiversity of the eco systems and engaged in seed production and marketing. This will help them to restore degraded land, prevent desertification, preserve the eco-systems by scaling-up successful restoration practices with well-adapted native

In addition to these climate change adaptation benefits, the regeneration of these areas can make a significant contribution to carbon sequestration and mitigation. The restoration of degraded lands in the Sahel region through NTFP-based projects will provide an additional incentive to increase the biomass in the area through direct paid payments for carbon off-sets. It will be important to build these links over time and SURAGWIA, working in synergy with other investments such as the proposed GREENFUND initiative, is co-help to strengthen the institutional arrangements necessary for the purpose.

## 28. Pathways to Enhancing Livelihoods

The development of degraded lands can provide an important source for livelihoods through NTFP production and marketing. This provides a strong economic incentive to local communities to manage the lands and sustain the investments. The experience from AAD shows that the most immediate benefit that communities achieve is the increase in their income from the sale of products derived from these lands which can be an important source of income. The most immediate benefit that communities achieve is the increase in their income from the sale of products derived from these lands which can be an important source of income. Livestock is an important element of livelihoods in the Sahel and the increased availability of grasses and shrubs for livestock grazing will provide a significant economic benefit to local communities. The experience builds on the experience that improving non-timber forest product value chains provides a key incentive for local communities to manage their lands sustainably. This is particularly true when there are opportunities for the marketing of NTFPs in domestic, regional and export markets as there is an untapped and growing demand for these products.

## 29. Pathway to Accessing Financial Services

The SURAGWIA programme recognizes that smallholders have limited opportunities to access formal credit due to the low presence of formal financial institutions in the targeted area. The programme will provide a range of services to support smallholder access to finance, including for smallholders and non-timber forest product (NTFP) value chains. Individuals and SMEs often do not possess the documentation required to open bank accounts or access credit. They lack collateral or other mechanisms to guarantee their ability to repay loans. ## Knowledge sharing and monitoring tools: Changes in land use, especially communal lands, and reforms of government structures that enable greater decentralization and local control of natural resources can be significant variables for the success of climate change adaptation interventions.

### Increased coordination among the CGIW institutions, strengthening of the monitoring capacity, updating the situation-based use of innovative models to monitor CGIW violations. With such improved coordination and strengthening, the CGIW will be better positioned to provide timely information on CGIW violations and support implementation of larger scale climate action investments, as well as creating knowledge structures for sustainable land management in the CGIW.

### Figure 2 - Theory of Change

**If the capacity of CGIW institutions is strengthened through a combination of improved monitoring tools and innovative models, then CGIW will be better positioned to detect change.**

**Outcome:** Increased coordination among CGIW institutions, strengthening of the monitoring capacity, updating the situation-based use of innovative models to monitor CGIW violations.

### 31 - The large majority of the eight SURAGIGWA countries, highly diverse, do share a considerable number of water resources and are vulnerable to climate change impacts. These include increased temperatures, reduced accumulated reference evapotranspiration, sizeable reductions in the climatic water balance, and the 5-, 6- and 7-month dry seasons. The eight SURAGIGWA countries also face significant challenges related to land degradation and desertification.

- These areas host a vast biodiversity of plant and animal species, providing essential ecosystem services such as pollination, pest control, soil formation, water purification, and carbon sequestration.

### 3.3 SURAGWA will consist of three components designed to help make rural livelihoods more resilient to climate change:
1. **Component 1: Large scale landscape restoration to increase climate change resilience and mitigation**.
2. **Component 2: Strengthening the role of non-timber forest products (NTFP) value chains benefiting vulnerable communities' livelihoods**, which will address the challenges rural communities face in accessing markets and financial services.

### Component 3: Strengthening CGIAR institutions at country and regional level, which will focus on institutional coordination, results monitoring, resource mobilization and knowledge management and sharing.

> about a new paradigm shift through providing critical support in a sustainable manner. The trade-offs or the opportunity cost associated with some of the interventions and the rationale for the specific set of interventions chosen are discussed.

34. Additionally, the set of interventions across the land restoration and NTFP value chain development components will leverage synergies with a targeting approach that builds upon the identification of programme intervention areas through participatory mapping exercises (refer to Table 9 for Component 1 selection criteria). This includes a prioritization exercise including through remote sensing and drones, to ensure consistency with the climate resilience goals. The prioritization exercise will also include the identification of priority NTFP value chains with existing production in the identified restoration areas. The prioritization exercise will be conducted during the programme design and preparation phase after which specific investment sites, including both highly degraded communal lands and individual private landholdings, are selected for restoration (refer to Table 8). Through a participatory community-based approach taking into consideration gender equality, social inclusion and conservation of cultural heritage, the selection process is designed to ensure that communities are involved in preparation with the understanding that the participatory approach that will be used for restoration site selection might provide opportunities for the development of new NTFP products and expansion of existing NTFP producer groups (refer to Table 10 for Component 2 selection criteria).

35. SURAGWUA uses a strategic approach for beneficiary selection that prioritizes communities most vulnerable to land degradation, poverty, and climate change. The programme will also ensure that beneficiaries have demonstrated commitment to sustainable land management. The selection process is focused on the availability of degraded lands in terms of size and quality, as well as the existence of existing NTFP production or other livelihood activities, including in-kind contributions such as land and labour, the non-existence of unresolved land issues and/or inter-ethnic conflicts, and the willingness to participate in the programme. Furthermore, the programme will engage smallholder farmers, pastoralists, and local cooperatives who are directly affected by land degradation and climate change. The selection process is designed to ensure that preference is given to communities that have experience with agroforestry, soil and water conservation, or traditional land management practices. In addition, the programme will prioritize women’s groups as they can be key actors, ensuring that women, youth, and marginalized groups have equitable access to programme benefits. The selection process also ensures that the participation of local cooperatives and community-based organizations will help ensure that beneficiaries have secure land tenure, reducing the risk of dislocation or eviction. By aligning with the principles of gender equality, social inclusion, and social sustainability goals, the programme can maximize its environmental impact and long-term viability.

## Project Phase

### Land Restoration/Investment Site Selection

| Type | Criteria | Meaning of Verification |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Climate | The site is impacted by climate extremes and natural disasters, has a high vulnerability to climate change, and has a limited rainfall date or very low rainfall. There are no records by village elders. | Photographs, recent record of extreme weather events, and the presence of the majority of the community members at the local government agency during the meeting and a resolution signed by the women leaders. |
| Physical | There is at least a minimum parcel of 30 hectares (74 acres) of land with good soil fertility. | Photographs, names of species, photographs of the area, and records prepared in consultation with local community members. |
| Technical | A site visit by technical experts indicates that the selected site can benefit from the menu of restoration activities proposed for implementation within the program. | Photographs, names of species, photographs of the area, and records prepared in consultation with local community members. |
| Population | There are at least 40 minimum households who have a land tenure agreement signed by all parties involved. | A record of Huts will be maintained to show that there is no conflict over land ownership. |

Based on the availability of resources to climate change adaptation and restoration, the selection criteria for the project sites are as follows:

- Based on the presence of a majority of women in the community who have signed a resolution at the local government agency during the meeting and a resolution signed by the women leaders.

## Selection criteria of the beneficiaries for component 2: A number of criteria has been elaborated to guide the programme in selecting the most appropriate sites for medium-land degraded lands (MLDLs) for restoration projects, and distributors and processors of non-timber forest resources (NTTPR) and fodder – referred to collectively as NTTPY value chain actors. The selection process will be based on a competitive tendering procedure. In order to select the criteria, they will require some flexibility and adaptation, taking into account countries’ contexts and specificities. For example, ensuring that women have equal access to NTTPY value chain opportunities (see criterion b below) will require specific measures in the selection process. The same applies for other groups of beneficiaries who may be subject to some additional conditions that would be elaborated in the different beneficiary countries according to their specificities.

## B

### Producer systems and/or resilient producers

On-farm and/or tree resources for their livelihoods.

Involvement in restoration of degraded is considered a priority.

Availability of the natural resources (for materials, biomass, etc.) and other relevant resources required to produce the NTFP resources selected for the value chain. Each project should have a clear understanding of the availability of these resources.

Market demand requirements: Beneficiaries should be able to meet market demand requirements/quality standards for the NTFP products they are producing. This includes meeting consumer expectations, consumption, improving their own food and nutrition security, as well as providing other benefits to targeted end users (including domestic and export markets). The project should also ensure that the NTFP products produced meet the required quality standards.

Sustainability: Beneficiaries should be committed to social and environmental sustainability in production, processing, and marketing of the NTFP products. This involves practices such as conservation and enhancement of biodiversity and ecosystem services, and equitable sharing of benefits among groups involved in the value chain. The project should include both men and women, including youth, with equal access to NTFP value chain opportunities and training, particularly targeting socially disadvantaged or vulnerable groups.

Economic viability: Beneficiaries should be able to generate a profit from their production activities. This requires a comprehensive business case of the costs involved in producing the selected NTFPs within the selected value chains and the ability to manage these resources effectively.

Commitment: The beneficiaries should commit themselves to participating actively throughout the life of the programme (to maximise opportunities). Projects should also ensure that beneficiaries find a suitable way of engaging the beneficiaries.

#### Component 1: Large-scale landscape restoration to increase climate change resilience and mitigation, and for by local communities.

##### Outcome 1.1: Highly and moderately degraded lands restored under climate-resilient management practices.
##### Outcome 1.2: GHG emissions reduced, avoided or removed/sequestered
##### Output 1.1: Local community groups organized, trained and knowledgeable in land restoration activities and monitoring

38. In each programme area, specific climate data analysis and GIS mapping will be undertaken to identify preliminary sites for restoration. The programme will then work with communities to prioritize and select highly degraded common lands and moderately degraded farmlands according to the specific criteria set out in Tables 7 and 8. Following this, a Memorandum of Understanding (MoU) will be signed between the project management unit and the local community groups, which is then formalized through locally acceptable agreements. The MoUs will include a commitment by the communities to participate fully with technical resources whose capacity will be developed. Communities will then take responsibility for (i) providing labour for planting and maintenance activities; (ii) ensuring that all planted trees are protected from grazing animals; and (iii) overseeing the seedling/planting, monitoring and maintenance activities, and manage the benefits and proceeds generated by the restoration projects. The MoUs will also include a commitment to ensure full participation of women and youth in the committee and in its leadership positions, given their key role in the establishment and maintenance of restored areas.

##### Activity 1.1.1: Identify potential sites for restoration and to inform resource base for value chain planning based on analysis of climate data, GIS maps and potential for impact with local authorities and community

39. The purpose of this activity is to assist communities and local authorities to work together on identifying specific restoration areas, and their potential linkages with NTFP value chain development. The project will generate the necessary information through a combination of field visits, interviews, focus group discussions, climate data analysis and by generating GIS Maps that will be used for consultations with local community groups and authorities. This activity will define priorities for restoration and for NTFP value chains (sub-activity 1.1.1).

##### Activity 1.1.2: Identify and mobilize communities for participation selection on the specific sites, ensuring inclusion of women.

#### Outputs 1.1 through 5 under this component do not have grant making or cash transfers elements to the communities involved.

40. This activity will start with consultations of the local communities having expressed an interest in restoration and in NTFP production (sub-activity 1.1.2.1). Identified by sub-activity 1.1.1 above, Subcontractor-managed NGOs and service providers (SPs) will facilitate the mobilization of local households into community groups (CG) and provide technical assistance to ensure that CG identify appropriate restoration and NTFP production activities and customer/tenants rights applying to these sites in a sustainable and long-term basis provided by CGI (sub-activity 1.1.2). Community restriction committees – including separate community women’s groups (CWG) where necessary – will be established at the village level to ensure that CGs are able to manage their restoration activities on land of moderately degraded individually/family-owned, productive organizations will be established (sub-activity 1.1.2.4). To facilitate the development of these CGs and CWGs, a series of training sessions for local communities will be held to support the development of gender and safeguards related documents (sub-activity 1.1.2.5).

The process for engagement of local communities will build on best practices used by the CGI Land Tenure and restoration teams in their work with local communities in other countries, including the establishment of a community governance of tenure and engaging groups with status as well as groups with customary (formal/Informal) tenure and land rights. The CGI Land Tenure and restoration teams will also provide technical assistance to support the land and natural resources, as identified in the Technical Guide on the Integration of the Voluntary Guidelines on the Responsible Governance of Tenure for the Implementation of the United Nations Convention to Combat Desertification and Land Degradation Neutrality.

### Activity 1.1.3: Train government’s restoration teams and community management organizations

45. The programme will follow a “training of trainers” approach, first at national level, and then moving down to community level, where village technicians will be identified among the membership of the community-level Restoration Committees (sub-activity 1.1.3.1). This training will focus on the development of skills for strengthening the capacities of community members in land restoration techniques has been successfully applied in Sanelian land restoration efforts, including CGI, by CGI and by other organizations.

45. In order to implement this activity, the programme will select candidates for becoming field trainers at national level from among CGI’s Land Tenure and Restoration teams (sub-activity 1.1.3.2). These trainers will be trained in a Farmer Field School (FFS) and on conservation, protection and land restoration techniques (sub-activity 1.1.3.1). Once trained, these trainers will then train village technicians at the community level using CGI’s FFS model, focusing on soil conservation and protection and land restoration techniques (sub-activity 1.1.3.2).

### Output 1.2: Native seed supply systems strengthened to ensure the availability of genetically appropriate seeds that provide increased climate resilience.

46. Good physiological quality (capacity for germination and propagation) and quantity of seeds and seedlings of well-adapted native species are critical factors in ensuring successful restoration outcomes, especially when large-scale interventions in the Great Green Wall countries. There is a significant shortage of good seeds from wild native species that can be used to restore degraded land. In response to this need, CGI’s Land Tenure and Restoration project, initiated a large-scale effort in Niger, Mali, Burkina Faso and Nigeria to produce restoration seeds. This effort has been successful in producing over 10 million seeds of 25 native tree species. CGI will continue to work with research institutes and rural communities associations. Committees will be trained and organized for the collection and conservation of native seeds (germplasm) from wild native trees, shrubs and grasses. It is expected that native seed (germplasm) supply systems will set up to ensure supply of around 2,000 tons of restoration seeds per year in each of the four countries involved in land restoration sites. Communities will be encouraged to develop small private business for gathering wild native seeds and establishing and caring for native species nurseries, to be used in their village and farm plots and beyond.


---
**Page 35**
---

### Activity 1.2.3: Identify and train community technicians on good quality and quantities of restoration seeds and native seed supply

45. The purpose of this activity is to organize community members involved in seed collection and storage, and build their capacity for seed production and distribution.

B Subsequently, the programme will provide technical assistance for the formation of community-level seed supply groups (sub-activity 1.2.3.1) and training workshops will be cascaded down from community technicians to community members under the next activity, 1.2.2.


### Activity 1.2.4: Identify and organize train community members involved in seed and seedling production for land restoration activities

46. This activity is essential for scaling up local communities' capacity for land restoration at affordable cost. It involves a two-stage process: first the training of community groups on seed collection, storage and seedling production (sub-activity 1.2.2.1) and then the training of communities and equipping them with seed collection, treatment and storage kits to prepare seeds for restoration by seeding and planting (sub-activity 1.2.2.2).


### Activity 1.2.3.1: Establish community groups for seed production

47. Many restoration activities will be implemented through direct seeding. But where the context is suitable, especially in terms of the availability of water sources, community nurseries can make an important contribution to efforts to ensure a sustainable supply of high-quality seeds and seedlings. To this end, the programme will establish and equip community groups to operate nurseries and vegetable gardens, and to collect and store seeds for restoration activities (sub-activity 1.2.3.1). The community groups will be trained in nursery management and trained to maintain managed these nurseries and use the seed collection equipment and storage facilities effectively (sub-activity 1.2.3.2). Finally, the programme will create a network of community restoration sites (activity 1.2.3) for exchange visits.


### Activity 1.2.4.1: Local communities' seed supply of native seeds is integrated into national system seed

48. The purpose of this activity is to connect the local community nurseries that have been established and supported under sub-activity (1.2.4.1) with the national seed system, so as to ensure a sustainable supply of high-quality seeds for preserving and further disseminating good quality seeds and can thus strengthen the national restoration seed supply, especially in terms of locality and nationality. This will also help to improve the performance of other GGG restoration efforts, but also to provide a source of seed for local communities.


### Output 1.3: Targeted highly degraded lands restored through arrangements for rainwater harvesting and enhanced soil permeability and through local communities engaging in planting of seeds and direct seeding

50. On highly degraded lands, where natural regeneration is blocked by the poor condition of the eroded soil and lack of seed sorbent, the programme will carry out large scale mechanized land preparation to increase soil permeability for perennial grasses and shrubs (sub-activity 1.3.2). This will be followed by direct seeding or planting of seeds, enabling seeds and/or seedlings to grow and establish in the dry and harsh conditions in the Sahel. Building up the biodiversity of these degraded lands will help to restore the ecological balance of local communities and landscapes in such extreme conditions, while restored areas will also act as important carbon sinks.


51. However, due to the advanced level of degradation of those lands, experiences drawn from AAD have shown that heavy mechanized interventions are needed to ensure positive results, as manual preparation methods are not efficient enough for these degraded lands (sub-activity 1.3.2). In addition, the costs associated with mechanized interventions (including land preparation, seeding/planting) are found to be cheaper than all the manual methods

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL_V3.10.1_PAGE 30

## Activity 1.3.1: Highly degraded land restoration plans prepared and implemented through mechanized, animal, and manual traction techniques

As part of this activity, there is a significant investment from the project (financial, for mechanized ploughing) and from the local communities (labour), for non-mechanized soil preparation, and seeding and planting of considerable areas of highly degraded lands. Therefore, it requires prior agreement on site-specific restoration plans, which will be developed in close collaboration with the communities and stakeholders involved under Output 1.1. Following agreement on the site-specific restoration plans, private sector service providers - with an emphasis on smallholder farmers - will be engaged to implement these plans through mechanized deep ploughing of agreed restoration sites (sub-activity 1.3.1.2). These larger scale land preparation activities are expected to take place in March/April, ensuring that the restoration sites are ready for rainwater harvesting, seeding and planting as the rainy season sets in.

## Activity 1.3.2: Seeding and planting in prepared sites with communities/villages

Following the completion of site preparation activities, local communities will engage in direct sowing of seeds from prioritized native grass fodder and woody species and in planting of seedlings coming from village nurseries (sub-activity 1.3.2.1). The costs of these restoration operations will be generated through activities under Output 1.2 above.

## Activity 1.3.3: Training of community members in monitoring & maintenance

The monitoring and maintenance of seeded and planted sites is essential for obtaining optimum restoration results. Therefore, local communities will be trained for management (including protection through physical means or social fencing) of the restored lands. This training will also include the establishment of a monitoring plan for the monitored sites during two years following planting/seeding (sub-activity 1.3.3.2).

## Output 1.4: Targeted moderately degraded lands planted with a range of species to restore and enhance the landscape, improve livelihoods, and support biodiversity and protected forest ecosystems

In other degraded areas with some surviving plant species, often phyto-pastoral, age-ecology and agro-forestry techniques will be used to enrich productivity on household plots (i.e. up to 3 ha per household), improving the production of food crops and/or fodder products. The programme will use agroecology principles and techniques which is fundamentally based on the understanding that agriculture is a complex system involving biological, ecological processes, it helps solve local problems through context-specific solutions. Agroecological innovations are based on the principles of sustainability, resilience, and adaptation to climate change, and the empowerment of producers. By strengthening their autonomy and their capacity to adapt, agroecology empowers producers and populations in rural areas.

## Sub-Output Africa: Studies show that agroecology can contribute to food and nutrition security while restoring resources, ecosystem services, and biodiversity (Annex 2). These studies also show that agroecological practices are more resilient than conventional agricultural systems. SAGURAWIDA will consolidate the production base and economic stability of the agroecology sector by facilitating the interaction between community groups and private operators developing promising services such as farmer advice, mechanization, production and marketing of seeds and organic inputs, mechanization, etc.

Activity 1.4.1: Sub-national moderately degraded land restoration plans prepared and implemented

57. Since this activity concerns the restoration of moderately degraded land, soil preparation will rely mainly rely on animal traction and manual labour. Beneficiary households will be supported with key information to prepare costed plans for the restoration of their prioritized sites (sub-activity 1.4.1.1). Local communities will also receive technical guidance in preparing these plans.

Output 5.1: Rural community capacities strengthened for sustainable management and restoration, and verification of restoration results.

58. To ensure greater sustainability of programme actions and not depend on continuous programme support, the village regeneration committees will reinstate pre-existing village organisations already established through other project activities and/or will be supported in establishing new ones (sub-activity 1.4.1.2). Village youth-based village technicians will provide support and follow-up to the restoration and maintenance with programme technical support as well as financial resources. The village regeneration committees will also be provided with training and guidance for large scale restoration of highly degraded lands, as well as for soil and water conservation and revegetation techniques (sub-activity 1.4.1.3). Village technicians will be responsible for the monitoring and reporting on progress of the village pilots. The programme will encourage information/knowledge exchange among these technicians establishing best practices and lessons learned.

59. The village technicians will also monitor, ground truth and report on progress of the pilots, which will be fed into the programme’s reporting system linked to the national and regional GGW M&E system. Aerial and satellite information will be used in conjunction with field visits by the programme team to verify the reported results and provide further verification of results reported by communities. During the first two years, the programme will provide direct support to them in their monitoring activities (sub-activity 1.4.1.3). The village technicians will also be responsible for local coordination and acknowledgement by the GGW-Nile and other national institutions involved in restoration monitoring.

Activity 1.5.1: Village technicians trained and equipped in managing restoration areas and verifying restoration results

60. Village technicians will be nominated by the communities, and confirmed in collaboration with the programme’s technical assistants, ensuring a balanced representation of gender and youth. Village technicians will then be trained on data collection tools and technologies, as well as participatory approaches (sub-activity 1.5.1.1). Village technicians will also receive training in soil preparation techniques, including the use of animal traction for restoration activities and the verification of results (sub-activity 1.5.1.2). These village technicians will in turn be responsible for training communities on restoration and maintenance activities.

### Component 2: Development of climate-resilient, low emission non-timber forest product (NTFP) value chain benefits vulnerable communities’ livelihoods

#### Outcome 2: Enhanced resilience of livelihoods

1. This component will have an important impact on the livelihoods of the local agro-silvo-pastoral communities and smallholder NTFP collectors, processors and sellers in the Great Green Wall zones in the selected Sahel countries.
2. This component will address the main constraints faced by the actors along the NTFP and fodder value chains (VC). These constraints include: (i) lack of technical knowledge and capacity to add value; (ii) lack of information on the volume of production of the different resources; (iii) inadequate infrastructure for processing; (iv) lack of access to finance; (v) insufficient marketing and branding and promotion of the product attributes; (vi) weak linkages between actors in the VC; (vii) poor market access, especially for smallholders responsible for harvesting, informal production and local distribution of many of these products from the Sahelian tree crops; and (viii) lack of institutional efficiency within these value chains has the potential to ensure food security and enhance income for women.

This component is organized to deliver three main output: (i) Output 2.1: Technical and institutional capacity building in NTFP processing, marketing and market information for NTFP stakeholders; (ii) Output 2.2: Enhanced access of smallholders and SMEs to financial services. This regulation will be achieved through the following interventions: (a) Strengthening the capacities of actors as described above; (b) Improving linkages between actors in the value chain, including producers, processors, distributors and traders; (c) Enhancing market information for NTFP stakeholders; (d) Creating a conducive environment for smallholder access to financial services. The interventions will be assessed against the following output indicator: (i) Number of value chain organizations (including producer, processor, distributor organizations) established or operational; (ii) Increase in net gross sales generated by actors along the NTFP and fodder VC; (iii) Increase in the number of smallholders accessing financial services.

## Activity 2.1.2: Train and coach local P&Es and MSMEs in sustainable production and collection practices to enhance NTFP quality and availability

### Under Activity 2.1.2, trainings will be conducted for enhancing the capacity of local producers/collections (men, women, youth) on sustainable production and collection practices of non-timber forest products (NTFP). The main constraints highlighted by the collectors and processors during the field consultations were related to the collection methods, quality of the raw material, and post-harvest handling. In the case of ganxet, bananas, boddies and jujube fruits, etc., the objective of the training is to improve the method of harvesting, processing, packaging, storage, transport and storage of the NTFP. The capacity building of producers/collectors will combine theoretical and practical sessions on the collection methods, quality of the product and the efficiency of post-harvest operations.

### Considering the diversity of the socio-economic basis, the trainings would focus on the primary products per group and the specific technical aspects to improve their collection/processing practices. This is the case for, among others, fodder and honey, products that are very specific and will require tailored approaches and tools.

During the implementation phase, specific attention will be paid to support for youth, women and other marginalized groups.

### Activity 2.1.3: Train and coach P&Ms and MSMEs to improve the processing practices (including packaging and labelling) of the selected products

Under activity 2.1.3 technical training will be conducted to improve the processes and packaging and labelling of the selected products. These include palm kernel, coconut, cocoa beans, coffee, pepper, vanilla, cashew nuts, etc. This will help P&Ms and MSMEs to improve the efficiency of their operations as well as the quality of the final products, and hence their profits and competitiveness in the market.

The training will be conducted on topics such as good agricultural practices (GAP), food safety standards and regulations, packaging and storage, conservation, etc. The practical training will be delivered in collaboration with specialized research institutes or private actors that are equipped for this purpose.

### Activity 2.1.3: Train and coach P&Ms and MSMEs to improve the processing practices (including packaging and labelling) of the selected products

The implementation phase will also include the development of new technologies that result out of partnerships with institutions on emission-lowering technologies and equipment to enhance NTFP processing at scale (See Activity 2.2.3). The findings from market analysis investigations will be used to design and align the training modules to help P&Ms and MSMEs improve their competitiveness in the market.

### NTFP processing training modules and resources will be developed and updated, based on the needs of the VC actors (sub-activity 2.1.3.1). Using these modules and resources technical assistance (training and coaching) will be provided to P&Ms and MSMEs

#### Output 2.3: Increased access to markets for smallholder NTFP actors (including micro and small enterprises)

Products derived from GGW tree crops that deliver ecological and socioeconomic benefits show great potential to contribute to the achievement of the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs). These products have a high value in the NTFP market, and the incomes of the actors engaged in the sector, especially for smallholders-MSMEs including women, are very low. The GGW tree crops are also under threat from climate change, particularly in the Great Green Wall area, as well as the latter's ecological/environmental vulnerability to climate change. In this context, it is important that the actors involved in the sector have access to viable, socially and economically restorative restoration efforts constitute a major opportunity to deliver restoration services while generating additional livelihood opportunities to the communities. This output is composed of a series of interventions that aim at increasing market access for smallholder NTFP actors (including micro and small enterprises) on national, regional and international levels. The following are the main activities envisaged under this output.

#### Activity 2.1.2: Identify NTFPs with market and sustainable production potential that can be integrated in land restoration efforts funded under Component I

On one hand, the lack of knowledge about the value of the NTFP is related to lack of proper information of the potential of the various NTFPs (Balanites, Jujube, Babassu, Tamarindus, Shea, etc.). There is a deficiency of robust baseline data on the number of trees, production levels and export volumes of the NTFP in the GGW countries. This intervention will aim at addressing these gaps by conducting surveys to collect information on the potential resources for the NTFP and (ii) setting up a mechanism for the monitoring of the NTFP resources.

#### Activity 20.1: This activity will promote and support a participatory assessment and monitoring of the resources for selected NTFPs in GGW countries, with the aim to identify the most promising ones. The results from this participatory approach will strengthen the capacities of the national institutions for ownership and sustainability. This intervention will
