## Activity 2.24: Enhance access to national, regional and international markets for NTFP, including through the setting up of norms and standards to improve the marketing of the NTFP.

### Background
Under Activity 2.24.a, the programme will enhance access to national, regional and international markets for NTFPs. Lack of market information and poor quality of NTFPs in the GGOV countries makes it difficult to quantify accurately the market potential of the prioritized value chains. Except for shea and gum arabic, few market actors are aware of the demand for other NTFP products in both domestic and international markets, primarily in the food and cosmetics industries, are expanding their product and ingredient range to include Sahelian value chain products such as shea butter, honey, vanilla, and cocoa. The programme will carry out the following interventions: (i) Conduct market investigations and value chain assessments; (ii) Facilitate access to markets through the development of national standards and norms for NTFPs; (iii) Facilitate market linkages between producers and buyers through roundtables.

### The programme
The programme will facilitate discussions and negotiation between the producers/processors and the buyers (off-takers). This will be done in collaboration with the Ministry of Agriculture, Forestry and Livestock, and other institutional buyers such as WFP for their school feeding programmes[4]. This option and opportunity has been discussed with WFP during the consultations workshop in Naima – Niger. (information and/or training of the 2006 pilot project participants on market access and value chain development will be provided to the Ministry of Agriculture, Forestry and Livestock to rehape their plans and objectives in terms of targeted markets). These interventions will help in overcoming market barriers and improving the marketing of NTFPs. The programme will also support the private sector’s active engagement of NTFP actors in the private sector in commercially viable, socially and ecologically responsible ways.

[4] The following elements should be considered in terms of criteria in the process of selection of the business coaches: Experience, attitude, willingness to share experience in the field/locations. Accessibility, connectivity and networks, expectations, a line of trust; old you know well, etc.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V.3110 | PAGE 38

B

## Objective of this intervention is to assist the beneficiary countries through their specialized institutions in the elaboration, validation and dissemination of National Technology Platforms (NTP) for food processing and the production and commercialization of the concerned products as well as supporting the consumer.

The NFTP sector is characterized by a lack of clear and consistent regulation in terms of norms and standards governing the production and commercialization of the concerned products. This results in an uneven quality of these products, with some GQW countries more advanced in terms of setting up norms and standards for the products and commercializing them on their domestic markets than others.

In many cases, the NFTP sector is not covered by national legislation and regulations, which are based on norms and standards for more than 90% of its NTFP. In some countries, the norms and standards exist, but the national legislation does not provide for their implementation or enforcement (sub-activity 2.2.1). The programme will also intervene in the information/communication of the norms as well as for the training of the actors on these norms.

The programme will also collaborate with research institutions in order to identify properties of the various NTPR (anti-inflammatory, diuretic and digestive products), fight against diabetes and obesity, prebiotic properties, gluten content, etc. The programme will also support the elaboration of a national strategy for the promotion and implementation: (i) Support to the elaboration/update of norms and standards for the NFTP. For this sub-activity, the programme will provide technical assistance to the specialized institutions in the elaboration of the norms and standards.

(ii) Support to the updating of norms and standards (sub-activity 2.2.1). (i) Information, sensitization and/or training of the NTPF value chain actors on the norms and standards (sub-activity 2.2.2). Considering that some countries are much more advanced in terms of setting up norms and standards for their NTFP than others, the programme will also support the elaboration of a regional strategy for the promotion and implementation of these norms and standards among the beneficiary countries through regional workshops (sub-activity 2.2.4). Significant contributions from the regional entities (ECOWAS, EAC, SADC) are expected to be mobilized in order to ensure that the benefits of this intervention will be capitalized.

The implementation of this intervention will also cover the areas that will be restored under component I for the period 2015-2016.

The programme will facilitate market linkages between NTP producers and buyers through information sessions and B2B roundtables for business facilitation (sub-activity 2.2.4.5), addressing another key constraint to increased production and commercialization of NTFPs, which is the lack of access to markets. The programme will also address requirements in international trade, targeted to SMEs with export potential (sub-activity 2.2.4.6). Finally, a regional workshop will be organized on the elaboration of a regional strategy for the promotion and implementation of the SURAQOWA multi-country programme (sub-activity 2.2.4.7). This will be a periodic and rotated (among the beneficiary countries) activity that will gather the main stakeholders in the implementation of the programme, including representatives from specialized institutions, private sector actors, regional institutions, research institutions, civil society organizations, non-governmental organizations, other programme implementation partners, private sector actors, regional institutions, civil society organizations, non-governmental organizations and other relevant stakeholders.

Activity 2.2.5: Identify, train and equip PQA and MSMEs with emission-lowering technologies and equipment to enhance NTFP processing

Under Activity 2.2.5, partnerships will be established with institutions working on emission-lowering technologies and equipment for NTFP processing. For most of the NFTP, technology advanced manufacturing processes and significant R&D investments are required for enhancing the efficiency of the process and enhancing the quality of the products. The programme will provide technical assistance to specialized institutions that are specialized in the research and manufacturing of food processing equipment and more specifically in emission-lowering technologies and equipment for NTFPs (sub-activity 2.2.5). ### Output 2.3 Suitable credit and insurance products designed for smallholder NTFP value chain actors (PO, cooperatives, etc.) and increased stakeholder capacity for using these financial products in their NTFP value chains

The output aims to ease the specific barriers around access to finance for smallholder farmers, producer organizations, and other actors involved in the production of NTFPs. This will be achieved by:

- Reducing transaction costs
- Scaling up income generating activities from agricultural value chains, particularly of Non-Timber Forest Products (NTFPs) such as honey, beeswax, nuts, fruits, etc.
- Improving access to credit and insurance products in rural areas, low literacy, lack of collateral, limited products and services tailored to NTFPs and poor financial literacy
- Reducing the barriers to accessing finance for smallholder farmers and other actors involved in the production of NTFPs (at national as well as at country level). The output will harness synergies with other access to finance interventions financed by the Green Climate Fund (GCF) such as the GCF’s support to the development of a financial inclusion strategy, loans or technical assistance for local financial institutions in the Great Green Wall through its Green Finance Facility (GFF), and the GCF’s support to the development of a financial inclusion strategy, loans or technical assistance for local financial institutions in the Great Green Wall through its Green Finance Facility (GFF).

The output will be implemented by:

- The GCF
- The GCF’s partner organizations such as the SGRUGAWA fund and/or other partners

The output will be financed by:

- Country budget
- Donor contributions
- Loans or technical assistance from local financial institutions in the Great Green Wall through its Green Finance Facility (GFF)
- Other sources of financing, by country and time period.

GCF aims to build and scale the resilience and adaptive capacity of producers and other actors involved in NTFPs value chains. This will be achieved by:

- Reducing transaction costs
- Scaling up income generating activities from agricultural value chains, particularly of Non-Timber Forest Products (NTFPs) such as honey, beeswax, nuts, fruits, etc.
- Improving access to credit and insurance products in rural areas, low literacy, lack of collateral, limited products and services tailored to NTFPs and poor financial literacy
- Reducing the barriers to accessing finance for smallholder farmers and other actors involved in the production of NTFPs (at national as well as at country level). The output will harness synergies with other access to finance interventions financed by the Green Climate Fund (GCF) such as the GCF’s support to the development of a financial inclusion strategy, loans or technical assistance for local financial institutions in the Great Green Wall through its Green Finance Facility (GFF), and the GCF’s support to the development of a financial inclusion strategy, loans or technical assistance for local financial institutions in the Great Green Wall through its Green Finance Facility (GFF).

The output will be implemented by:

- The GCF
- The GCF’s partner organizations such as the SGRUGAWA fund and/or other partners

The output will be financed by:

- Country budget
- Donor contributions
- Loans or technical assistance from local financial institutions in the Great Green Wall through its Green Finance Facility (GFF)
- Other sources of financing, by country and time period.

### Activity 2.3.1 Train and sensitize value chain actors on financial products offerings, enhance financial literacy and record keeping

Activities under Output 2.3.1 tackle access to finance constraints through demand and supply side interventions that will be implemented in a holistic manner. From the demand side: The programme will map the existing relevant financial products and services available in the GGW regions, by country for the agriculture sector (e.g., smallholder farmers, producer organizations, etc.) and other actors involved in NTFPs value chains, including their capacity to use these products and services. This will be done through stakeholder engagement with key stakeholders such as local financial institutions, MSMEs, and other familiarize value chain actors (e.g., smallholders, farmers organizations, MSMEs) with the suite of financial services

### Activity 2.3.2 Technical assistance provided to develop five new financial products (of which at least one must be a microfinance product) and associated value chains for financial intermediation, including those collaborating with GRIEFMEN.

98. This activity aims to support the enabling environment for access to financial products and services. Through the design of innovative financial products, the programme will aim to increase the availability of credit for smallholder farmers through short- and long-term finance to support value chain activities. These activities will be undertaken collaboratively with GRIEFMEN (sub-activity 2.3.2.1). The programme will also provide technical assistance to financial institutions in order to enhance their capacity to offer these new products, including the design of credit risk management systems and other tools that will enable them only partly to enhance the reach of financial services in remote areas with low bank branch penetration.

99. Before designing any new financial products, the programme will assess the existing financial products related to NTFPs (sub-activity 2.3.2.1). The assessment will be based on a review of existing products and their performance (sub-activity 2.3.2.1). On the basis of this assessment, it will design two new credit products, one aimed at working capital and the other for long-term financing. These products will be designed in collaboration with financial institutions (sub-activity 2.3.2.2). Subsequently, the programme will test and disseminate these two new credit products to a wider range of financial institutions (sub-activity 2.3.2.3).

100. The programme will design one agricultural insurance product (indeed) for the drylands context, with GRIEFMEN (sub-activity 2.3.2.4). This product will be designed in collaboration with financial institutions and financial institutions (sub-activity 2.3.2.5). Finally, the programme will develop two new savings products, one digital and one cash-based, for smallholder farmers and share this with national and regional financial institutions (sub-activity 2.3.2.5).

### Activity 2.3.3 Train loan staff (loan officers and others) from financial institutions, including those collaborating with GRIEFMEN, on the new financial products and services to be offered in their respective regions (national or regional level)

101. To ensure supply side first, considering the limited lending to NTFP value chains, the programme will train and sensitize loan officers in financial institutions (national agricultural banks, commercial banks, microfinance institutions) on the profitability of NTFP value chains, showcasing successful experiences and prospects, as well as an agricultural financial service provider (sub-activity 2.3.3.1). Second, it will train loan officers and other personnel who disclose credit in understanding, assessing and managing agricultural loans, including those related to NTFPs (sub-activity 2.3.3.2). Third, the programme will also provide training for loan officers on how to assess and manage microfinance products (sub-activity 2.3.3.3). Fourth, loan officers will be organized in virtual mode (sub-activity 2.3.3.2). Many of these trainings will also be organized as a regional level activity.

### Activity 2.3.4 Facilitate linkages between last-mile providers of finance (producer organizations, village savings groups, microfinance networks) and financial institutions, including those participating in GRIEFMEN.

102. This activity aims to facilitate linkages between financial institutions at all levels, including the provision of credits at the last mile through producer organizations, village savings groups, microfinance networks and other relevant actors (sub-activity 2.3.4.1). The programme will also provide technical assistance for the development of credit products by last-mile providers of finance such as village savings groups, microfinance networks and producer organizations with national and regional financial institutions (sub-activity 2.3.4.2) and with other local and regional development banks. The country-level training activities under sub-activity 2.3.3 serve as the forum for these linkages.

103. The programme will provide last of mile rural finance in GNF GWG regions, by country (sub-activity 2.3.4.1). This will be done through the provision of credit to producer organizations and financial institutions, including those participating in GRIEFMEN (sub-activity 2.3.4.2).

*Currently, the availability of funds for local financial institutions also remains a hurdle. However, through its Green Financial Facility (GFF), the Government of Rwanda has demonstrated its commitment to lending and thereby ability to dedicate credit to the end users and thus the present programme does not aim to duplicate this intervention.*

## Activity 2.3.1: Boost access to credit through digital financial solutions in at least one country and facilitate scale up, if successful

### Objective:
This activity aims to increase access to finance, particularly in rural and remote areas where long distances to physical branches make it difficult for farmers to obtain credit.

### Description of the Activity:
The programme will collaborate with UNCDF to rid a digital credit solution in one country, accompanied by a advisory services that manage the risk of default. This activity will be co-financed by UNCDF on a national agricultural development bank. FAD will provide technical assistance and capacity building for financial institutions and farmers.

### Expected Outcomes:
The programme will work with financial institutions to roll out digital credit solutions in one country (sub-activity 2.3.1). It will then roll out this digital credit solution accompanied by an e-adviser weather information service, a mobile app, and other advisory services as organized to discuss performance of the product and explore potential for scaling up (sub-activity 2.3.5).

## Activity 2.3.6: Improve knowledge management and exchanges to increase adoption of best practices across both components

### Objective:
Finally, the programme will also facilitate knowledge and peer-to-peer exchanges between financial institutions at national level in each country participating in the programme.

### Description of the Activity:
The programme will build on existing regional networks such as the existing pan-African network of the African Rural and Agricultural Credit Association (AFRACA). Their member organizations are from 24 countries, including all SURA/GWIA countries. The programme will also work with other regional organizations. They are present in five of the eight SURA/GWIA countries. The programme will organize regional seminars for financial institutions to share experiences with NTFP value chains in the GWG region (sub-activity 2.4.6.1).

## Component 3: Strengthening the Great Green Wall institutions at country and regional level

### Outcome 3: Enhanced institutional capacities of National GGW Agencies and of PA-GGW for monitoring, coordination, and resource mobilization

#### Building capable and efficient institutions is pivotal to achieving the very ambitious goals assigned to the GGW initiative. This will also enhance the sustainability of the programme’s results, and to enable the GGW Initiative to effectively address the challenge of scaling up.

The main target and beneficiaries of the capacity development action are the National GGW Agencies (NGGAs) and the PA-GGW at national level. The GGW initiative at the national and regional levels, namely: (i) the national GGW agencies in the 8 participating countries (sub-1.1) and (ii) the PA-GGW at country and regional level (sub-3.2).

#### While some gains have already been made in building these institutions through the efforts of the member states and the impact of the African Union, capacity remains insufficient as pointed out in several recent analyses.

The GGW initiative will continue to build on existing institutional capacities by focusing on the needs and interests of beneficiary institutions, and by complementing the efforts deployed by other partners, and in particular the effort to build a GGGF specific institution. The GGW initiative will also focus on building the capacity of the national strategic agencies that are fundamental to the successful handling of the country- (and regional-) types of GGW investment. This includes: (i) monitoring and reporting capacities, which will be the major focus of SURAGGW’s establishment of a GGW Investment Monitoring and Reporting System (sub-3.2.1); (ii) resource mobilization capacities, with particular emphasis on carbon finance (Output 3.2.4); and (iii) institutional capacity building.

#### The intervention strategy of the component is strongly based on three principles: first, working jointly at the regional and national levels on a common set of capacity development areas of critical importance for all the Sahel GGW countries; second, focusing on the most important gaps in terms of knowledge, skills, and interpretable tools and practices between countries and at the regional level as far as for example land restoration ecosystems are concerned (Output 3.2.1); third, ensuring that the capacity development efforts are tailored to specific contexts which mean that the paths taken by each country to build certain capacities or to develop their GGW institutions mandates may be different. The SURAGGW programme will adapt to these realities and propose a

**113.** Finally, the component provides capacity building elements that are innovative on at least two levels. Firstly, the capacity building activities targeted at improving and mainstreaming the use of monitoring tools (while taking care to build a system that is interoperable and therefore sustainably embeddable). Secondly, the actions in favour of action on carbon finance are also innovatively intensive, particularly with regard to the capacity to implement the Green Wall Initiative in the region of Africa, which is at an extremely important stage in the sub-region despite its importance and potential.

**Outcome 2.1:** GWG Land restoration interventions systems and regional level operational and functional

**114.** In some countries as well as regions are a weak emphasis on these issues, even though none of them have made progress in this regard. These current tools being used are not very efficient and provide little information for decision making. The monitoring arrangements are also partial and do not always adequately cover the entire area of land restoration interventions. Furthermore, there is no systematic use information for decision makers and sectoral planners in GWG countries. The activities and sub-activities under the component will help expertise and capacity to use innovative and cost-effective land use assessment tools (e.g., FADs) and other monitoring tools. This will also contribute to improving the overall efficiency and efficacy with other monitoring and reporting efforts on biophysical and socio-economic outcomes from GWG interventions. Reporting of these indicators will be done in a timely manner, as well as through an innovative approach by reporting to high-level platforms including the UN General Assembly who will raise the profile of the Great Green Wall.

The approach taken by the multi-country programme will be to build on the achievements of the AAD project, which has developed technology-based approaches that are cutting-edge and efficient, and at the same time are locally appropriate. The monitoring arrangements under this component will be innovative in terms of their design and implementation. The monitoring capacity will be built in a partnership manner, building on existing institutions and capacities in each country and region. This will be done through the establishment of National Green Wall Agencies (NGWAs) within the Great Green Wall National Agency (GGWNA) with expertise in monitoring technologies will be identified for partnerships. These NGWAs will also be responsible for the development of a national monitoring framework, which will be aligned with the national and regional monitoring partner organizations. The GWG NAs and partner organizations will be trained to use these tools and technologies and will have the capacity to train others as well. This will enable these institutions to play a leading role in supporting the adoption of the systems and basic elements by as many GWG land restoration interventions and projects as possible.

**115.** Building on previous work under AAD, and in close collaboration with PAIGC, SURAGANA has also contributed to the identification of a set of common GWG initiative indicators initiated within the framework of the Accelerator with the support of GGGI. The development of these indicators is currently being finalized as part of the GGGI-GWG partnership framework which is subject of a multi-country consultation, so that it will facilitate the work of harmonization of indicators and data collection across countries and regions. These indicators are also aligned to the Global Framework for Ecosystem Restoration Monitoring (EFMR) and therefore showcase the contributions of GWG to the UN Decade on Ecosystem Restoration.

**116.** FAO has already selected and established biophysical baselines at the country level and for GWG restorable lands (see Earthdata.org) and trained county experts in the use of these tools. The information will continue to be used by the component to support the development of a regional monitoring framework that will help coordinate sustainable development and climate action efforts in the region using a climate-based GIS. Baselines will be updated and enhanced through the different FAO Operatools, in particular through the development of user-friendly software tools for data collection and analysis, including remote sensing, geospatial information, and machine learning, integrating socio-economic and biophysical information. The System for Earth Observation, data acquisition and processing, and the use of satellite imagery will be used to support the development of a wall-to-wall information from collected sample data using Geel Earth and for monitoring specific indicators and remote sensing tools will be used to collect data on land cover change and other relevant indicators using the GGGI GHG software. The resulting wall-to-wall information will be completed with socio-economic and biophysical field data collection in 2024 and 2027. It will entail the use of a data collection software package (PodeToolBox) that

### Combined use of reference-based monitoring of restoration sites as linked to Component 1 activities and (B) household and market level monitoring of critical socio-economic indicators is linked to both Component 1 and Component 2 activities which will also be site-specific.

#### Under this component, a regional orientation will also be provided for tools for estimating GHG impacts per unit of land, expressed in GCO2-e per ha and per year*, including the Africa Open Data methodology and the EX- ACT tool (Ex-Ante Carbon Balance Tool) - with the latter having been used to quantify the carbon impact of SUGARWIGA. The training will be provided by the CGIAR Research Program on Climate Change, Agriculture and Food Security for the analysis of soil carbon, which is an essential component of the CO2 results of SUGARWIGA and other activities under this component. Training in the use of the EX-ACT tool will also be provided to support climate change adaptation. SUGARWIGA will strengthen the capacity of CGIAR actors to use the Fertilizer Tool for Agroecology Performance (FTAP) and the Soil Carbon Estimation Tool (SCET) as well as the Tapestry Approach (TAPE). This tool shows how agroecology can contribute to more sustainable food and agricultural systems. TAPE will be used in collaboration with CGIAR actors, including SUGARWIGA, to support climate change adaptation strategies that also contribute to the empowerment of producers through self-diagnosis and assessment of their system's transition level and performance. Assistance on this tool will be on a tailored basis, depending on country interest.

#### Activity 3.1.1: Development, testing and deployment of National GGW land restoration monitoring tools and systems

- The programme will analyze existing monitoring mechanisms and tools used throughout the PAGWIG and each of its sub-components (sub-activity 3.1.1.1). This analysis will be complemented by a review of existing needs and identify needs at national and regional level (sub-activity 3.1.1.1). Subsequently, GGW monitoring modules and systems will be developed in collaboration with CGIAR actors, including SUGARWIGA, to support the development of NAGSIN for national GGW monitoring. Equipment will include computers, laptops, printers, tablets and smartphones (sub-activity 3.1.1.2).

#### Activity 3.1.2: Transfer knowledge and skills for the use of the GGG Land restoration monitoring system by national actors

- The programme, working closely together with national and regional CGIAR institutions, will: i) compile manuals, guidelines and other relevant documentation on the use of the GGG land restoration monitoring system; ii) provide training to national actors in the use of the GGG land restoration monitoring system. Subsequently, monitoring tools will be deployed and documentation produced under activity 3.1.1 will be shared (sub-activity 3.1.2.1). The programme will provide hands-on training and piloting of GGG land restoration monitoring tools (sub-activity 3.1.2.3).

#### Activity 3.1.3: Development and deployment of Regional multi-stakeholder Monitoring platform

- The next step in enhancing and "de-projecting" restoration monitoring systems in the GGG zone is to develop and deploy a regional multi-stakeholder monitoring platform (sub-activity 3.1.3.1). This will be done by integrating restoration results and lessons learned from different approaches applied in different biophysical and socioeconomic contexts across national and regional stakeholders.

#### Sub-activities:

- 3.1.3.4: The programme will also develop a prototype of the platform, design, test, and deploy the GGG platform and all its modules, including not just remote sensing applications but also a soil carbon analysis tool*, ensuring inter-operability between Regional platform and monitoring sub-activity 3.1.3.1). Subsequently, the programme will set up a regional multi-stakeholder monitoring platform (sub-activity 3.1.3.2) that will be piloted in at least three countries and populated with regional layers from various sources (sub-activity 3.1.3.2). This product will be translated in at least two languages, including English and French. The programme will also develop a prototype of the platform, design, test and deploy the GGG platform and all its modules, including not just remote sensing applications but also a soil carbon analysis tool*, ensuring inter-operability between Regional platform and monitoring sub-activity 3.1.3.1). Subsequently, the programme will set up a regional multi-stakeholder monitoring platform (sub-activity 3.1.3.2) that will be piloted in at least three countries and populated with regional layers from various sources (sub-activity 3.1.3.2).

### Activity 3.1.5 Prepare regulatory national frameworks for the surveillance of all GGW labelled interventions in all countries

#### 120. With restoration monitoring systems and restoration projects/programmes databases operational, it will be important to agree on national regulatory frameworks for the surveillance of GGW labelled interventions in GGW countries. The framework should include a definition of what constitutes an intervention that is GGW labelled under this framework, and the division of labour for applying it may vary somewhat from one country to another, so it will be important to develop a common understanding among a multi-stakeholder process at a regional level and within respective frameworks in each of the 8 countries.

#### 126. The programme will support the drafting and multi-stakeholder validation of a regulatory framework document for restoration monitoring, as well as the publication of this document, in each of the 8 countries (sub-activity 3.1.5.1).

### Activity 3.1.6 Develop operational partnerships between the NAGGWs and PAGGW and scientific and technical institutions in the region (such as universities, research institutes, CGIAR, Aghylef, ACMDA) on issues of ecological restoration and land use.

#### 127. The programme will train selected staff from scientific and technical institutions in GGW land tenure monitoring methodologies and tools, including the development of Memorandums of Understanding (MoU) and agreement on joint working modalities between NAGGW/PAGGW and technical institutions identified and trained under the programme (sub-activity 3.1.6.2).

### Activity 3.1.7 Strengthen the capacities of national stakeholders in the use of the relevant tools developed by FAF, including the GGW Land Tenure Monitoring System (LTM), the Electronic Surveying and Mapping Centre (ESMC), the Sahra and Sahel Observatory (OS) and GIS.

#### 128. The programme will also support capacity building for national stakeholders in the use of these tools as research institutions – has developed many restoration monitoring tools that are highly adapted to Africa’s difficult IT context, with many data layers and complex algorithms that require a lot of computing power than is available on most computers within 30 days. Training relevant national and regional actors in the use of these tools will improve the performance and reduce the cost and the effort required for restoration monitoring.

#### 129. The programme will also develop and deploy tools and training modules, and provide hands-on training courses to key institutions and stakeholders (sub-activity 3.1.7.1). The programme will also provide training over a year for 15 land restoration monitoring specialists from NAGGW and partner institutions (sub-activity 3.1.7.2).

### Conclusion

#### 130. In conclusion, it is important to note that within the framework of output 3.1, the said “regional” activities are also being implemented in a manner that ensures that they are aligned with the GGW regional strategy and interoperable mechanisms and tools whose future management and development are under the responsibility of the PA-GGW (which will itself receive support from SURAGGWA to ensure this role). # Output 3.2: National and Regional GGW Institutions planning and coordination capacities strengthened

## Activity 3.2.1: Establish National GGW Coalitions to promote coherent coordination and planning at country level

### 131.
Through this output, the programme will strengthen the capacity of GGW institutions at the national and regional levels to address the challenge of planning and coordinating ongoing land and landscape restoration initiatives that are recognized as an integral part of the GGGI dynamic. The GGW has fostered a great deal of interest in all the countries involved in the GGW, which have been able to identify potential partners for their own programmes and/or joint partners, government agencies and their programmes. This has resulted in a multiplication of land restoration interventions that are to be aligned with GGGI through the information varies from one country to another; the difficulties of coordination are strongly felt by all.

### 132.
While this problem is linked to the broader issue of aid coordination, SUBREGAWA can provide systemic support to NAGGWs to improve their planning and coordination performance. The output will contribute to the objective of GGW institutions at national level to be able to plan and coordinate land restoration interventions in a more coherent way by providing them with tools for better information management and sharing, as well as better coordination and planning. This will result in well-defined benefits in the form of well-functioning and inclusive multi-stakeholder platforms that are able to provide better information on the initiatives taken and promote the coordination of interventions. These regular exchanges will progressively lead to a more precise definition of the principles of intervention to which they commit themselves by being part or joining the initiative.

### 133.
The planning exercises initiated by the GGW NAs are often undertaken in terms of forecasts and poorly followed up by the entities implementing land restoration projects. The activities under this output will contribute to the objective of GGW institutions at national level to be able to plan and coordinate land restoration interventions in a more coherent way by providing them with tools for better information management and sharing, as well as better coordination and planning processes frameworks, which will improve the flow of information on the initiatives taken (a better basis for planning and coordination).

### 134.
In order to generate a larger impact, SUBREGAWA will align itself with the recent initiative of setting-up national coalitions in each of the GGWI countries. These coalitions will serve as a foundation upon which planning processes frameworks can be built. The GGW NAs will also be supported by SUBREGAWA in the conduct technical exchanges aimed at coordinating the coverage and geographic targeting strategy of GGWI interventions, with a view to progressively defining the basic principles of intervention to which they commit themselves by being part or joining the initiative.

### 135.
The programme will establish a baseline for this result by undertaking a critical analysis of the existing coordination mechanisms in place within each country. The output will also contribute to the objective of GGW institutions at national level to be able to plan and coordinate land restoration interventions in a more coherent way by providing them with tools for better information management and sharing, as well as better coordination and planning processes frameworks.

### Activity 3.2.2: Prepare issue planning and regulatory frameworks for the coordination of all GGW aligned interventions (projects, programmes, activities, etc.)

#### Output 3: National and regional climate change institutional capacities/frameworks strengthened to integrate land restoration investments in climate change adaptation and mitigation programmes

This output aims at building the capacities of the institutional actors of the Great Green Wall (GGW) to mobilize resources for land restoration, including through the implementation of complementary activities included in other SURAGAWA components to strengthen GGW resource mobilization capacities. Under component 1, the GGW will be supported by a multi-country programme that will focus on building national and sub-national enabling actors to demonstrate climate change mitigation and adaptation impact and efficiency – thus remedying the lack of institutional capacity for land restoration in many countries. The GGW will also support the use of smaller value chain support to engage local communities to commit land and labour to restoration efforts, it is not only a key element of the GGW multi-country programme as a whole, but also a key element of the theory of change of the SURAGAWA multi-country programme as a whole.

#### Output 3.1: National and regional climate change institutional capacities/frameworks strengthened to integrate land restoration investments in climate change adaptation and mitigation programmes

A range of resource mobilization strategies have already been prepared in the past at the continental and country level, but these are often fragmented and not well coordinated. The GGW is a more complex initiative that generates harder to generate as traditional donors have also reduced their development budgets given the pressures they face from other priorities. In addition, the GGW will be implemented through a multi-country programme with development efforts to be undertaken by the SURAGAWA multi-country programme, will be focusing on national agencies and institutions in each country, which may not have the capacity or resources to implement this type of content. However, for efficiency purposes the effort will be coordinated at the regional level (through the SURAGAWA multi-country programme) with a focus on building enabling actors at the national level through a coordinated manner through orientation sessions organized by groups of countries. Support will be tailored to help countries in their efforts to mobilize resources for land restoration, including through the implementation of complementary activities included in other SURAGAWA components to strengthen GGW resource mobilization capacities.

#### Output 3.1.1: National and regional climate change institutional capacities/frameworks strengthened to integrate land restoration investments in climate change adaptation and mitigation (i) support for the acceleration efforts of national GGW agencies to climate fund development

In relation to Carbon Finance, the output aims at harness opportunities and strengthen capacities in GGW countries to access carbon finance. The GGW is a large-scale initiative that could generate significant revenues which incentivizes scaling up. The Africa Carbon Markets Initiative identifies "forestry and land use" as one of the types of projects that can be eligible for funding under the CDM. This type of project would allow for the kind of sequestration that could become viable to attract international financing requiring large tracts of land and a large amount of investment in order to achieve significant carbon sequestration. The GGW is also expected to generate transactions under article 6 of the Paris Agreement, also linked to compliance markets in buyer countries. In addition, opportunities exist for the GGW to engage with national governments and/or policy committees to create domestic climate change carbon markets or carbon taxes with potential offset demand.

* Roadmap Report, p.17


---
**Page 54**
---

142. The barriers to access international carbon markets include scarce experience in carbon finance in agriculture and forestry, fragmented carbon market activities in the region with a limited number of projects, and lack of capacity for project development and implementation as well as of tailored methodologies appropriate for the drylands context. At policy and regulatory level, insecure tenure, unclear land use rights, and weak enforcement mechanisms are also barriers to access international carbon finance opportunities, the barriers to incentive restoration relate to the lack of existing models in the CGIAR region and capacity gaps in integrating agriculture and forestry into domestic carbon market or tax and offset schemes.

143. The programme will work in coordination and partnership with national and regional initiatives that strengthen carbon markets for landscape restoration in Africa (Nigerian Carbon Market Initiative) and the Green Climate Fund (GCF) across sectors. It will collaborate with these initiatives to harness specific opportunities and strengthen capacities of countries in the CGIAR region to access international carbon finance, including through capacity building and technical assistance. The programme will also seek support under the programme aims to unlock financing for additional sequestration beyond those financed directly through the GCF or other existing mechanisms. It will do this by developing a comprehensive approach that addresses relevant constraints within the sectoral and geographical scope of the programme, through five categories of activities: (i) strengthening public and private sector understanding of and capacity to engage with carbon markets for climate change adaptation and mitigation; (ii) strengthening access to international carbon finance; (iii) strengthening national carbon market systems in Nigeria; (iv) piloting the establishment of a domestic carbon accounting framework that integrates agriculture and forestry in Nigeria, and identify additional countries for potential replication; and (v) supporting the creation of carbon finance opportunities for restoration as part of developing national carbon markets.

Activity 3.3.1: Strengthen public and private sector understanding of and capacity to engage with carbon markets for climate change adaptation and mitigation through land restoration

144. Under the set of activities related to Activity 3.3.1, the programme will facilitate access to international carbon finance for landscape restoration in the GOW through training regional technical staff on how to identify and enhance carbon finance options, relevant to the specific regional (Sahel) and landscape (dryland) context. Specifically, it will assess the carbon market opportunities at national level and within the CGIAR region, including by identifying suitable locations and approaches to pursue carbon finance opportunities. It will also take stock of the regions on-going experience in accessing international carbon finance for landscape restoration and identify lessons learned. It will develop and assess scenarios and implications for choice of options. SURAGOWA will assess relevant carbon finance methodologies currently under development or envisaged by other initiatives, including those that are not yet fully developed (for example, landuse/region/capital, system/technology specific). Currently, the SURAGOWA countries do not have a comprehensive approach to access international carbon finance for landscape restoration. The information is patchy and dispersed and outdated. This sub-activity will fill this gap and ensure new developments in this dynamic field are captured and disseminated within the CGIAR region. It will also develop a database of relevant methodologies and update this overview and database at regular intervals beyond its implementation period, complementing existing databases such as those developed by GCF.

145. Under sub-activity 3.3.1.c, the programme will strengthen country understanding of and capacity to pursue carbon finance for landscape restoration in the GOW through regional training, knowledge sharing and peer-to-peer learning on carbon markets and the African Carbon Market Initiative. It will provide training to national and subnational policymakers on carbon market opportunities and methodologies. It will also facilitate exchange visits between countries and existing and potential methodologies in the Voluntary Carbon Market and Article 6.1. It will also facilitate learning exchanges between countries in the region, linked to existing initiatives and organizations (see Annex 2).

Activity 3.3.2: Pilot the establishment of domestic carbon accounting framework that integrates agriculture and forestry in Nigeria, and identify additional countries for potential replication


---
**Page 55**
---

### GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL_V3.11.0_PAGE 49

#### Annex 2 discusses the carbon finance landscape in Nigeria and highlights its feasibility as an entry point for the programme, given the country's strong political engagement, institutional frameworks and potential to serve as a model for other Sahelian countries in developing domestic carbon pricing and compliance markets. The mandated Nigerian Carbon Accounting Framework (NCAF) is based on the voluntary carbon accounting framework for Nigeria’s carbon market or tax. Carbon credits from land use can potentially be marketed domestically and internationally through existing platforms such as the Voluntary Carbon Standard.

#### Annex 2 also discusses the integration of the agriculture and forestry sectors in Nigeria's carbon market policies and regulatory frameworks which would help create the incentives for private investments to generate carbon credits for restoration to date, and provide a framework for the inclusion of agriculture and forestry under the Nigerian Nationally Determined Contribution (NDC) on Climate Change on the inclusion of agriculture and forestry in their planned domestic carbon tax or emissions trading scheme. It will also help engage different stakeholders from the public, private and civil society sectors to feed into land use planning and restoration activities.

#### Annex 2 further discusses the importance of developing a national carbon accounting framework (NCAF) that is validating and/or developing methodologies and mechanisms to enable carbon finance opportunities for GGO restoration in line with international voluntary carbon market standards.

#### Understanding and meeting the requirements for concrete international voluntary carbon market opportunities or evolving Article 6 financing opportunities linked to their specific technical potentials and policy contexts will be essential. The NCAF will also provide a framework for the inclusion of agriculture and forestry under the Nigerian Nationally Determined Contribution (NDC) on Climate Change, and enhance countries' capacity to engage with carbon finance opportunities for GGO restoration.

#### Under sub-activity 3.3.2.1., the programme will assist Nigeria to integrate agriculture and forestry sectors in the planned national carbon accounting system as well as domestic carbon markets or carbon tax regimes (carbon offsets, credits, etc.). The NCAF will be developed through a participatory process involving relevant stakeholders, building on a general assessment of the potential benefits for land restoration from the programme in the Great Green Wall region. This will include developing methodologies and mechanisms to enable carbon finance opportunities under international voluntary carbon market standards.

#### Under sub-activity 3.3.2.2., Technical Assistance will be provided to Nigeria’s National Council on Climate Change (NCCC), and other relevant bodies, on addressing specific issues in national carbon accounting systems and domestic carbon markets, e.g., carbon tax and offsets or other options.

#### Under sub-activity 3.3.2.3., the programme will use the lessons learned from the process, approach and lessons from the pilot phase to support Nigeria’s NCCC and other relevant bodies in developing methodologies and mechanisms for integrating agriculture and forestry sectors into their planned national carbon accounting systems as well as domestic carbon markets (e.g. carbon offsets) by companies in sectors that have a compliance target within the domestic carbon market system, e.g., Colombia.

The proposal aims to provide information and capacity development support to policymakers and local developers at the country level, in partnership with the West Africa Alliance on Carbon Markets, which has confirmed that it views land carbon as a key element of its strategy for engaging with the sector, expressed its interest in working alongside the SUGAWIIGA programme. This is very much aligned with the vision of the Economic Community of West African States (ECOWAS) Regional Climate Strategy and Action Plan 2020-2030.

### Output 3.4 GGW knowledge management and communication capacities strengthened for mobilizing investment in land restoration

153. Knowledge management is an essential function on which the GGWP has not performed particularly well to date. The need is clearly expressed and recognized by many actors for improvement of this function, in particular in a context where the GGWP is expected to play a key role in mobilizing investments in land restoration. This point was very clearly underlined by the Green Wall Implementation Status and Way Ahead 2030 report and its recommendations. The GGWP has already made some progress on this front with the development of the IGGF, which is expected that IGF (with GCF co-financing) will take the lead through the IGDEFINN project, by strengthening the capacity of the GGWP to manage knowledge and information. In addition, the technical expertise developed in SUGAWIIGA will be shared through the regional KM support platform. This output will capitalize on the substantial technical expertise of FAO in analyzing and presenting technical papers and policy briefs.

154. Although highly visible within the international arena, the GGWP initiative suffers from several communication deficiencies. The first is related to the lack of a clear communication strategy for the GGWP initiatives organized by the practitioners working on land restoration issues in drylands. The second communication gap concerns the production of technical papers and policy briefs that are not always up-to-date or relevant. This is partly due to the success of the GGWP initiative relies to a large extent on the adherence of the communities to the approach, as most of these communities are not literate and therefore do not have access to information. It is therefore crucial that the GGWP NAs have effective and efficient communication mechanisms that have wide outreach capacity in order to ensure that all stakeholders involved in land restoration initiatives, such as women and youth, as they play key roles and are the main beneficiaries of the services and products of technologies developed under the GGWP initiative. The GGWP NAs should also develop a strong public relations and communication media and will help to establish sustainable working relationships between the GGWP NAs and field communities in order to ensure that all stakeholders involved in land restoration initiatives, such as women and youth, are aware of the GGWP and the GGWP NAs to play a leading role in the organization of scientific events and in particular the Africa Land Summit.

Table B.3.1 In Annex 4 presents a detailed mapping of SUGAWIIGA activities by country, and intervention under GCP-financed projects.

## B.2 Implementation arrangements (max. 1500 words, approximately 3 pages plus diagrams)

The SURAGAWA design envisages working closely with a range of regional and international organizations supporting the implementation of the Great Green Wall initiative (GGW) as well as the National Governments in sub-Saharan Africa. The GGW is an ambitious initiative launched by the African Union (AU) to combat desertification, land degradation and resilience to climate change and the fight against food insecurity and migration. At the national level, Member States are expected to develop a Green Wall Action Plan (PA-GGW), which will be aligned with the National Strategic Plans of each country and national GGW priority actions. The PA-GGW is expected to play a key role in coordinating country-level

### Project Governance

164. A Regional Programme Steering Committee (PSC) will be set up to provide general oversight and strategic guidance on the implementation of the programme, and ensure that it is aligned with the GGGF objectives and well coordinated with the priorities and policies of the GGF. The PSC will identify how best to capitalize on the synergies with other projects and programmes and draw on lessons learned and how best to upscale them in the GGW initiative. The PSC will be responsible for ensuring that the programme is financially sustainable, including ensuring the cost-financing flow from the counterpart(s) and if need be, enhance resource mobilization through innovative financing mechanisms. The PSC will also ensure that the programme is designed to overcome constraints and benefit from opportunities, in order to ensure complementarities and viability of the programme. The PSC will be responsible for developing a work plan for the implementation of the programme, including for scaling up the programme and for identifying synergies and leveraging with other major international initiatives and building on lessons learned from implementing the priorities of the area, the catalytic performances, lessons learned and identifying opportunities for collaboration.

165. The first Programme Steering Committee will take place in the Pan African Agency of the Great Green Wall (PAA-GGW) in Niamey, Niger, or in a hybrid format. PSC members will consist of representatives of the African Union Commission as Chair of the PSC, and representatives of the GGGF Secretariat, the UNCCD, the European Union, other international partners, GGW-Nifs (and/or representatives of National Steering Committees or GGW coalitions) and FAO. The membership of the PSC will be reviewed on an annual basis to ensure that it reflects all key components of the programme and the GGGF global vision. The PSC will invite its key donor and implementing partners, including those in the Sahel on the GGW initiative and the Southern African Development Community (SADC-GGW) as observers, as well as other relevant stakeholders such as civil society organizations, research institutions, universities, Direct Access Entities, financial institutions, research institutions and private sector representatives will be invited as observers. The PSC will be responsible for overseeing the implementation of the GGW initiative and the implementation of multi-country programmes. PA-GGW will serve as secretariat for the PSC meetings and it will be responsible for (i) providing logistical support to the PSC, (ii) managing the administrative functions of the Secretariat in collaboration with the Regional Programme Management Unit (PMU) (described below), will include: (i) Planning and coordination of the programme activities, including preparation of meeting agendas, production and dissemination of background documents for the meetings, and (iii) production and dissemination of meetings reports.

### B

**169. SURAGAWA will also establish National Steering Committees (NSC) in each of the 8 target countries where activities are being implemented on the ground. This will be an inter-ministerial steering committee as part of the GOW national coalitions, which will include the GGW NAs and the NA to the Co-Chair, relevant implementing partners, research institutions, civil society organizations, and other stakeholders. The NSC will consist of representatives from the beneficiary countries and representatives from the beneficiary communities will be invited to the NSC as observers for advice and consultation on project implementation. The NSC will meet at least once a year in each country. There will be no meetings during the inception phase and there will be a disbursement condition for each country. Staff of the CLU will serve as secretariat to the NSCs.**

**Accredited Entities**

* **167. FAO will serve as the Accredited Entity (AE) for the programme. The multi-country programme supervision function will remain independent of the Executing Entity functions performed by FAO (i.e., executing Entities section below). As AE, FAO will be responsible for ensuring that the project is implemented in accordance with the GCF and effectively perform the AE functions. As such, FAO will be responsible for the overall management of the project, including: (i) ensuring that the project is implemented according to the approved project cycle; (ii) providing oversight throughout project implementation; (iii) ensuring funds are effectively managed to deliver results and achieve objectives in line with the GCF’s strategic plan and the GCF’s country strategy for each country; (iv) reporting to the GCF; and (v) project closure and evaluation. FAO will ensure these responsibilities in accordance with the detailed provisions of the GCF funding agreement, as well as any other relevant agreements.**

* **168. FAO will act as AE will be attributed to Resident Offices and districts at FAO headquarters (HQ), in Rome Italy and the FAO Regional Office for Africa (RAF), Sub-Regional Offices, and FAO Country Offices in target eight countries.**

* **To fulfill the AE functions, the Project Task Force (PTF) will be established by FAO in line with PTF project cycle guidelines and will be composed by the Budget Holder (BH), Lead Technical Officer (LTO), Funding Liaison Officer (FLO), HQ Technical Officer and other technical officers, as appropriate. Members of the PTF will perform the necessary functions to ensure that the project is implemented according to the approved project cycle and within the implementation period, as required. The PTF will be accountable for the quality of programme documentation and reporting in accordance with the GCF funding agreement. The PTF will also actively work to manage the agreed results of the programme and ensure appropriate use of resources.**

## 171. The regional Programme Management Unit (PMU) will be responsible to facilitate the management of the overall programme, in close cooperation with the country programmes and the respective FAO Sub-Regional Office for Africa in Senegal. In addition, a Liaison Officer will be based in the PA-GGW in Nounakout, who will act as focal point between the PMU and the Country Implementation Units (CIUs). The liaison based in PA-GGW Mauritania will enable building synergies with current and planned projects co-financing by GCF. The PMU will also provide overall management of the regional level activities, including the CIUs efforts to implement activities at country level, as well as implement regional level activities. It will also monitor the progress of all programme activities in close cooperation with the respective FAO Sub-Regional Office for Africa and all programme activities. Furthermore, the PMU will provide overall management of the programme, in collaboration with the GCF Secretariat.

## 172. The actual implementation of the programme will be undertaken at the country level through Country Implementation Units (CIUs) which will be housed at the FAO country offices (in the case of Mauritania and Senegal) or at the respective national institutions (in the case of Mali, Niger, Burkina Faso and Côte d'Ivoire). The Coordinator will be appointed by FAO in consultation with the regional counterpart institutions. Each CIU will also need to appoint a focal point for each activity. The CIUs will consist of an implementation team consisting of a project specialist, an ecological and monitoring and evaluation specialist, and a social safeguards and gender specialist, as well as a secretariat.

## 173. Direct implementation on the ground will be undertaken at country level through a combination of modalities that will use the well-established capacity of FAO and government co-Existing Entities in the respective host countries. The CIUs will consist of an implementation team consisting of a project specialist, an ecological and monitoring and evaluation specialist, and a social safeguards and gender specialist, as well as a secretariat.

## 174. In line with the programme implementation arrangements outlined above, the Funded Activity Agreement (FAA) will be signed between FAO and GCF and GCF proceeds received by FAO in its capacity as Accredited Entity will flow to the respective CIUs. The FAA will outline the terms of the agreement for the execution of activities funded by GCF proceed and the execution of programme activities funded by GCF proceed.

## 175. The Project Agreement will be signed between the Government of each of the eight countries which also include relevant clauses related to co-financing (and execution of the cofinancing) from the Government (Subsidiary Agreement).

## 176. FAO, a specialized technical agency of the United Nations, is well placed to undertake the planned activities given its experience in addressing climate change adaptation and mitigation, reducing poverty and boosting resilient development. FAO is a key technical partner of the African Union and its Member States, which have devised a clear and determined strategy for scaling up their national climate strategies and action plans. The African Union Commission and AU member states as they prepared their national climate strategies and action plans, including NAPs, are fully aware that the success of these strategies will depend on the availability of adequate resources and mobilised partnerships. A major outcome was that all country action plans include large-scale restoration of high-value ecosystems such as forests, wetlands, grasslands, savannas, mangroves, and other semi-natural and natural landscapes. FAO will build on its ongoing partnerships - African Union Commission, PA-GGN, UNCCD, EU, ADB, UNEP, IUCN (WBG), FAO, AFSD, BADEA, regional research and other organizations and NGOs – to ensure proper coordination of the activities in the countries. The capacity of the GEF Secretariat, the technical expertise, the institutional and organizational qualities and experience are well suited to manage this large multi-country programme. Additional resources will be mobilized from the GCF, the Global Environment Facility (GEF), the World Bank, and Global Affairs Canada, reflecting the ongoing partnership and collaboration towards scaling up climate change adaptation and mitigation.

## Justification for GCF funding request

### B.3. Justification for GCF funding request (max: 1000 words; approximately 2 pages)

182. GCF funding is requested for the CGI because of the large unmet needs of the programme. The recent UNCTAD report on “The State of Smallholder Farmers in Africa” highlights that while progress has been made, implementation continue, the CGI’s objectives and all the co-benefits of this flagship initiative will not be met. According to the report, smallholder farmers are still struggling with access to finance, markets, and inputs. The CGI will be required to support countries in achieving the CGI objectives by 2030. With the shift in focus of the CGI from food security to climate change adaptation and mitigation, CGI will need to continue supporting smallholder farmers and pastoralist communities to increase food security, create jobs and boost the local economy through the implementation of CGI’s activities. The CGI will also need to support countries in achieving their national targets to ensure long-term sustainability in the entire Sahel region. Stronger support, coordination, and engagement with CGI partners is required for this purpose. CGI will continue to be a key partner in efforts for harnessing emerging technologies and innovations and promoting policy options that unlock market opportunities and capital for scaling up interventions. Financial needs for impact at scale are estimated to average between US$ 450-1300 million per year, depending on the specific CGI activities implemented within each country and the international sources. GCF funding is critical not only on terms of the direct support it can provide but the potential for leveraging other resources.


---
**Page 65**
---

183. GCF funding is justified because the programme is located in very vulnerable and fragile areas of the selected countries that are increasingly affected by climate risks and disasters. The enhancement of the livelihoods through the increase of extreme weather events, the variability of rainfall and the harsh climate conditions, in combination with sometimes poor land-use practices, are eroding the productivity of the fragile lands and ecosystems. Of the eight countries, seven have been identified as being highly vulnerable to climate change impacts and are characterized by acute resilience indices. The land tenure pattern in the areas being targeted includes both common property resources and individual holdings. These lands are often used for grazing livestock and growing crops. They are also used for water collection in the common lands over which the pastoral and farming communities have use rights. The individual households who are the owners of these lands are not able to invest in them because they do not have access to credit, while the upfront costs of rehabilitating the land are high and the benefits are diffused among a large number of households who are often unable to pay for inputs such as seeds or fertilizers. In addition, the small size of individual holdings and their risk profile is such that, if any, financial institutions have the risk appetite to loan to them for adapting their agricultural practices. The lack of tenure security also means that there is no certainty about the future use of these lands; there is no security of tenure, production is scattered and volumes and quality do not justify investments with the high associated costs. This makes it difficult for private investors to invest in these areas.

The public sector has limited resources and other urgent priorities which take precedence. These countries are highly vulnerable to climate change impacts and are characterized by acute resilience indices. They also have vast migration benefits from the restoration of agro-pasto-landscapes and ecosystems, as well as improving the resilience and livelihoods of the poor communities depending upon these areas.

184. GCF funding is being requested for SUGAWAIA to ensure the success of the GGG initiative by providing significant financial resources in support of the implementation of the Green Climate Fund (GCF) in Africa. GCF support is imperative for the GGG which is an African-led flagship programme demonstrating how the harnessing of power from nature can help countries achieve their climate and development goals. The GGG has been a key driver in the implementation of the post-2020 global biodiversity framework, GGG is among the iconic global campaigns targeted for funding by the GCF. Since its launch in 2018, SUGAWAIA has made significant progress towards its objectives and has attracted financing from a host of other donors and have been doing so for the last two decades, the financial needs are not only high but also increasing with time. The SUGAWAIA initiative is being implemented by a multi-co-organized by France, the United Nations and World Bank in January 2021. However, not all the resources required to implement this initiative have been secured yet. In addition, the countries involved are facing a post COVID context. Sahelian countries are struggling with budgets and funding and firm commitment and support is required. While the funding requested from GCF is relatively small in relation to overall GGG financing needs, it will contribute significantly towards the achievement of the SUGAWAIA initiative’s objectives which aim at enhancing climate resilience of vulnerable communities threatened by climate change.

The SUGAWAIA multi-country programme will support smallholders to enable them to undertake land restoration and rehabilitation activities on their degraded or abandoned degraded farmlands. For highly degraded common lands, this investment cost will include the cost of deep ploughing through the use of both mechanized, animal traction and manual methods; the cost of planting trees and shrubs in combination with grasses for soil recuperating soil health, provision of seeds and seedlings and the cost of capacity building and training in the use of machinery and other tools. The investment will also include the cost of establishment of nurseries as well as costs of restoring degraded lands and the surrounding ecosystems. Thereafter, the management of the restored lands will be done by the communities themselves through the creation of community-based organizations such as animal fodder. As the trees grow in the managed restoration areas, the production of non-timber forest products will provide sufficient economic incentives for local communities to continue managing and using these lands on a sustainable basis.

186. SUGAWAIA will also provide funds to hire service providers to organize the women and men who rely on non-timber forest products and help them facilitate access to markets and build links with the private sector to earn

additional income from a range of promising products such as Balanites, baobab, neem, jujube, forage crops, etc The programme will finance the cost of establishing and organizing commodity groups, basic equipment and investments in land restoration activities. The GCF grant is essential as the Government does not have the resources to cover these investment costs due to the limited public sector capacity to generate funds and the high level of country indebtedness that inhibits diversification of funding sources. The GCF grant will provide a significant proportion of the required financial incentive for it invest without providing the local communities and smallholder farmers with initial support in helping them to establish groups, produce, increase volume and quantity of non-timber forest products to make the enterprise viable for them.

187. GCF concessionality is further justified based on the significant potential for SUBAWAGNA to generate climate change mitigation benefits through land restoration activities. The programme will finance investments in the rehabilitation of the degraded lands in the Sahel and helping smallholder producers adapt to the climate threat by diversifying their livelihoods, increasing resilience and improving food security.

The level of concessionality requested for the countries under SUBAWAGNA is justified based on their economic and environmental vulnerability, the high level of country indebtedness and the low level of readiness to adopt to climate change. The programme will finance investments in land restoration activities that are expected to generate carbon sequestration and investment in vulnerable hotspots at minimal risk. Component 3 is designed to strengthen the institutional capacity for the implementation of the project through the establishment of a model and replicating the positive experience from successful projects of this nature. The investment in this component is justified based on the need to provide initial support to communities and institutions strengthening in supporting land restoration activities by local communities and smallholder farmers.

B.5 Exit strategy (max. 500 words, approximately |1 page)

189. The multi-country programme has developed a clear exit strategy for its investments. Under component 3 SUBAWAGNA will make just one commitment in the restoration of both highly degraded common lands and moderately degraded smallholder farmlands. The programme will monitor and support the investments for the first two years after which it is expected that communities will be able to sustain themselves without further support due to better defined use rights and the additional incentives of using the grasses for forage for their animals and other uses. Communities will also have the capacity to manage the resources in a sustainable manner, with groups or establish new ones to undertake the land rehabilitation and value chain development activities. The programme will provide initial support to communities through the establishment of Village Technicians who will provide technical advice and support and follow up on the restoration activities and assist in the monitoring and maintenance of the investments once they are established. These technicians will be paid a salary by the communities, with the programme providing support and assistance on an on-going basis. They also have the capacity for creating supportive networks and working together to ensure that the resources are managed sustainably.

The communities prioritize rewarding the Village Technicians from the first benefits or incomes coming from restoration activities through their own efforts. This provides additional incentives to them to continue their support. Communities under SUBAWAGNA will be expected to establish commodity groups, which will provide an additional incentive for productivity seed and planting production for land restoration activities. These groups are expected to sustain themselves through the sale of the products they produce. The programme will also provide initial support in the form of deep ploughing equipment, training local technicians in restoration techniques and providing a model of community organization that can be replicated by other communities and sapping from local supplies to support the restoration activities on their own either individually or collectively.

## Exit Strategy under Component 2 dealing with non-timber forest products hinges on the continued interest of smallholder producers in NTFP production and marketing and its engagement with the private sector.

### Techniques for improving and enhancing bio-diversity from research and forestry centres in the selected countries will be incorporated into the modification in the curriculum for extension personnel staff and community

### Incorporating knowledge of the traditional and local practices so that the techniques are implemented by those trained in these methods

190. The exit strategy under component 2 dealing with non-timber forest products hinges on the continued interest of smallholder producers in NTFP production and marketing and its engagement with the private sector. Local communities will be involved in the development of value chains for NTFPs, which will be supported by the private sector along these chains. The private sector actors are involved in aggregating the products, transporting them to markets, and marketing them. Smallholder producers have shown interest in a private sector interest in a range of NTFPs; however, their engagement has been limited due to lack of community organization and weak linkages with the private sector. This project will provide the necessary support for communities according to an agreed schedule. During the formulation stage, private sector entities have shown interest; e.g., recruiting purchasing arrangements, and utilizing their own funds to up-grade the processing and marketing of NTFPs.

191. It is also expected that SURAGOWA will facilitate links to enable the value chain actors to access finance from a range of commercial financial institutions. The Output on Access to Finance (Output 2.3) will address key challenges in accessing credit for smallholder producers, including lack of collateral and weak linkages with financial sustainability beyond the project cycle through building the capacities of financial institutions and the providers of credit in rural areas such as village savings associations and microfinance institutions. The development of innovative products and channels such as digital financial solutions and enhanced access to insurance will be critical for smallholder producers, especially those living in remote or sparsely urbanized areas. The development of innovative products and channels such as digital financial solutions and enhanced access to insurance will be critical for smallholder producers, especially those living in remote or sparsely urbanized areas.
