## The Output on Access to Finance (Output 2.3) will address key constraints to finance, particularly around the uptake and efficacy of credit. The activity ensures the outputs sustainability beyond the project life cycle through building capacity for financial service providers in rural areas such as village savings associations and formal or informal microfinance institutions. The development of innovative products and channels such as digital financial solutions will enhance the potential reach of financial services to small-scale farmers, agribusinesses, and other enterprises. This will enable them to access credit from banks, farmers' organizations or MSMEs to build their collateral for future credit, even beyond the project life cycle. This can be achieved through the use of a GCF Access Facility (GCF AF) which is an innovative financing mechanism that allows entities such as Banque Agrofin du Sénégal have applied for accreditation with GCF and in the future they could use the GCF Facility to expand their own operations to contribute further to the objectives of the GGW as well.

## The interventions designed under Component 3 are focused on strengthening the capacity at the country and regional level to galvanize further support for the GGIF Initiative. SGRAGWA will seek to strengthen existing institutions, such as national and regional GCF entities, in order to provide a more effective and efficient structure. Similarly, existing coordination and governance frameworks will be used to the fullest extent possible, while ensuring that all interventions are implemented in an integrated manner with other initiatives in a manner that is consistent with national and international standards. This will contribute to long-term sustainability by ensuring that GGIF-related activities are embedded within the broader institutional framework.

## The development of strong capacity for monitoring and evaluation is designed to demonstrate the success of the initiative based on strong and credible evidence and generate additional funding sources. The component will primarily focus on strengthening the capacity of national and regional GCF institutions, which in turn will enable the national and regional GGW institutions subsequently be able to generate information of significant value to their decision makers and their development partners at a reasonable cost. ### 196.
The programme will support both capacity building and technology transfer. Capacity building activities will include building the technical expertise of community members in rehabilitation of degraded rangelands, soils, lands and enhancing their capacity for adaptation to climate change through training on water harvesting techniques, soil conservation practices, agroforestry systems and production of both local plant varieties, forage crops, appropriate shrubs and bushes for soil conservation and regeneration. Technology transfer activities will include the introduction of new technologies and best agricultural practices in the face of change in precipitation and temperatures by varying the planting time and using suitable seeds and other inputs. It is expected that USD 10 million will be used from the GCF funding amount for capacity building at the


---
**Page 76**
---

### GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 70

#### 2019

The project will also introduce new technologies for rehabilitation of land in the area such as direct ploughing and contour ploughing which mitigates the impacts of floods, storms, and landslides on the crops by reducing soil erosion up to 50 percent, controlling runoff water, increasing moisture infiltration and retention, and improving soil structure. The project will also introduce new technologies for rehabilitation of private sector farms that will use their equipment for contour ploughing and making contour beds and establishing terraces. These technologies are expected to reduce the cost of land rehabilitation by USD 33 million of GCF funds will be used to rent farm equipment from private sector service providers and to pay for the services.

There is also expected to be transfer of technology with regards to monitoring and evaluation tools to measure the impact of the programme activities as a result of increase of vegetative cover, carbon sequestration, and biodiversity. The project will develop a Framework for Ecosystem Restoration Monitoring (FERM) and an associated geospatial platform to monitor progress in restoration projects at different scales. The FERM framework is guided by the best available science. The national governments will be trained in the use of the FERM platform and will be responsible for monitoring their own projects. The project will support these systems with a science-based restoration movement through transparent and fit-for-purpose monitoring to support the United Nations Decade on Ecosystem Restoration. Users are able to monitor progress at different scales, from local to national, and can also use the platform as an advocacy dimension for their ecosystem of interest. The FERM platform also has functionality for uploading national and sub-national restoration plans and will be used by governments to track progress against these plans.

The platform includes functionality for creating compelling restoration impact stories, based on user specific geographical data, which can be shared with stakeholders and the public.

For the more advanced user, FAO will provide and train users in the integration with GCF’s geospatial processing platform, SEPAL, to analyse and regularly update the programme’s geographic information with near real-time satellite imagery. This will allow users to monitor progress of their projects and share their information on restoration progress at different scales through the FERM Registry which will be interoperable with other platforms such as the GCF registry for reporting and monitoring.

Under the programme, training and orientation will also be provided on the EX-AD (Ex-Ante Carbon Assessment) tool. The EX-AD tool is a decision support system that allows users to assess the carbon sequestration potential of their land use change scenarios in terms of tonnes of CO2 equivalent per hectare annually and its equivalent in terms of economic returns: (i) Tool for Agroecology Performance Evaluation (TAPeE) which will aim to provide evidence to policymakers and other stakeholders on how agroecology can contribute to the achievement of the Sustainable Development Goals, (ii) Tool for Agroecology Transition (TaATe) which will aim to provide evidence to policymakers and other stakeholders on how agroecology can contribute to the achievement of the Sustainable Development Goals. The TAPeE and TaATe tools are designed to be used by governments, civil society organizations, research institutions, and private sector actors at different levels and in different contexts.

The DyTAES network™ will also enable cross-sectoral and cross-ministerial cooperation. It will contribute to the empowerment of farmers and other stakeholders through capacity building on agroecology knowledge and performance. Assistance on this tool will be on an tailored basis, depending on country interest.

### EXPECTED PERFORMANCE AGAINST INVESTMENT CRITERIA

The assessment is of the performance of the project/programme against the investment criteria as set out in the GCF’s Total Investment Framework.

#### D.1 Impact potential (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

203 SURAGIRWA is designed to contribute to the achievement of GDP’s objectives by providing a paradigm shift towards low emissions agriculture and forestry practices that reduce greenhouse gas emissions and help vulnerable communities in the Sahel adapt to the impacts of climate change. Preliminary estimates indicate that land restoration efforts funded under the project will result in an average reduction of 10 million tonnes CO2 equivalent (CO2e) by 2035.

The main investment area which is expected to contribute to creating carbon sinks is the restoration of 1,273 million hectares of degraded lands. The analysis shows that the land restoration activities are expected to result in a net increase due to the restoration activities in eight Sahel countries covered by the programme. The analyses are mainly based on the results from the first phase of the project and the second phase is expected to be more robust as it will be measured using the Collect Earth methodology to confirm measured canopy cover into carbon stocks change.

204 The multi-country programme is expected to directly benefit 1,881,352 people and indirectly benefit an estimated at least 7.9 million people through the promotion of agro-ecological practices in degraded lands. These targeted communities are mainly agro-pastoralist farmers and use an agro-eco-pastoral system that provides them with a diversified income from livestock production, crop production, and non-timber forest products such as description, and fishponds, reducing runoff, increasing infiltration of rainwater, regenerating grasses for livestock and improving soil fertility.

205 The logged communal areas own around 172.6 million ha of large ruminant and less than 30,000 ml small ruminants, based on the assumption that participating households own livestock in the same proportion as in the country on average with each HH owning an average of one large ruminant and two small ruminants. The project will contribute to improving animal health and status and productivity. It is expected that with increased availability of fodder and forage crops and diversified income from livestock production, crop production, and non-timber forest products such as description, and fishponds, reducing runoff, increasing infiltration of rainwater, regenerating grasses for livestock and improving soil fertility in hard land will also have an appreciable impact on methane emissions.

206 The programme will also introduce improved and new climate-resilient livelihood options such as through increased participation in the production of non-timber forest products (NTFPs) and agro-ecological practices. This is expected to be achieved by the establishment of 1,500 ha of NTFP plantations and the sale of these forest products. Food security is expected to improve both directly and indirectly, as the main immediate impact will be through increased livestock productivity due to access to improved feed resources and water security with a result of increased crop yields. The programme will also contribute to increased fish production by increasing the availability of forage crops and fishponds. Water security will improve as a result of ecosystem restoration and retention of water in soils and its remediation from pollution, which will be achieved through the establishment of 100 ha of agro-ecological degraded lands which will strengthen climate change resilience through selection of more resilient plant species, creating buffers to reduce runoff and erosion.

207 The programme is expected to have a significant impact on women. About 8,236.813 beneficiaries of the programme or 57% are expected to be women (Annex 2). Of these, 937,040 are expected to be direct female beneficiaries and 1,297,773 are expected to be indirect female beneficiaries. The programme will contribute to increasing women’s income and employment opportunities through its investments in both land rehabilitation and NTFP which is a key source of income for women. Women’s participation will be encouraged and the specific targets and budgets allocated for women in each of the output areas. This will be achieved under an transformative approach which requires identifying structural and non-structural barriers preventing women’s access.

## Strategic Programming Target Area D

### GCF-SURAGAWA proposal USP-2 Target area

#### Description
The programme is aligned with the banking results for 2024, 2025 or the updated Strategic Plan: (i) Support for developing countries to achieve a reduction of 1.27 billion tonnes of CO₂ equivalent by 2030 through climate-resilient land use and sustainable restoration while supporting food security.

#### Contribution
- **Food**: 190 to 280 million beneficiaries adopting low-emission climate-resilient agricultural and fisheries practices.
- **Ecosystems**: 120 to 190 million hectares of terrestrial and coastal areas protected, restored or brought under sustainable management.
- **Locally-led Adaptation**: 900 to 1500 local private sector early stage actors supported through market-based seed and early-stage capital for innovative climate-resilient agricultural business models and technologies.
- **Innovation and Market Creation**: 90 to 120 national and regional financial institutions supported with resources, and other green finance, particularly for SMEs.
- **Greening Finance**: Supporting local financial institutions to develop their capacity to mobilize resources and financing capacities for future green solutions.

#### D.3. Paradigm shift (main: 500 words; approximately 1 page)

There are several key elements included in the SURAGAWA approach which are expected to catalyse impact beyond a one-off programme investment. In this respect, SURAGAWA is expected to be transformative and have long-term benefits for countries by supporting them to develop climate-resilient rehabilitation investments in highly degraded common lands and moderately degraded farm lands. The local government will be supported to establish rental markets for land and associated resources, with the aim of raising revenues thereby encouraging them to make investments in these lands. Furthermore, the programme will facilitate the development of community-based institutions that can manage these resources collectively or individually. The establishment of the rental markets will also lead to the development of associated energy services, such as solar power generation for irrigation and other uses, including water supply etc. The establishment of community groups to collect local seed varieties and the setting of private nurseries

D.3. Sustainable development (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)
213. The programme is expected to address several of the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) namely SDG 1 which is aimed at eradicating poverty, SDG 2 which commits to zero hunger, gender equality (SDG 5), decent work and economic growth (SDG 8); Climate action (SDG 13); Life on land (SDG 15), and Partnership for the Goals (SDG 17). The programme will also contribute towards SDGs 6 (clean water) and 14 (life below water) as it is being made in soil and water conservation (SWC), which show that these investments result in improved agricultural productivity, increased resilience to climate change, enhanced biodiversity, increased carbon sequestration, and increase in biodiversity. These investments also have an impact in reducing migration and poverty by improving the livelihoods of the communities involved.
214. Environmental benefits: The programme is designed to help secure several environmental benefits through its investments in participatory land restoration and subsequent sustainable management of natural resources. This will be achieved through the rehabilitation of degraded lands, which will result in carbon sequestration through increasing the grass and tree cover on degraded lands. It is expected that CO2 emissions will be reduced during the programme’s lifecycle due to land restoration by local communities through the introduction of trees and shrubs, as well as the reduction of soil erosion from agricultural lands. For the latter, the programme will rehabilitate the selected sites through deep ploughs which creates micro-catchments for water retention and infiltration, and by planting grasses and shrubs that help in loosening the soil and improving nutrient access. Tree and grass seedlings are trapped in micro-catchments where they grow and provide shade to other plants, as well as providing food and shelter for pollinators and other animals into the area, increasing biodiversity. The programme will introduce indigenous trees and shrubs which have been shown to be more effective at soil retention and water retention and maintain soil temperatures suitable for crop production. In Niger, farmers have developed innovative techniques such as “tree planting” and “tree planting with grasses” that help in soil retention and the area, also introduce farmer-managed natural regeneration (FMNR) first pioneered by outside actors but quickly adopted by local communities. FMNR has been shown to be more effective than the great droughts of the early 1970’s and the 1880’s, have ingeniously modified traditional agronomy, water and soil management practices to restore the fertility of their land.
215. The programme will help improve food security by providing training and social, present challenges for climate adaptation for pastoralists.113 The herders and farmers in the area complete their annual migration cycle from the dry season pastures to the wet season fields. D

## Economic co-benefits: smallholder income diversification.

It is expected that the programme will lead to a range of economic benefits that will enhance the income of poor smallholder farmers, and thus provide the underlying basis for poverty reduction in the Sahel region. These include increased productivity of lands through increased production of forage and fodder crops, increased productivity of livestock, and improved access to markets. The programme will also increase the availability of food products such as Bambara, gum arabic, baobab leaves, neem, honey and sale of seeds and seedlings, etc. The GCF project will contribute to the diversification of smallholder income sources in the Sahel countries. The programme is designed to strengthen selected NTFP value chains identified by local communities in Burkina Faso, Mali, Niger and Senegal. This includes increasing the production volume of each of the NTFP and assist with better collection, storage, transport and processing techniques to enhance the value of the production. The experience of on-going projects has also demonstrated substantial potential for diversification through the introduction of new crops (e.g., sesame in Burkina Faso; Senegal). Improved beekeeping and honey production also offer a sustainable source of income. Well-fertilized lands with improved soil fertility allow the cultivation of cash crops such as cotton, while other species growing on the rehabilitated sites allow some income generating activities like Cessia tora for the production of charcoal or firewood. The programme will also contribute to the diversification of smallholder income sources through the rehabilitation of the lands, during crop production and the collection, sale, transport and processing of NTFPs. This includes increasing the production volume of each of the NTFP and assist with better collection equipment and machinery for land rehabilitation and its upscaling. The activities are expected to generate employment opportunities in the rural areas through additional employment for drivers, transporters, mechanics, processors and other value chain actors.

## Gender-sensitive development benefit: Gender inequality within the Sahel is particularly pronounced. Women play a key role in the production of food crops such as millet, sorghum, maize, rice, beans, wheat, fodder, fuelwood, care of livestock, vegetable gardening, etc. This responsibility has become harder to assume due to extreme weather events and changing climate which impacts water, food and feed availability. The programme will contribute to the empowerment of women in the Sahel region by increasing their access to land, credit, inputs, markets and extension services. It is expected that this will increase the productivity of smallholder farms and pastoralists. The programme will enhance women's income and employment opportunities through increased production volume of each of the output categories, including women from smallholder farmers and pastoralists. The programme has identified a Gender Action Plan (Annex 8) which indicates clearly how women will be involved in all aspects of the project implementation. Specific activities are proposed for each of the output areas. Specific actions will be developed to strengthen the technical and managerial capacity of women, including training on gender equality and empowerment, and awareness raising about the appropriate indicator to measure women's participation, livelihoods, employment and incomes. However, it is important to note that this project does not aim at changing the social norms and cultural resilience for the agriculture and pastoralist livelihoods in the Sahel. Such an approach is likely to generate sustained change only if it is accompanied by a broader programme of action aimed at gradually and slowly change some of the deep-seated patriarchal norms and the traditional perceptions held about girls and women.

### 218 The Sahel countries, and in particular the GGW area are highly vulnerable in both economic and environmental terms. They are also among the most affected by climate change and its associated vulnerability to climate disruptions and assessing a country's readiness by considering economic governance, institutional capacity, and social resilience. In this regard, the 2019 N-Gain Index for countries shows a high degree of vulnerability and lack of readiness. Five of the countries in the project are in the bottom quintile (25%) of the 191 countries assessed. The average score is 34%, which places them at the bottom 21% of the 191 countries assessed. The chronic nature of the vulnerability and lack of readiness is indicated by the fact that some have static lines and their movement in their scores over the last 25 years as indicated by the figure below.

### 219 Participating countries in this programme are highly vulnerable to climate threats and risks such as drought, rising temperatures and variability and unpredictability in rainfall which can cause disease in both plants and animals, locust infestations etc. Their exposure and vulnerability to climate hazards is projected to increase in the future due to climate change and associated extreme weather events such as heat waves and heavy rain events. In most scenarios, temperature rise will accelerate, meaning that the continent on average could be 5°C warmer by 2081-2100 compared with pre-industrial levels. This would have a significant adverse negative impacts on the fragile landscapes in this vulnerable area. The desert has been encroaching on the land and is expected to continue to do so, leading to further degradation of the environment. The reduction in productivity directly impacts food security, income, employment and it increases rural poverty. The figure below outlines the vulnerability and readiness indexes of the Sahel countries. In the current vulnerability index and readiness to resilience -the eight countries rank between 123-186 out of 154 countries.

The debt-to-GDP ratios and the debt to GDP ratios are very high and exceed 50% in most cases with rates as high as 73% in Senegal and 65% in Chad. There is considerable uncertainty about how much medium-term debt sustainability will be affected by the economic impact of COVID-19 which is clear, however that buffers have been eroded and another subsequent crisis would make it even harder to light unless there are efforts to reduce debt risk and shift to better ways of accessing resources.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 80

## Senegal

Senegal's Nationally Determined Contribution (NDC), adopted in 2015, is part of the forward-looking "Plan Sénégal Emergent (PSE)" vision, its strategy and development pass, as well as the National Climate Change Strategy.

### The Sahel countries are low-emission countries. Nevertheless, each has made a commitment to lower emissions further taking into consideration their business-as-usual scenarios. At the end of 2015, all the Sahel countries had submitted NDCs and most recently updated them in which they stressed their vulnerability to climate change, especially for food security, water resources, and health. They also highlighted the need for adaptation actions and the inclusion of adaptation actions.

### While the proposed NDC adaptation actions reflect each country's priorities, there are some actions that are common to the countries given that they face similar challenges in terms of climate change vulnerability. These include: improved land management, afforestation/reforestation/forestry and improved practices; climate-smart or conservation agriculture including agroforestry, soil fertility improvement, water harvesting and efficient use, and early warning systems.

### Improved husbandry, feed and animal management practices can enhance productivity and assist in reducing methane emissions from livestock, water harvesting and efficiency, and early warning systems.


| Table 24 | Country NDCs and Climate Change Interventions for SUDAN/SAHEL |
| --- | --- |
| Sudan/South Sudan | Migration of people and animals to other countries by 2050 (i.e. 29-47%) compared to the Business as usual scenario |
| Chad | Cumulative reduction of GDP emissions by 2030 (unconditional and conditional measures) with an average annual growth rate of 10% compared to the baseline scenario |
| Djibouti | The Republic of Djibouti is committed to reducing CO2 emissions by 20% by 2030, i.e. nearly 284 million tonnes |
| Mali | Commits to reducing emissions by 25% for forests and land use, including the restoration of forests and land; each, by 2020, and in common with other countries in the region, is contingent upon international support, although there are no specific measures. The NDC includes a section on adaptation, though only for this period (2015-2020) |

### Six priority areas:
* Ensuring water access
* Improving land management and soil fertility
* Forestry, fishery and tourism actions
* Reduce vulnerability to climate change by focusing on the most exposed social, economic or geographic groups
* Strengthening institutional capacity to maintain the services they provide
* Ensure the development of a national adaptation plan

### Actions:
* Agriculture, livestock and fisheries: Develop interest in agroforestry, soil fertility improvement, improved inputs (organic fertilizers including compost), conservation agriculture, climate-smart or conservation farming, improved fodder banks, croplivestock integration, animal species, breeds and genetic resources
* Water: Improve water access for all users, especially women and children; improve irrigation systems and water collection structures. Wastewater treatment and reuse of treated wastewater

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 82

## There is strong ownership in the Sahel for SURAGWA.

At the project inception and formulation stage, there was close participation through regional and country level workshops, individual interviews, and extensive desk research to identify specific activities, financial requirements, local contributions and set targets for each country, using well-established participatory approaches. The process of identifying specific activities was further identified by identifying opportunities for seeking international climate finance for the project, establishing partnerships with other donors and agencies, and engaging in dialogue with countries on their needs also making efforts for additional technical assistance and funding for the establishment of a Green Financing Facility (GFF) in each country. This approach has ensured that the project is owned by the countries, credit contributes to de-risking the sector and foster greater knowledge on climate finance and adaptation assets in the Sahel region. The project will also help countries overcome the key barriers that countries face in loan financing and enable them to engage in green financing.

## Design component 3) is the outcome of a two-year on-going consultation process with the selected beneficiaries, stakeholders and partners involved in the project implementation.

The design concept note and the Full Funding Proposal was based on a series of workshops that allowed participants to express their needs and to align future activities with existing programmatic frameworks at the national and regional level. The workshops were also used to identify specific areas for action such as land rehabilitation and degradation/restoration, coordination and planning, communication, resource mobilization and knowledge management. These priorities are also consistent with those expressed in the GGGI strategy prepared by the region and the GGGI country strategies prepared by each of the countries involved in the project.

The second round of engagement to identify more specific areas of work - among the themes listed above - where SURAGWA can make the most value, taking into account the capacity building initiatives of other Development Partners.

## Efficiency and effectiveness (max. 300 words; approximately 1 page)

### Financial Structure

The financial structure of the programme is based on cost estimates of land rehabilitation on both highly degraded and moderately degraded farm land as well as the other costs associated with the project activities, including institutional strengthening for enhanced coordination, implementation and monitoring and evaluation.

The total cost estimate for the project is US$ 105 million. The project has been designed to be implemented in a phased manner over three years (2014-2017) and will be funded by the Global Environment Facility (GEF). The GEF has confirmed the project as part of its Land Degradation Neutrality (LDN) initiative.

The project is expected to benefit 3.5 million people in the Sahel region, including vulnerable communities in the proposed sites. The programme has been designed to provide only a one-time input into the land rehabilitation process and it is up to farmers and pastoralists to sustain the restored areas because the potential for forgone or NTFPs from the restored land will be greater than what they can produce on their own. If they do so, then the technical, organizational and commercial capacities of the farmers and pastoralists have been strengthened through the project activities. The programme is designed in such a way that the restoration of degraded land will ensure scaling up of project results.

### Private Sector Incentives

In order to make the investment viable, there is a strong case envisaged for private sector involvement. This can be encouraged by the development of a vibrant farm equipment rental market for hiring deep ploughs to implement the Valameter System (VS). This is a low-cost, high-profit business opportunity that can be replicated in other countries and regions within the range of countries where it has been implemented. SURAGWA has modified the earlier approach of providing subsidies to farmers by creating an institutional framework for the development of a rental market for a more sustainable approach to land restoration. In addition, the value chain activities for NTFPs

## Cost-Benefit Analysis of the Mitigation and Adaptation Outcomes: GCF is being requested to provide financial support for projects that will mitigate climate change.

The following table provides a summary of the cost per tonne of CO2e avoided by the projects, which are expected to avoid between 1.05 million tons of CO2e equivalent at an unit cost of USD 3.41 per metric ton. This is below the average cost of USD 8.6 per metric ton for carbon removal technologies that have been deployed in large-scale projects and is likely to be a relatively low-cost option for carbon sequestration compared to other types of carbon removal activities such as afforestation and reforestation, direct air capture, and blue carbon.

This SUGARWAGA unit cost of USD 3.41 per CO2e equivalent compares very favourably with the most recent estimates of the social cost of carbon, which the World Bank puts at just under USD 55 and USD 108 per ton for carbon dioxide emissions in 2020.

## Results of the Economic and Financial Analysis: The Economic and Financial Analysis (EFA) of the proposed project provides a comparison between the cost per unit of carbon sequestration for each of the typical carbon removal activities and the land restoration measures that are expected to be implemented in the projects (afforestation, reforestation, soil carbon sequestration, direct air capture, blue carbon, agro-forestry) as well as the social benefits of smallholder tree value chains such as agro-processing, storage and marketing.

The result of the financial analysis from the (the private – point view of smallholder beneficiaries) compares the cost per unit of carbon sequestration for each of the typical carbon removal activities and the land restoration measures that are expected to be implemented in the projects (afforestation, reforestation, soil carbon sequestration, direct air capture, blue carbon, agro-forestry) as well as the social benefits of smallholder tree value chains such as agro-processing, storage and marketing.

The analysis shows a return of USD 1.52 on average for each USD invested, with outcomes ranging from 1.1 USD to 3.19 USD per tonne of CO2e avoided. The adoption of the proposed project by smallholder farmers in the region would result in an increase in household income (up to 65%) over the first two years of the investment, depending on the level of adoption.

233. Economically speaking, SURAGAWA is a moderately viable programme, generating a net present value (NPV) of USD 158.4 million and an economic internal rate of return (EIRR) of 19 percent (on a total budget of USD 220.0 million, USD 163.0 million of which would be funded by GCF), even without considering the potential for co-benefits. The project has the potential to benefit local communities involved, in combination with their extremely limited access to financial services and the high level of poverty in the region. It is also expected that the project will generate significant additional funding from the GCF.

The economic potential of the project, when the projected GHG mitigation were valued appropriately, is much higher. Using more conservative prices, at current market price (31 USD per tCO2-eq), SURAGAWA would have an NPV of USD 405 million and a net present value internal rate of return (NPV-internal rate of return) of 97.5 percent over a 20-year period. These results further emphasize the need to consider the co-benefits that are expected from this project, as well as its potential economic benefits created. An analysis was undertaken of the returns on some of the key NTFP which show high potential for profitability.

### Industry Best Practices: SURAGAWA has been patterned on the best practices of the Action Against Desertification project in its pilot and scaled up versions. Lessons have been learnt from these projects over the years, including the following:

- The use of a participatory approach (participatory project preparation technique); primary emphasis on developing community buy-in, management skills, and benefits derived from the project.
- The decision to purchase only one small tractor in the previous design as a more cost-effective, efficient and sustainable option. The first of this is the decision not to purchase any heavy equipment by the project but to encourage the use of rented equipment that will be available for other projects in the region.

The second adjustment is the focus on seed and seedling production to ensure that adequate seed is available for future crops and for immediate local needs, which can also lead to change.

### Leverage and co-financing: By demonstrating effective approaches for greening the Sahel and helping to build a market for NTFPs, SURAGAWA will create considerable resources that are potentially available from a host of development partners committed to providing funds for climate change mitigation projects in the region. The project has been designed with a strong financial structure potential to attract funds from a host of other agencies such as the World Bank, EU, ADB, AFDB, IFAD, GEF etc., which will be able to co-finance the project.

### Mobilization of private and commercial funds: It is expected that SURAGAWA will result in mobilizing both private and commercial capital for the sustainable development of the Sahel. When pastoralists and farmers are given a real incentive to present viable capital investments made by the project, they can be expected to invest their own resources as well as those from other sources such as it is the eco-systems on which they depend. Similarly, enterprises dealing with NTFP value chains are particularly vulnerable in the Sahel region because of the lack of access to credit and financial services and increase their incomes. These small enterprises are expected to invest their own capital as well as have access to other sources such as microfinance institutions (MFIs) and commercial banks for financing arrangements for enhanced access to financial services such as the IFAD supported IREGEFP project, that SURAGAWA will be able to leverage. This is expected to increase the confidence of investors and confidence in financial institutions of the viability of NTFPs as a viable business opportunity and send a clear signal to other private sector actors about the potential for investment in NTFPs such as livestock, income, employment and business growth and climate mitigation and resilience. On-going projects such as the GCF supported project in Sudan (Sudan Forest Investment Project) and the GCF co-financed project on gum arabic in Sudan, show an increase in smallholder investment in restoration once producers receive better prices for NTFP products.

## production and marketing

### Specific studies and analysis to inform the form and scale of investments

The project activities will consist of multiple components: a) promotion of restoration activities on degraded lands through the provision of appropriate equipment for afforestation and reforestation, b) establishment of an effective monitoring system.

**Cost Indicator 6. Decrease in costs of investments considering technology demonstration, dissemination or transfer and training**

### low

#### Rural communities will benefit from the adoption of technologies for large-scale restoration, as well as remote sensing and reporting systems.

MAGORI and other Afforestation sites systematically apply a participatory indicator framework pilot project, and evaluation methodology. The pilot project is designed to demonstrate how to integrate climate change adaptation activities (e.g., Carbon sequestration) into the value chain of afforestation activities and technologies through value chains, as well as financial products that support climate change adaptation and mitigation (reg. Carbon credits).

#### Multi countries

In the context of NTFP, project actors will have access to information on how in relation to the application of appropriate technologies for the improvement of efficient production and marketing of NTFP products.

**Under Outcome 2:**

The project will support market linkages with entrepreneurial groups and other stakeholders. Project financial and technical assistance include among other things, training and engagement activities for greater group cohesion and entrepreneurs' skills in product distribution.


### White NTFP

While NTFP is not included in all of the areas of intervention, project actors across various chapters have access to information on how to add suitable climate change adaptation options through market chain development.

#### Multi countries


**100% Many actors across the value chain do have access to equipment. However, it is inefficient or not fit for purpose, with some value chains having no access at all. The project will provide appropriate equipment and training as required. Please refer to Annex 2 for further details.**

## Outcome 3.1
### Enhanced institutional readiness and capacity of Agencies and of PA-GGW for monitoring, coordination and mobilization

**Core Indicator:** The extent to which the Agencies and PA-GGW are able to use the core set of methodologies (biophysical and socio-economic) referenced in the GGGF workplan.

* **Annual Performance Survey**
    * Review of knowledge products, 5 annual reports
    * Review of mobilization pledges from support events

**Banned Outcome Survey**

* **Review of resource mobilization mechanisms supported by programmes, with a focus on their own resources (in-kind and human)**

NAIGW have the mandate to collect information through directly visiting institutions. The core set of methodologies (biophysical and socio-economic) referenced in the GGGF workplan.

The GGGF are committed to strengthening national capacity in knowledge products activities and mobilization mechanisms, with a focus on the use of the core set of methodologies (biophysical and socio-economic). This will be achieved through resource mobilization mechanisms supported by programmes, with a focus on their own resources (in-kind and human).

A. Governments are committed and willing to provide national capacity building services.

A: Governments and partners are willing to provide technical assistance including indicators, methodologies, tools, and resources for the development of national capacity building services.

A: GGGF is committed and will continue to provide technical assistance including indicators, methodologies, tools, and resources for the development of national capacity building services. This includes national and regional level opportunities, including indicators, methodologies, tools, and resources for the development of national capacity building services.

National and regional level expertise can be leveraged with relevant staff with appropriate experience and skills.

|  |  |  | Increase in the frequency of collaboration mechanisms | Final target to be reported at regular intervals. |

### Assumptions
- A: Political stability, improved and maintained humanitarian access, and a conducive programme environment.
- B: There is relative calm and order within the region and no significant tensions in the area that could spill over into the project.
- C: The agro-economic approaches are being applied to Component 1 and there is consideration of the need for transhumant pastoralism. Plus increasing awareness on different livelihoods, common land use, and conflict resolution mechanisms.

## Co-benefit 2: Increased income distribution towards climate sources
|  | Share of income from climate-related sources | Baseline outcome assessment and impact evaluation | Mid-Term target to report an increase in climate sources |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
|  | Baseline to be reported at regular intervals. | Final target to be reported on an annual basis. |

### Assumptions
- A: Land use activities are economically viable locally.

129 Studies show that the AAD approach has a statistically significant impact on the mix of income sources of households engaging in permanent and seasonal livestock production, with increased diversification towards climate variability and climate-related livestock related production (through increased fodder production), and demonstrating an increase in the share of income from climate-related sources.

### Activities

#### Activity 3.3.1: Strengthen public sector climate sector understanding of and capacity to implement integrated landscape approaches for climate change adaptation and mitigation through land restoration

The activity will enhance the knowledge base, conduct a landscape analysis of existing markets and identify opportunities for carbon finance options, capacity building on regulatory and policy frameworks and the platforms with which to connect with public and private actors. This will continue attracting additional NGRW interventions.

#### Activity 3.3.2: Pilot the establishment of national carbon accounting framework for the integrated management of forestry in Nigeria, and identify additional resources for potential replication

The activity will enhance revenue generation from carbon credits through the pilot project. ### E.7 Monitoring, reporting and evaluation arrangements (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

#### 236. Reporting from FAD to GCF

Programme-level monitoring and evaluation will be undertaken in compliance with FAO well as GCF policies. FAO will ensure the existence of a well-designed, operational and effective impact monitoring and reporting system to analyse and quantify the causal and attributive linkages between project activities and outcomes. The multi-stakeholder monitoring and reporting system will allow for understanding the efficacy, targeting and verifying the assumptions made in the project design. This will be achieved through the use of an adaptive learning plan, in close coordination with IGRENFIN, so that elements emerging from the monitoring systems can be used to inform future projects. The GCF Secretariat will provide technical support on a regular basis. The M&E Specialist in the Programme Management Unit (PMU) will be responsible for transmitting project progress reports to the GCF Secretariat, as well as results of biannual outcome surveys and end-of-project final evaluations.

#### 239. SURAGAWA monitoring and reporting

In addition to support to GCF projects on knowledge management and innovation, the GCF invited the Food and Agriculture Organization to develop a monitoring and evaluation framework for the Suragawa project (see below). The framework will be developed in close coordination with IGRENFIN and other stakeholders across the GGW. The monitoring and evaluation framework will provide a multi-scale Monitoring and Evaluation System (MES) that will include progress on carbon sequestration indicators, as well as biannual outcome surveys (see below for further details). The MES will be based on the GCF M&E Framework, which is currently under development. The International M&E Specialist will provide capacity building to all country level M&E specialists and contribute to the development of a monitoring and evaluation framework that will ensure consistency in the reporting process across GGW countries, as well as the African Union. The M&E specialists in the Country Implementation Units (CIU) will be responsible for collecting data on project progress and outcomes at the project level, as well as output and outcome-level indicators to the PMU on a regular basis. The monitoring process intends to follow up on the implementation of the project during its execution phase and evaluation phases, and evaluate its outcomes and fulfillment of proposed targets. Furthermore, Steering Committees will be established in each GGW country to provide guidance for the project implementation by Ministries and stakeholders with a view to overseeing annual work planning and budgeting, as well as review of progress on project indicators on an annual basis. The indicators to be monitored are included in the log frame in section E.


---
**Page 129**
---

### Safeguards and Gender Action Plan reporting: The programme places particular importance on ensuring the inclusion of gender responsive components in all project components relevant to women and more vulnerable populations (including Internally Displaced Population) and will develop a comprehensive gender action plan for the project, including plans for gender, internally displaced people and the social and environmental framework to safeguard. They will also ensure a comprehensive and holistic monitoring system, besides the quantitative impact monitoring.

### SURAGAWA Biennial Outcome Reporting: Independent consultants or research institutions will be contracted to conduct an independent review of the project’s progress and identify attribution in relation to activities, outputs and outcomes as per the SURAGAWA MEL. At the end of each biennium, a report will be submitted that includes information on outcome-level and co-benefit indicators, identifying as much as possible contribution of project activities and outputs to these indicators. The review will be based upon the data and information in the SURAGAWA MEL System and will be geo-referenced. The Biennial Outcome Review will also include a qualitative assessment of the project’s impact on gender equality practices and knowledge across all components of the project (and including the scorecard methodology for the project at the component level and at individual level), as well as a qualitative NCDV assessment and beneficiary feedback survey.

# RISK ASSESSMENT AND MANAGEMENT

## Risk Factor and mitigations measures (max. 3 pages)

### Selected Risk Factor 1: Displacement & Insecurity; political instability

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Other | Medium | High |

242\. The rise of external movements within the region has been a significant cause of violence in the Sahel. Religious extremist groups and militias have gained momentum in the Sahel region causing the region to become an epicenter for conflict. The armed conflict in Africa's Central Sahel zone has forced more than 2.5 million people to flee their homes, with over half of these being women and children. The primary driver of this displacement is violence, occurring mainly in Nigeria, Mali and Burkina Faso. Alongsie terrorist invasions, the region has also been affected by climate change, which has led to increased competition for resources between tensions between groups. Violent conflict in the Sahel, compounded by the impacts of climate change, has resulted in violent displacement of people from their homes. The presence of foreign military forces, international and regional forces, international contingencies, and violent extremist organizations has also hindered humanitarian response. This situation has also contributed to political instability in some SURAGGWA countries.

243\. In selecting its areas of operation, SURAGGWA will avoid regions where there is violent conflict and displacement due to safety reasons and intervene in areas where the security situation allows the programme to be implemented safely. The selection process for the implementation of the programme will follow high-level UN guidance, including: (i) respecting UN-imposed sanctions; (ii) following UN instructions re interruption of the implementation of 'non-critical' activities.

### Selected Risk Factor 2: Conflict over meagre resources

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and Operational | Medium | Medium |

244\. Conflict in the Sahel is primarily rooted in political and governance-related weaknesses. However, environmental fragility and climate change exacerbate these conflicts. There is overdependence on climate-sensitive agriculture-based livelihoods which poses additional risks with growth in population as well. The growth in population has led to increased competition for resources such as water, land, and pasture. This has exacerbated the problems of resource degradation. Combined with climate uncertainty, this results in low yields and high crop losses despite the use of improved agricultural practices. These conflicts are further exacerbated by overpopulation over the dwindling natural resource base and causes conflicts among the farming and herding communities who rely on livestock. These conflicts can become severe during periods of droughts as communities by and fight for


---
**Page 131**
---

# Mitigation

## SURAGIWIA programme is designed to clarify the arrangements for using the common lands through consultations with local communities.

### Selected Risk Factor 3: Programme Governance and Management Arrangements

#### Category | Probability | Impact
---|---|---
Governance | Medium | Medium

Experience has shown that in a programme which is being implemented in a wide range of countries, coordination can pose a challenge between the Green World (GW) institutions, the programme's Country Implementation Unit (CIU), and the FAO. This could also be caused by inexperience or poor procurement procedures.

### Mitigation Measure(s)

The programme will adopt a phased approach in which there will be a gradual increase of activities in the various countries. Those countries which require more time to prepare their institutional arrangements will be provided with the opportunity for an early start. The programme's Country Implementation Unit (CIU) will be strengthened by providing it with proper training in the contracting and procurement arrangements.

## Selected Risk Factor 4: Sustainability

### Category | Probability | Impact
---|---|---
User-communities conflicts are one of the main risks to the success of the programme, so conflict assessment, prevention and management is a key element. The programme will adopt a participatory approach in the design and implementation of the project, and will use the FAO's Environmental and Social Management Framework, especially its Appendix E: Conflict assessment, prevention and management.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 126

## Governance Medium Description Medium

### Selected Risk Factor 5: Elite Capture

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Medium | Medium |

**Discussion**

Some of the land sites that will be implemented on are used by farmers, agro pastoral, and nomadic populations – all of whom engage in traditional land-use arrangements that provide mutual food and livelihood security. These arrangements include grazing for livestock, particularly during the rainy season, and are sources of wild plants and wood gathering. The introduction of intercropping can lead to a reduction in the availability of these resources due to exclusion. The use of area enclosures – a land management practice that seeks to restore degraded land by excluding livestock from certain areas – is also likely to reduce access for some groups, particularly women who are often excluded and face increased vulnerability, and in the absence of good land governance, possibly causing harm. Furthermore, increasing the value of these resources through intercropping can lead to a reduction in their availability due to exclusion or competition with other users.

Women can be particularly impacted in their capacity as one of the primary users of common lands for gathering non-timber forest products and wood fuel. The introduction of intercropping can also reduce access to these effectively restored, but where parcels were also sold outside of the community, in areas that lacked good land governance arrangements.


## Mitigation

### Selected Risk Factor 6: Survival Rates of Vegetation

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Medium | Medium |

**Mitigation Measures**

SURAGAWA has been designed with an understanding of customary laws, traditional use rights, coping strategies of the most vulnerable and how these risks can be impacted by changes in the rehabilitation of the common lands. The project will ensure that all arrangements are made to protect the livelihoods of the most vulnerable segments of the local communities, especially women. Thus emphasis will be placed on ensuring that clear, accessible, and transparent land-use arrangements are put into place for the benefit of these groups; these are properly communicated, accepted and respected by all concerned. Arrangements will be made in accordance with customary laws and techniques to put in place an appropriate monitoring and supervision mechanism and grievance redress mechanisms.


---
**Page 133**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 127

## Description

The key goal of increasing tree cover in and on demand regions has shown very varied results in some projects. In regions with medium to low desertification risk, local governments have reported larger areas as having been afforested than were actually planted. This is often due to the lack of monitoring systems for overambitious tree-planting projects in and semi-arid regions where the survival rates of tree seedlings is often as low as 10%. This is especially true in areas receiving less than 500 mm annual rainfall.

### Selected Risk Factor #7: Limited Access of Women

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Prohibited practices | Medium | Medium |

**Description**

254. Typically, women's access to the common lands and farm land is restricted due to social and patriarchal norms. There is a danger that the rights of women can be further compromised when degraded lands are rehabilitated. This is because women have less access to resources than men in many countries. In addition, those individuals who have access to land, women can be especially disadvantaged in the Sahel and are forced to forage for firewood and water on their own. Women are also more likely to be displaced by climate change in Sub-Saharan countries to look for work or they belong to households which are the head of the household.

### Selected Risk Factor #8: Limited Participation of Financial Institutions

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and Operational | Medium | Medium |

**Description**

256. The main risks for the access to financial institutions have stemmed from two factors. First, the limited participation of financial institutions could hinder programme implementation given their role as key stakeholders. Second, in countries where the government does not have a strong financial institution, particularly if IGREEM/IFD does not start up.

257. IGREEM will mitigate these risks through several channels. First, the programme's access to finance activities are designed in consultation with key financial institutions in the countries, in response to their existing situation and needs. Second, the programme has established a dedicated unit for the engagement of financial institutions. Second, the programme has consulted with the IGREEM/IFD executive agency, IFAD, on

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 129

## Selected Risk Factor #9: Anti-Money Laundering and Countering Financial Terrorism Risk

### Category: Governance
#### Probability: Low
#### Impact: Low

**Description:** Risks of money laundering and countering the financing of terrorism. The inherent risk is assessed as high, but the following mitigation measures would lower the risk to low.

## Mitigation

FAO includes in the project agreement signed between FAO and the eight Governments clauses related to AML/CFT, as follows:

a) The Government shall comply, and shall require all persons and entities engaged in its activities under the Programme to comply with, all internal anti-money laundering, counter-terrorism financing laws, rules, and regulations.

b) The Government undertakes that it will not engage in any prohibited practices, nor will it allow any persons or entities under the Project that they shall not engage in any prohibited practices. The Government undertakes and confirm that if a person or entity is found to be engaged in such prohibited practices, the Government will take appropriate action.

c) Consistent with numerous United Nations Security Council resolutions adopted under Chapter VII of the UN Charter, the Government and FAO are firmly committed to the international fight against terrorism and, in particular, against the financing thereof. The Government undertakes that it shall not directly or indirectly provide support to individuals or entities: i) associated with terrorism, as included in the list maintained by the United Nations Security Council pursuant to Chapter VII of the UN Charter; ii) who are listed on the list maintained by the European Union under Regulation (EC) No. 2650/2012; iii) that are subject of sanctions or other enforcement measures promulgated by the United Nations Security Council, the European Union, or any other international organization to which the Government is a party in the implementation activities under the Programme.

d) The Government will ensure close monitoring and supervision through its offices in the regional office and HQ in order to ensure that the activities are implemented in full compliance with the signed project agreement.

FAO will establish appropriate fiduciary management and control measures to ensure that materials or technology procured under this programme are used only for the purposes intended and are not diverted or misused for unauthorized purposes. FAO will also implement appropriate due diligence procedures, including background checks mechanisms, corporate policies on head and other contract practices, FAO Vendor Sanctions Policy (Admin circular 2014/03), and the United Nations Security Council Resolution 1674 (2006) on sanctions against terrorist organizations. For more information please visit: <https://www.fao.org/es/mipenmipnog/investigations/> including those of the Office of the Inspector General (OIG) will be to be consulted.

# GCF POLICIES AND STANDARDS

## G.1 Environmental and Social Risk Assessment (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)

### Approach of the Programme to a Social Risk Category

258. The proposed environmental and social (ES) risk assignment for the programme is “Category B” (Moderate Risk) based on the screening of ES risks and impacts associated with the: (i) type, nature, and scale of proposed interventions; (ii) potential adverse effects on human health and safety, including occupational health and safety, and environmental settings (covering inherent risks) of the potential intervention sites. The risk assignment considers the programme’s ability to manage identified risks through mitigation measures and/or safeguards of a given risk’s impact on achievement of the intended programme results. It considers the effects of ES risk mitigation measures already in place for programme areas, but does not assume any future additional mitigation measures outside of those proposed in SUMP or ES risk management.

### Summary of the Outcomes by ES Instruments

259. Based on the screening, the design-team proposed: (i) an Environmental and Social Management Framework (ESMF); (ii) a Gender Action Plan (GAP); and (iii) a Stakeholder Engagement Plan (SEP), including a Grievance Redress Mechanism). The instruments describe and respond to the potential ES risks and impacts associated with the programme. They are developed during field visits, key informant interviews, and focus group discussions with programme stakeholders. Quantitative and qualitative data collected through primary and secondary sources were used to assist the analysis.

#### Social risks, impacts, and mitigation measures

260. Social impacts of the programme are largely positive. The programme activities aim to improve the livelihoods and resilience of communities who rely on common and private lands in the Sahel region, with a focus on increasing cereal production through improved land management practices. The programme also aims to increase community engagement under Component 1 is expected to increase collaboration and potentially improve conflict resolution mechanisms. Under Component 2, the programme will provide access to credit for women’s groups, incomes, and access to financial credit. Institutional capacities built under Component 3 will support improved coordination, collaboration, and management.

#### Key social risks perceived as moderate in social conflict as the value of restored lands increases; (ii) management and/or conditional use rights over the land tenure arrangements; and (iii) engaging with vulnerable populations, including traditionally underserved Sub-Saharan African Communities. To mitigate these concerns, the programme has the principles of Free Prior Informed Consent (FPIC), Community Land Rights, and the United Nations Declaration on the Responsible Governance of Tenure of Land, Fisheries and Forests in the Context of National Food Security into the implementation process. The programme also includes a Gender Action Plan (GAP) to ensure gender equality and mitigate (when possible as is) the risk of social conflict. The ESMF also details the programme’s approach to engaging with vulnerable populations, including traditionally underserved Sub-Saharan African Communities.

#### Environmental risks, impacts, and mitigation measures

262. The programme is expected to have large positive environmental impacts, including improved climate resilience through increased soil fertility, reduced erosion and groundwater recharge, and natural resources management. Potential negative environmental impacts are expected to be minor, limited in timescales, and reversible with appropriate mitigation measures. These include: (i) potential increase of greenhouse gas emissions from activities; (ii) potential indirect increase of pesticide use due to increased production; (iii) increased water consumption for irrigation; and (iv) the risk that some land may become degraded as a result of overuse. These risks are managed through overall programme design (e.g., training under Components 1 and 2 which will ensure understanding of restoration principles and agroecology-integrated management systems). Limitations to

The types of activities held in existing parks (e.g., restoration activities only), and (ii) Environmental and Social Management Plans (ESMPs) for programme areas. The ESMP will also take into account the territorial and environmental knowledge of the affected ethnic minorities/vulnerable populations, inherent environmental risks include those related to climate change, desertification, land degradation, drought, and other adverse effects across the Sahel. The ESMP provides the screening tools to be used at programme sites to ensure that these risks are sufficiently addressed. If a critical location for restoration activities is located within or close to the boundary of the negative list), or if a designated critical location for restoration activities will involve identification and removal of any protected area, the project will not proceed without obtaining any clearance from the programme office. Considerations of any potential adverse impacts on biodiversity or other environmental impacts are assessed as being positive.

## Engagement and Information Disclosure during Implementation:

263. Stakeholder engagement will depend on the target communities and is designed, as per the SEF, to be inclusive and participatory in nature. The ESMP will also take into account the territorial and environmental knowledge of the affected ethnic minorities/vulnerable populations, inherent environmental risks include those related to climate change, desertification, land degradation, drought, and other adverse effects across the Sahel. The ESMP provides the screening tools to be used at programme sites to ensure that these risks are sufficiently addressed. If a critical location for restoration activities is located within or close to the boundary of the negative list), or if a designated critical location for restoration activities will involve identification and removal of any protected area, the project will not proceed without obtaining any clearance from the programme office. Considerations of any potential adverse impacts on biodiversity or other environmental impacts are assessed as being positive.

### Programme-level Grievance Redress Mechanism (GRIM)

264. The programme has designed a GRIM to provide complainants with redress procedures that are accessible, equitable and transparent. The GRIM will be implemented in accordance with the FAO’s Operational Partners Implementation Modality (OPIM). As such, with the implementation of the OPIM, the FAO will ensure that all relevant safeguards are in place.

### Capacity of the Executing Entity to implement the ESMP and ESMP arrangements for compliance monitoring

265. The Executing Entity in each country would either be: (i) FAO; or (ii) a national entity which has passed through the assessment and evaluation of FAO’s Operational Partners Implementation Modality (OPIM). As such, with the implementation of the OPIM, the FAO will ensure that all relevant safeguards are in place.

### Gender Assessment & Action Plan (Gap)

266. As part of the formulation process, the Gender Assessment & Action Plan (GAP) were prepared for SURAGAWA (see Annex 8). Based on: (i) desk review of relevant literature on gender, forestry and climate change; (ii) interviews with key stakeholders including government officials, civil society organizations and NGOs; and (iii) primary data collected through field-level consultations. Field-level consultations supporting the GAP were conducted at the national level by representatives from the Ministry of Environment and Forestry and civil society organizations; representatives from relevant technical departments of counterpart ministries, the GGOA Agency, and other stakeholders in the forestry sector; and representatives from women’s groups. The findings from these consultations held at the country level (see the SEF - Annex 7 for a full overview of consultations), coupled with the desk review literature, informed the development of the GAP.

Based on these findings, the GAP provides specific actions to assist women in building their resilience to climate change and to ensure that they are not disproportionately affected by climate change. The GAP also outlines how the SURAGAWA programme will ensure equitable participation and benefits from the programme investment accrue for women. Key highlights are explained below.


---
**Page 137**
---

267. Within the Sahel, women make up anywhere between 40 to 80 percent of the agricultural labour and pay, but a critical mass for enhancing food security and nutrition. They are less likely to own land throughout Africa, but this gender discrepancy is particularly pronounced in Sahelian countries, where only 1% of our own agricultural land is owned by women. The African Development Bank’s Gender Equity Index indicates that the gender gap is even higher among women. The African Development Bank’s Gender Equity Index indicates that the gender gap is even higher among women. The African Development Bank’s Gender Equity Index indicates that the gender gap is even higher among women.

Social and economic factors, such as poverty, lack of education, and limited access to resources, often mean that women are less likely than men to manage their own income or assets. Women in rural areas are more likely to be responsible for collecting water and fuel, preparing food, and feeding and caring for small humans, etc., Climate variability and the increasing frequency of extreme weather events have a disproportionate impact on women’s livelihoods because they rely heavily on natural resources when there are few alternative livelihood options in rural areas.
