# Secretariat’s assessment of FP268

## Proposal name: Scaling-Up Resilience in Africa’s Great Green Wall (SIURAGWA)

### Accredited entity:
Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations (FAO)

### Country (ies):
Burkina Faso, Chad, Djibouti, Mali, Mauritania, Niger, Nigeria, Senegal

### Project/programme size: Medium

## I. Overall assessment of the Secretariat

1. The funding proposal is presented to the Board for consideration with the following remarks:

| Strengths | Points of caution |
|-----------|-------------------|
| The programme directly supports the African Union-led Great Green Wall (GGW) initiative, which aims at enhancing climate resilience. Specifically, it contributes to the GGW goals of restoring 100 million hectares of degraded land and creating 50 million new jobs while sequestering 250 million tonnes of CO<sub>2</sub> equivalent by 2030 through large-scale land restoration efforts. | The programme is to be implemented over a decade in eight countries, including several fragile states with high levels of conflict, which might impact the timely delivery of the investments. To address these concerns, the proposal includes contingency measures and adaptive management measures, including a contingency budget. |

2. The Board may wish to consider approving this funding proposal in accordance with the term sheet agreed between the Secretariat and the accredited entity (AE) and, if considered appropriate, subject to the conditions set out in annex II of document GC/JR/2/02.

## II. Summary of the Secretariat’s assessment

### 2.1 Programme background


---
**Page 159**
---

The Sahelian nations within the GOW region encounter significant climate-related vulnerabilities and developmental obstacles. A substantial segment of their populations remains deprived of essential healthcare services and educational opportunities. Their economies are predominantly agrarian, with a large proportion of the population engaged in subsistence agriculture dependent on rainfed agriculture. The region has been particularly affected by climate change, which has caused prolonged droughts, torrential rainfall and ecosystem degradation that has severely impacted food security.

In response, the Scaling Up Resilience in Africa's Great Green Wall (SURAGWAA) programme aims to enhance resilience through landscape rehabilitation and sustainable land management practices, including restricting native species, promoting climate-resilient NTFP and fodder value chain production, and improving access to markets for smallholder farmers. Through community engagement, particularly the empowerment of women, SURAGWAA will enhance financial linkages and address existing bottlenecks to support innovative value chain financing models that can increase income generation opportunities and improve livelihoods.

The proposed funding for the programme is USD 221,990,312, utilizing grant instruments. The GCF contribution to the programme is a USD 150 million grant. The programme will be implemented in seven countries: Burkina Faso, Chad, Djibouti, Mali, Mauritania, Niger and Senegal.

The programme targets approximately 1.88 billion direct beneficiaries and 3.86 billion indirect beneficiaries. Together, these beneficiaries comprise 13 per cent of the total population of the target countries. The estimated carbon sequestration over the programme’s lifetime is 20 years is 65 million tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent.

GCF categorises the programme as category B (medium) for its environmental and social safeguards (SS).

### 2.2 Component-by-component analysis

The programme is structured into three components:

1. **Component A: Landscape rehabilitation, climate resilience and mitigation** - for local land communities (total USD 143,890,465; GCF cost USD 82,098,471 in grants)

This component will initiate the process of land rehabilitation by engaging with rural communities and working closely with them to identify specific sites, prepare and carry out interventions, maintain and manage the restored sites and assess the impacts, starting with initial baseline assessments.

Outputs under this component include that community groups are organized and trained for land restoration activities and monitoring; native seed supply systems have been strengthened through partnerships between local organizations and national institutions; degraded climate-tolerant highly degraded lands are restored through arrangements for rainwater harvesting and soil conservation; degraded lands are rehabilitated using planting in plantations and direct seeding; targeted moderately degraded lands are planted with a range of species to restore and enrich the landscapes of agroforestry, agroecology and agriculture.


---
**Page 160**
---

silver carbon sequestration, biomass and protected forest ecosystems, and rural community and local authority capacity is strengthened to verify the results of the restoration and inform the management of restored areas.

### Component 2: Supporting the development of climate-resilient, low-emission NTFP value chains benefiting smallholder communities' livelihoods (total cost: USD 369,107; GCF cost: USD 32,327,507 in grants)

#### Sub-component 12: This component aims to improve the resilience and livelihoods of the local agro-silvo-pastoral communities through NTFP collectors, processors, and sellers in the GFW zones in the selected Sahel countries.

Outputs under this component include that the technical and organizational capacities of producers and other actors involved in the agro-silvo-pastoral systems are strengthened in priority NTFP value chains; there is increased access to markets for smallholder NTFP actors (including through the GFW market facilitation mechanism); financial products are designed for smaller NTFP value chain actors (producer organizations, cooperatives and micro-enterprises) that can be used by them; and enhanced stakeholder capacity for using these financial products in their NTFP value chain activities.

#### Sub-component 13: Outputs under this component include that the technical and organizational capacities of the institutions officially created and mandated to spearhead the GFW initiative at the national and regional level, including the relevant agencies in the eight participating countries and the Pan-African Agency for the Great Green Wall.

Outputs under this component include that the GFW land restoration monitoring system at the national and sub-national levels is strengthened; the capacity of the GFW institution planning and coordination capacities are strengthened; national and regional climate change institutions/capacities/frameworks are strengthened to integrate land restoration investments in a climate-resilient manner; and institutional, technical, financial, knowledge management and communication capacities are leveraged for increasing support to climate-resilient land restoration.

### Monitoring and evaluation (total cost: USD 768,750; GCF cost: USD 394,250)

Project management (total cost: USD 1,028,635; GCF cost: USD 570,000)

The project budget management will finance project management expenses in various fields, to be financed by the GCF.

A contingency budget is provided to address unforeseen challenges, such as unexpected cost increases owing to higher inflation or security costs.

### III. Assessment of performance against investment criteria

#### Sub-component 18: The programme consistently scores medium to high against the GCF investment criteria. A detailed assessment of the programme's fit with the GCF investment criteria is provided below.

#### 3.1 Impact potential Scale: High


---
**Page 161**
---

3.2 Paradigm shift potential

The programme represents a paradigm shift in the agriculture sector by developing sustainable value chains for NTFPs and creating innovative financing models to attract private sector investment. This will enable smallholder farmers and pastoralist households by investing in climate-resilient land management, building smallholder capacity through training and linking them with markets for NTFPs and fostering more equitable partnerships with private sector buyers and financial institutions.

3.3 Sustainable development potential

The programme contributes towards the following Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs): SDG 1 (no poverty), SDG 2 (zero hunger), SDG 8 (decent work and economic growth), SDG 13 (climate action), SDG 15 (life on land) and SDG 17 (partnership for the goals). It is expected that the programme will realise environmental, social, economic, and gender co-benefits.

3.4 Needs of the recipient

The Notre Dame Global Adaptation Index rankings for the SRTGWANA countries show a high degree of vulnerability to lack of economic governance, and social readiness for climate investment. Seven out of the eight target countries in the programme are least developed countries with a low self-financing capacity.

3.5 Country ownership

No objection letters have been issued by the Governments of all target countries.

In response to severe droughts, the African Union launched the GGW initiative in 2007 with support from the Green Climate Fund (GCF). The GGI was established as a platform for countries to nominate national designated authorities and developed tailored mitigation and adaptation strategies. The GGW programme is an evolution of this initiative. Each country’s strategy was developed through extensive regional and national consultations, during which various identified challenges were addressed and potential local contributions. Countries also established partnerships, institutional arrangements and focal points for implementation, and are actively seeking additional climate finance.

3.6 Efficiency and effectiveness

The programme will provide grants to build the technical, organisational and commercial capacities of farmers and pastoralists. This will enable them to adopt more resilient farming practices and invest in restoring additional land, thereby expanding the programme’s


---
**Page 162**
---

### Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies

#### Environmental and social safeguards

##### 4.1 Environmental and social safeguards

29 The Environmental and Social Management Framework (ESMF) shared by the AE including a Stakeholder Engagement Plan (SEP) and Grievance Redress Mechanism (GRM) aligns with the GCF’s ESMF. The ESMF is in development, in line with the FAO Framework for Environmental and Social Management and GCF’s revised ESP. The programme will be subject to an environmental and social risk screening and a financial risk screening. The AE has further developed and shared a conflict assessment that will be guided by FAO/UNDP.

30 The risk screening triggered the following standards relevant to the programme namely:

- ESS4 - Animal, Livestock, and Aquatic Genetic Resources for Food and Agriculture: The programme is envisaged to help rehabilitate and regenerate the land and soil in highly degraded areas. This will be achieved through the promotion of agroforestry and mixed agroforestry in the area. While it will not be implemented inside protected areas, it will be in proximity to them. The AE has developed a conflict assessment that includes examples with examples being the restoration of sand dunes to prevent sitation of protected Ramsar wetland sites.

- ESS5 - Pest and Pesticide Management: During programme implementation, no procurement or supply of chemical pesticides is envisaged; however, there is risk that increased production of crops will lead to an increase in pest pressure. The AE has developed a conflict assessment that includes the principles of agro-ecology and Integrated Pest Management (IPM) are included in the ESMF shared by the AE. IPM is a strategy for managing pests on ecological pest management. In some communities, there may be use of the bio-pesticide Nete.

32 Additionally, the programme may provide some direct inputs in the form of seeds to farmers, in cases where local community seed production is insufficient. The provision will


---
**Page 163**
---

### Indigenous Peoples Policy and ES7: Indigenous Peoples

The ethnic minorities planning framework was developed to ensure that the design of the programme takes into account the engagement of all groups who would fall under the scope of the Indigenous Peoples Policy (i.e., not limited to the groups enumerated in the ethnic minorities planning framework) during implementation. The SEF noted that, while the indigenous peoples and relevant ethnic minorities were consulted during the programme's design, the ESF monitoring and evaluation framework did not include specific indicators for measuring the engagement of ethnic minorities within Senegal. The SEF commits to paying special attention to Indigenous Peoples when designing the monitoring and evaluation framework and in addressing complaints and grievances. In line with its roles and functions, the Indigenous Peoples Advisory Group is available to provide advice to the AE and executing entities.


---
**Page 164**
---

# 4.2 Gender policy

The AE has provided a gender assessment and action plan and therefore complies with the requirements of the GCF standard gender policy. The SIRAGENI Gender Assessment highlights the challenges women and marginalized groups face in the Sahel, especially in agriculture, land restoration, and climate adaptation. Women are burdened by socio-cultural norms and patriarchal systems restricting their access to resources such as credit, extension services, and decision-making spaces. They make up 60 to 80% of the agricultural workforce yet remain underrepresented in leadership positions. Early marriage, time poverty, and limited literacy exacerbate these challenges. Women are particularly vulnerable due to their reliance on rain-fed agriculture and increased exposure to risk from resource scarcity and climate change. The GCF prioritizes gender equality by increasing women's participation in adaptation efforts and land restoration initiatives, where their roles and perspectives are often ignored or underutilized.

The programme will generate a Gender Action Plan (GAP) outlining a suite of gendered activities and the AE has indicated that country-level GAPs will be prepared by a social safeguard specialist. The GAP will focus on addressing women's access to land and resources, reducing cultural inequalities and empower women to lead and benefit from land restoration and land restoration efforts. Key areas include promoting women's participation in agricultural activities such as planting, enhancing access to land and water resources, and supporting gender-sensitive agricultural value chains. The GAP prioritizes capacity-building for women's organizations and institutions, ensuring their active involvement in the programme through a gender-sensitive monitoring framework. It also focuses on increasing women's access to training in sustainable land management practices.

The AE is encouraged to strengthen the programme by integrating gender mainstreaming into all aspects of its operations. The GCF encourages the AE to further strengthen the programme by ensuring gender expertise is represented in all decision-making bodies of the programme.


---
**Page 165**
---

## Compliance risk (medium risk)

- The programme’s proposed activities, with their focus on land restoration, developing low emission NTFP and fodder value chains, and Great Green Wall regional and national institutions, are not subject to significant ML/TF risks.
- The AE has assessed inherent ML/TF as high due to jurisdictional and programme-related risks but residual risk is low due to mitigation measures including anti-money laundering/heroin financing (ML/HF) and counter-narcotics agreements with the eight implementation country governments, as well as close monitoring and supervision by the AE’s regional office.
- Additional mitigants to decrease the inherent ML/TF risk include the AE's existing Indicatory management system for monitoring and reporting on suspicious transactions. These include annual review and monitoring of a fraud prevention plan by the AE’s regional office, as well as a requirement that all programme activities are subject to country-level anti-money laundering assessments of the relevant ministries of environment of the eight target countries which will serve as a tool and found that they have adequate systems in place for evidence of elevated ML/TF risks.
- Considering the size and scope of this multi-jurisdictional programme, including a large number of implementing beneficiaries and relatively high levels of procurement, the compliance risk is determined to be medium.

### GCF portfolio concentration risk

- In the event of approval, the impact of this proposal on the GCF concentration risk remains within the monitoring thresholds of the Risk Appetite Statement in terms of results areas, single proposal or AE concentration.


---
**Page 166**
---

### 4.4 Fiduciary

The fiduciary framework of the SURAGWA programme is structured to ensure strong financial oversight, effective coordination and accountability across all levels of implementation. FAD will act as a single point of contact for all GCF funds allocated on an aggregated basis, in order to avoid any conflict of interest. FAD will assume full fiduciary responsibility for GCF-funded activities, including the management of resources, disbursement, accounting, programme supervision, monitoring, reporting and evaluation, in accordance with its internal control framework and GCF requirements.

The programme will be implemented through a multi-tiered governance and delivery structure. A regional programme management unit, based in the FAD Subregional Office for Africa, will provide overall coordination of the programme, ensuring alignment, integration and harmonization across countries. Country-level implementation will be carried out by country implementation teams (CITs), which will consist of national staff qualified in finance, procurement, safeguards and monitoring. Implementation will be supported by national governments, FAD and selected partners through agreed modalities that reflect existing institutional arrangements.

Strong governance and oversight will be provided by a regional programme steering committee chaired by the GCF Trustee, with representatives from the eight participating countries in each of the eight participating countries. These committees will ensure alignment with GGW priorities, facilitate multi-stakeholder engagement and support resource mobilization. The Pan-African Forest Landscape Restoration Programme (PAFLR) Secretariat will serve as the programme secretariat and will contribute to the coordination of co-financing and reporting.

### 4.5 Results monitoring and reporting

The funding proposal articulates a well-defined causal pathway grounded in a theory of change that emphasizes the role of large-scale land restoration, strengthening NTFP value chains and improving governance for landscape management as key drivers of enhanced resilience across eight Sahelian countries. This pathway is clearly mapped through the logical framework in the funding proposal and aligns with the GCF Integrated Results Management Framework (IRMF) for 2018-2023, which covers a five-year period aligned to the 10-year implementation period.

The project will contribute to three GCF results areas, namely forestry and land use for mitigation, and the most vulnerable people and communities and ecosystems and ecosystem services for adaptation. Accordingly, it commits to reporting against key GCF and supplementary indicators:

- **Gore indicator 1:** GHG emissions reduced, avoided or removed/sequestered (target: 65 million tonnes of Carbon dioxide equivalent).

The Accreditation Master Agreement was signed with the Accredited Entity on 8 June 2018 (the "AMX"), and became effective as of 3 January 2019 but was extended until 31 March 2024, through q) until the earlier of three years from the date of lapse of the five-year accreditation term or the date on which the GCF Board has approved the re-accreditation of the Accredited Entity pursuant to Decision R.37/18, and negotiation of a new agreement for the continuation of the arrangement.

The Accredited Entity has provided a certificate confirming that it has obtained all internal approvals and it has the capacity and authority to implement the programme.

### Annex 2

The GCF Board has approved the bilateral agreement between the GCF and one of those countries, namely Burkina Faso. In the other seven countries, namely Chad, Djibouti, Mali, Mauritania, Niger, Nigeria and Somalia, GCF is not provided with privileges and immunities. This means that


---
**Page 168**

among other things, GCF is not protected against litigation or expiration in these countries, which needs to be further assessed. Moreover, the ability of the GCF to undertake redress activities and/or investigations in such countries may be hindered owing to the absence of privileges and immunities for the GCF and its personnel.

Therefore, it is recommended that the Board considers whether disbursements of GCF proceeds should only be made after GCF has obtained satisfactory protection against litigation and expiration in these countries or that GCF has provided with appropriate privileges and immunities for the GCF and its personnel.

To address the matters raised in this section, it is recommended that any approval by the Board must include:

(a) Signature of the funded activity agreement in a form and substance satisfactory to the GCF Secretariat within 180 days from the date of Board approval; and

(b) Completion of the legal due diligence to the satisfaction of the GCF Secretariat.

# Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment of FP268

## Proposal name: Scaling-Up Resilience in Africa’s Great Green Wall (SURAGGWA)

### Accredited entity: Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations (FAO)

#### Country/ies: Burkina Faso, Chad, Djibouti, Mali, Mauritania, Niger, Nigeria, Senegal

#### Project/programme size: Medium

## Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel1

### 1. The Great Green Wall for the Sahara and the Sabei project was adopted by the African Union in 2007. Initially, it aimed at combating desertification and holding back desert expansion by planting trees across the Sahel. However, as climate change has brought more rain to some areas that now show that desert boundaries change based on rainfall, the aim evolved into restoring vegetation, harnessing water resources, and improving livelihoods in a mosaic of forested landscapes across the Sabei, in a mosaic of forested land, interspersed with farmland and grasslands.

### 2. The Sahelian countries are all highly vulnerable to climate change, and their adaptive capacity is limited by low incomes. The proposed programme for "Scaling-up Resilience in Africa's Great Green Wall" (SURAGGWA) aims at restoring degraded lands across the transformed Sahelian landscapes by restoring 100 million hectares of degraded land and creating 10 million jobs. SURAGGWA will contribute to climate change adaptation, enhance ecosystem-based resilience and contribute to climate change mitigation through carbon sequestration in restored lands across eight participating countries (Burkina Faso, Chad, Djibouti, Mali, Mauritania, Niger, Nigeria and Senegal).

### 3. The cross-cutting SURAGGWA programme seeks to: (i) scale up restoration practices with well-established technologies that are cost-effective, sustainable, and sequester more carbon; (ii) develop value chains for climate-resilient and low-emission non-forest timber products that can be marketed in the international market by local communities; and (iii) strengthen national and regional Great Green Wall institutions to ensure the sustainability of interventions.

### 4. With a total cost of USD 223 million, the funding proposal seeks to use USD 150 million grant from GCF. The co-funding is composed of cash in and kind contributions from the participating countries (Burkina Faso, Chad, Djibouti, Mali, Mauritania, Niger, Nigeria and Senegal) and the Food and Agriculture Organization (FAO). The project is to be implemented in a 10-year period and has a total lifespan of 20 years.

### 5. The programme will be executed by FAO and the governments of eight countries as executing partners. Funding for the project will be provided by GCF and the Governments of Chad, Djibouti, Mauritania and Senegal while active or co-led by the governments and FAO will be directly executed by cash-on-delivery.


---
**Page 170**
---

1.1 Impact potential

Scale: Medium to high

6. This cross-cutting project contributes to both mitigation and adaptation results.

- Greenhouse gas (GHG) sequestration and reduced emissions are accomplished through land-use change activities that restore degraded lands and pastoral systems. This involves a small reduction in emissions through a shift from fuelwood to solar for agriculture, livestock, and cooking.
- The most significant is the highly ambitious target for carbon removal through sequestration on restored lands. The programme aims to bring about (a) a shift over 1,465,813 hectares of moderately degraded savanna land in pastoral areas from low-carbon use systems such as extensive agroforestry with perennial shrubs and trees, increasing both the level of carbon storage in the soil and vegetation, and (b) a shift to highly productive land-use systems on 204,751 hectares of highly degraded savanna land into a productive state with local grass, shrub and tree species. The programme aims for an increase in above-ground biomass by 38% over five years.
- These two components taken together aim to sequester over 65 million tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent (C02 eq) over the project period. This is a significant contribution towards meeting the global climate change target, and corresponds to an anticipated sequestration of 2.56 t C02 eq per hectare and per year.

7. Beyond mitigation of global climate change, the programme is expected to benefit over 9.5 million people in terms of improved livelihoods and food security (see Box 1). Direct beneficiaries are those people who actively participate in land restoration activities (Complementary Activities 3a and 3b) or who directly benefit from their implementation (Box 2), while indirect beneficiaries reflect the spillover beneficial effects. The assumptions and calculations are not entirely clear to the ITAP but the multipliers and adoption rates are taken to be entirely realistic.

The targeted beneficiaries are agropastoral communities, namely communities engaged in both agriculture and pastoralism. These communities are found on moderately degraded farmlands, despite increasing temperatures and changing rainfall patterns. This will be achieved with the application of agroecological approaches that reduce soil erosion and soil fertility loss through improved land management practices, increased crop production, as well as through better adapted crop varieties, and improved livestock productivity. The resilience of these communities to climate change is also enhanced through the selection of more resilient plant species, creating buffers to wind and soil erosion.

10. SURAGOWA will also introduce new livelihood options like NTFP and intend increased employment opportunities for women who are the majority participants in the collection and sale of these agroforestry products. This is expected as a result of increased income which will lead to an increased access to food for consumption.

11. The ITAP has expressed concerns regarding the potential gendered opportunity in benefiting from the project activities, particularly for women in semi-nomadic pastoralist communities. The AE assures that semi-nomadic pastoralist communities can also be beneficiaries and that women are not excluded as they are often the primary decision-makers among mixed herders and farmers. According to the written question and answer exchange, semi-nomadic pastoralist women will benefit from the project through increased access to water systems supported for propagation of fodder species as feed to benefit their livestock in semi-pastoral communities. The AE also assures that the Women’s Empowerment Committees, where relevant, will and will benefit from restoration of livestock migration corridors.

12. The ITAP remains concerned about the long-term sociocultural effects of restoring land and land-use systems on pastoralist communities. The AE has assured that the project team follows up on this issue. It is suggested the AE should facilitate and contribute to research exercises in the region, related to land control and potential land-use conflicts between

# Green Climate Fund

## Project Overview

The project will work with smallholder rural communities and their organizations, as well as the institutions involved in the monitoring and support of the broader Great Green Wall initiative. While the project is not a direct land restoration project, it endeavors to make a substantive contribution towards carbon sequestration and climate change mitigation.

### 1.2 Paradigm shift potential

With the shift in focus of the Great Green Wall initiative from tree planting to more comprehensive landscape management including improved production systems of smallholder farmers and pastoralist communities to increase food security, create jobs and boost the local economy through sustainable land management, countries are now tackling systemic challenges in an integrated manner across a much larger area than previously possible within the region. The SABURAGWA program subscribes to the more recently established and holistic development approach.

### 15. Funding proposal highlights capacity-building among local communities

The funding proposal highlights capacity-building among local communities, to gain improved understanding of how to implement land rehabilitation investments in highly degraded landscapes. This is achieved through a combination of training workshops that promote management practices that support and assist natural recovery processes, using a range of native tree species as inputs of conservation value, and the establishment of demonstration plots of land degradation.

### 16. For large-scale and restoration projects

For large-scale and restoration projects, especially on communal lands, SABURAGWA seeks to use modern equipment such as ploughing machines: "Barren lands are ploughed using tractors and Deltino ploughs, which work by creating a pattern system of micro-basins and underground storage areas that retain water and nutrients for the growth of trees."

### 17. In relation to equipment/machinery

In relation to equipment/machinery, SABURAGWA seeks to facilitate the development of private sector companies in an innovative way: Rather than purchasing the equipment for earth excavation and similar works, the program seeks to support private sector and local business enterprises that can provide the necessary services at a lower cost. This is "over" with related potential problems of maintenance neglect; by hiring others to provide equipment and working on a contract basis, it allows for better management of the equipment, training and capacity development." Boosting such provider services in the agricultural/land management sector could qualify as an area of investment in the private sector.

### 18. In this context

In this context, it should be noted that the achievements in many parts of the Sahel of farmer-managed natural regeneration are mentioned only once in the funding proposal (in paragraph 20). This is because the project seeks to promote more sustainable ways to regenerate and multiply valuable trees whose roots already lay under the land surface. It is important to note, however, that this approach is consistent with the agroforestry approach and allows farmer-led approaches to flourish and benefit from the program.

### 19. As FAD is one of the leading agencies

As FAD is one of the leading agencies producing research on land restoration methodologies, the ITAF suggests that SABURAGWA could contribute to improving knowledge

### Sustainable development

#### Scale: High

This broad programme contributes to a range of Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs), most notably SDG 1 aimed at eradicating poverty; SDG 2 on reduced hunger; SDG 3 on gender equality, and SDG 5 on gender equality. It also contributes to SDG 6 on clean water and sanitation, SDG 15 on land-based ecosystem protection, and SDG 17 encouraging partnerships.

#### Environmental co-benefits

SURAGGA invests in participatory land restoration and subsequent rehabilitation of degraded lands, including the reclamation of drylands against erosion and desertification. The programme will rehabilitate selected sites through deep ploughing to improve soil structure, increase water infiltration and water retention, while loosening the soil and improving nutrient access. Tree and grass species will be planted in areas where they are absent or have been lost, increasing cover with native trees and grass species, providing habitat for pollinators and other animals in the area.

The funding proposal indicates that 25 million of the 100 million people who live in the Sahel rely on pastoralist livelihoods. Poverty is a great concern, and social and political tensions stand to increase as competition over land resources intensifies. This is particularly true if and when there is competition for or alternative uses of the land. The competition between herders and farmers for resources and land intensifies with drought and soil erosion.


---
**Page 173**
---

feed scarcity, open conflict may be triggered, for example if livestock stray into fields with standing crops.

26. Social co-benefits are critical for poverty alleviation and stabilization in the area. Water conservation investments are expected to improve food security and crop/pasture yields while conserving water resources and improving soil fertility, thereby enhancing farmers' livelihoods. Rehabilitation of degraded pastures will include working with communities to establish grazing areas that allow livestock access to a greater range of naturalized tree, shrub and herbaceous species. Clarifying use rights and allowing people to grow and sell crops on their land is expected to increase the income from agriculture. It is expected that this sedentary mixed herder and farming communities will benefit from SORAGOWA's comprehensive and participatory strategy of community involvement. The framework for the project includes a gender action plan (GAP) and an integrated approach that TAP suggests that the programme invest even further in knowledge management, including independent monitoring.

25. In order to generate economic co-benefits, income diversification will be critical. It is expected that SORAGOWA will enhance the incomes of smallholder farmers, increase production of crops on degraded farmland and increase the production of livestock and ruminant animals. The sale of seeds and seedlings, stand to provide important income diversification opportunities for local communities, including the local private sector in the SORAGOWA intervention areas. The market for these products will be enhanced by providing training in improved soil and better collection, storage, transport and processing techniques to enhance the value of the produce.

24. According to the gender action plan (annex B), building on gender assessments in the eight countries, women in the Sahel are disproportionately affected by increasing food insecurity, poverty and climate change, and are more vulnerable to economic and environmental shocks. Women provide 80 per cent of agricultural labour but undertake most of the responsibility for household work and child care. They also contribute a significant part of the food production through informal jobs and subsistence farming. Often unpaid, or poorly compensated, women's contributions to agriculture and rural development are underrepresented in the political sphere: "Women occupy only 421 seats in West African parliaments, representing 18.1% of all lawmakers" (Annan & Quayson, 2015).

23. The TAP gender action plan for SORAGOWA focuses on enhancing women's income and employment opportunities through NTFP where mostly women are engaged in the collection and sale of non-timber forest products. It will also enhance the capacities of women engaged with livestock, crops and NTFPs. The programme will also challenge existing gender stereotypes and promote the adoption of new roles and rights for women in the region. Women's knowledge systems and preferences for communication should be built upon when constructing a system of information-sharing in the programme.

22. As part of the TAP approach to SORAGOWA, the identification of project implementation processes implies taking into consideration limitations in terms of access to extension services and institutional credit, as well as barriers related to land tenure. For example, in contexts where women have no control over land or are not allowed to own it, their ability to manage the land areas women own or control tend to be smaller and of lower quality. Understanding the roles and responsibilities of men and women with respect to land ownership and management is a primary requirement for achieving effective outcomes in addressing land degradation.

21. The inclusive and participatory identification of specific intervention sites and a diverse range of locally appropriate native species are very important for the sustainable development

1.4 Needs of the recipient Scale: High

The participating Sahel countries include semi-arid areas in the Great Green Wall belt that are characterized by low rainfall and poor soil fertility, which limits their economic potential and revenue bases, so have little leeway for managing the effects of climate change, including higher temperatures and more frequent droughts. The vulnerability is further exacerbated by a high Global Adaptation Index showing high vulnerability to climate disruption and simultaneously a lack of resources.

The debt sustainability profile of the Sahel countries is low and the ratio of debt to gross domestic product (GDP) are very high, according to the finding proposal (paragraph 221). Figure 1.1 shows that the Sahel countries have the highest debt-to-GDP ratios among all programme countries except Nigeria (37 per cent) and Djibouti (43 per cent). The highest ratio is presented for Senegal with a debt-to-GDP ratio as high as 73 per cent.

The Sahel region, which includes semi-arid pastoral communities living in semi-arid regions, are extremely vulnerable to climate change and climate variability. In the last decade, the frequency of extreme weather events has increased; this has increased the frequency, intensity and duration of droughts and floods, rises in temperatures, and more frequent heat waves. These have had a significant impact on livestock assets, crops and livestock, disruptions in value chains and securing food prices. Combined with the impact of the coronavirus disease 2019 in the last few years, these events have pushed millions of households into poverty.

Altogether, the needs of the recipient are assessed to be high. The proposed project is also presented as having important steps towards addressing these challenges by promoting strengthening of institutions and capacity building for climate change adaptation, restoring and effectively managing dryland ecosystems to maximize their role in effective adaptation to climate change.


1.5 Country ownership Scale: Medium to high

Country ownership and countries' capacity to implement development programmes, may be challenging in the Sahel region. Many countries face, or risk facing, significant political instability, which can make it difficult for them to effectively engage with all participating countries and continue to be fully in line with climate change policies and commitments.

All the countries in the Sahel have ratified the United Nations Convention on Biological Diversity, the Convention to Combat Desertification, the Framework Convention on Climate Change and the Paris Agreement. By ratifying the UNFCCC these countries have committed to implementing national climate change strategies and action plans. In addition to their commitments submitted to the UNFCCC, the Sahel countries have prepared national climate change-related strategies, policies and actions (funding proposal, paragraph 220).

While many of the countries in the Sahel region have, they have still committed to reduce emissions. At the end of 2015, all the Sahel countries submitted their intended nationally determined contributions (INDCs) under the Paris Agreement. However, these INDCs were very limited and focused on reducing emissions; however they stressed their vulnerability to climate change, and especially in relation to food security. Proposed adaptation actions reflect each country's priorities, but there are common

needs for the restoration of degraded lands; afforestation/reforestation/agroforestry and improved pasture; climate-smart conservation agriculture (including improved seeds) and crop diversification; rotational grazing for livestock, enhancing productivity through improved husbandry practices; integrated pest management; soil health improvement through organic livestock; water harvesting and water efficiency enhancement; and early warning systems (funding proposal, paragraph 225).

The Food and Agriculture Agency, FAO, sees participation as the precursor to ownership. Hence, the main strategy for building ownership at various levels relies on participatory processes. At the national level, during the policy analysis and formulation stage, there was close participation through regional and country-level workshops, individual interviews, and extensive stakeholder consultations (annex 72). Annexes 73–75 summarize consultation activities in each of the countries. The FAO also conducted a global roundtable on climate change identification during concept development in 2018 and 2019, and subsequent consultations at global regional and national levels were held to ensure that lessons learned from the pilot phase during implementation in order to identify specific intervention areas, technologies and communication strategies.

Part One

I. Executive Summary

Women in the Sahel are disproportionately affected by increasing food insecurity, protracted crises, dire socio-economic conditions, and gender inequality. They represent a significant proportion of the agricultural labour force in the Sahel and, at the same time, they continue to have most of the responsibilities at home and perform most of the unpaid work. African women in the Sahel contribute from 60% to 85% of the food production, mostly working in informal jobs and subsistence farming in comparison to men.

**Discrimination and Inequality:** The key gender indicators in the Sahel region show that the discrimination against women within the family ranged from 43% to 88%, based on factors such as legal frameworks for marriage against women, the sex ratio at birth and reproductive rights. Socio-economic factors show several gender inequalities in the region, such as low mean age for first marriage, female genital mutilation (FGM), high pregnancy rates, and limited access to education, health services, decision-making, and leadership. This suggests a lack of law enforcement, and how customary frameworks can dictate people's behaviour. There is also a significant gender gap in terms of education, indicating the disadvantage women face relative to men.

**Representation and Decision-Making Power:** Women continue to have a minor representation in all decision-making and leadership positions in the West Africa region. The lack of women’s representation in government has negative impacts not only on policies and programs in place at the national level, but also on changes in societal perceptions. When women are underrepresented in political spheres and decision-making processes, it perpetuates gender inequality, which are the social norms and rules that govern the country cannot be challenged.

**Access to Resources:** Women face the greatest challenges in accessing productive resources although they play an important role in agriculture in the region. Women and specifically rural women face tremendous inequalities in terms of access to and ownership of land; access to inputs, extension services; financial services; technologies, trainings, and markets. Additionally, women are underrepresented in off-farm sectors where they may have better access to the resources needed to develop their activities. These challenges are some of the main drivers that exacerbate gender inequality and disempowering women to improving their livelihoods. Furthermore, the economics of the region and the region’s agriculture production is harmed, and women's fair and profitable involvement in intra-African agrarian commerce, regional, and international agricultural value chains is hampered.

3


---
**Page 183**
---

# Legal Frameworks and Enabling Environment

There has been an improvement in conditions over time regarding the legal and policy environment for women, some countries made more progress than others. Despite these legal improvements, women still face significant challenges, particularly in rural areas where traditional practices and beliefs often limit their freedom and opportunities. In many cases, customary law is recognized by national legal systems and can be used to resolve disputes where there is no formal law in place. However, customary laws can also perpetuate gender inequalities.

## Vulnerability to Climate and Gender-Specific Climate Impacts

The vulnerable climatic nature of Sahel region has gender implications that exacerbate women's vulnerability and have consequences not only in women's livelihoods but on food security, malnutrition, weakened adaptive tools, work burden and poverty in rural communities. The extreme weather events can cause extensive crop damage and affect more severely the most vulnerable groups such as women and children. Time is a critical and essential factor for adaptability to climate change shocks is the time available. Women tend to balance reproductive roles such as cooking, cleaning, and childrearing in addition to agricultural responsibilities. Rural households may shift labor patterns in response to heat stress in ways that increase women's work burden, but this can also be an opportunity for women to engage in income-generating activities during low-lying levels despite heat events. The time constraints women have can limit their ability to engage and participate in decision-making processes and other activities related to climate change adaptation. Also, it may be difficult for women to access training opportunities because of the gender biases that prevent them from engaging in training where men are the main trainers. Climate-induced migration in rural areas often affects women disproportionately and their ability to cope with climate change shocks. Women farmers are more likely to be left behind and struggle to manage agricultural activities independently.

All of the above circumstances hinder women's ability to invest in sustainable agricultural practices, diversify their income, and build resilience to climate shocks. The limited access to agricultural services also makes women more likely to rely on rain-fed agriculture, particularly vulnerable to droughts and other climate-related hazards.

## Women and girls bear a disproportionate brunt of the impacts when land is depleted and limited in availability. This is because of their significant involvement in agriculture and food production, reliance on forests, susceptibility to poverty, lack of education, and lower legal protections and social status. Despite of the increasing acknowledgment of the importance of gender equality in land tenure, distribution, poverty reduction, land preservation, and gender integration, the gender aspect in frequently disregarded in the execution of land-restitution initiatives. As a result, gender aspects are either overlooked or neglected in restoration practices.

Including women in land restoration processes implies taking into consideration their limitations in terms of external factors such as institutional credit, and barriers related to land tenure. In as much of low-income countries, women's ability to own, control, benefit from and access land in the Sahel region is significantly more limited than their male counterparts. Although evidence is still limited, available data


---
**Page 184**
---

from the World Bank show that percentage of land owned by women is always smaller than men in the same countries. On average, the land women own or control tends to be smaller and poorer quality. In many countries, women are responsible for food production and care for their children and other relatives as wives, mothers, or daughters. Understanding the roles and responsibilities of men and women, along with power relations in land management, is a primary requirement to achieving effective outcomes when combating land degradation and implementing gender-responsive and sustainable land-restoration initiatives.

## II. SURAGGWA Overview

The eight countries of the Great Green Wall (GGW) are among the world's poorest and most vulnerable to climate change, with women and girls often disproportionately affected. These countries ranked at the bottom of the Human Development Index in 2020, and a large majority of their population lacks access to employment opportunities or social services. Agriculture, livestock production, fishing, artisanal mining, and forestry activities form the basis of their economies, with over 70% of rural communities, including many women-led households, depending on rainfed agriculture. The Sahel has experienced some of the most extreme climate events on earth in the 20th century. Long-term climate change trends show higher temperatures and more frequent and intense droughts, floods, and heatwaves, as well as more extreme events. The degradation of ecosystems and depletion of vegetation and biodiversity has undermined livelihoods, disproportionately affecting women, and increased vulnerability. This has severely impacted the Sahel's ability to sustain natural landscapes, exacerbating food and nutrition insecurity and compromising the sustainability of livelihoods.

The Scaling-Up Resilience in Africa’s Great Green Wall (SURAGGWA) programme aims to advance the objectives of the GGW, an African-led initiative to protect the continent from desertification by restoring natural vegetation across an 8000 km area and a 15 km belt. The programme has evolved to focus not only on tree planting but also on holistic development in the area, addressing the unique needs and opportunities for each country. Sustainability is achieved through improved livelihoods, food security, political and climate resilience in eight Sahel countries recognized as the most vulnerable to climate change in Africa, including Burkina Faso, Chad, Djibouti, Mali, Mauritania, Niger, Nigeria, and Senegal. Additionally, the programme aims to build capacity for sustainable land management through improved access to financial resources, and knowledge management. SURAGGWA builds on FAO's previous support to Sahelian countries through the Action Against Desertification (AAD) initiative, the GEF/World Bank, African Development Bank, and FAO’s Operation Access.

SURAGGWA proposes three key interventions designed to address the technical, organizational, and financial barriers to alleviate climate change impacts and heighten resilience of local communities:

- Develop a programme to scale-up successful restoration practices, promote biodiversity, regenerate native species, and sequester carbon, with inclusion of gender-responsive activities.


---
**Page 185**
---

* Support the development of climate-resilient, low emission non-timber forest product value chains benefiting vulnerable communities' livelihoods, with inclusion of women's economic empowerment and food security in mind.
* Strengthening the Great Green Wall's regional and national institutions to secure the sustainability of interventions and scale up successful practices, with inclusion of gender-responsive policies and programs.

**For a full programme description, please refer to the Funding Proposal document.**

The programme will work with community organizations to build ownership and sustainability while strengthening community awareness and capacity in understanding ecological elements of their environment. It will empower women's participation and leadership, including training and building awareness on addressing climate change and land degradation issues. By connecting with existing financial mechanisms and innovative financing models, the programme will engage targeted groups, including women, in the GGW area. The program will establish linkages with existing providers, create innovative models of value chain financing, and de-risk lending to smallholders in the region.

The transformational activities of SURAGAWA will support the implementation of the GGW initiative led by the African Union, aiming to transform landscapes by restoring 100 million hectares, creating 10 million jobs, and generating $25 billion annually. The proposed programme (SURAGAWA) in the GGW area will make a direct contribution to the GGW targets through the Green Climate Fund's pledge at the Rio Planet Summit (January 2021) and catalyse changes needed to achieve these ambitious targets. The proposed program will restore approximately 1,334 million ha of degraded lands, benefiting 7.6 million vulnerable people in the GGW area. This is equivalent to removing 589 million tons of CO2 over 10 years or 65 million tons of CO2 over the programme lifespan (20 years). The program will make a significant contribution to: (i) the countries' Nationally Determined Contributions (NDCs) to climate change mitigation and adaptation; (ii) the African Union's pledge under the Africa Forest Landscape Restoration Initiative (AFR100) in committed countries, i.e. the concerted UN system Action plan in support of the GGW; (iii) the UN Decade on Ecosystem Restoration 2021-2030 with GGW as one of its flagship initiatives; and (iv) the United Nations Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs), including SDG Poverty (SDG 1), Zero Hunger (SDG 2), Gender Equality (SDG 5), Decent Work and Economic Growth (SDG 8), Climate Action (SDG 13), Life on Land (SDG 15), and Partnerships (SDG 17). This document and the accompanying Gender Action Plan ensure that women and girls are empowered and benefit equitably from the climate change mitigation and adaptation efforts through the programme.

### III. Objectives of the gender assessment

Gender equality is a fundamental requirement for achieving sustainable development and climate resilience. Climate change affects women and men differently due to differences in gender roles, responsibilities, and access to resources. Women often face higher risks and greater burdens, particularly in areas like agriculture and water. In addition, for conflict areas like the Sahel, women-led households – which are common – are particularly vulnerable because of their lack of customary rights to land, wells, and livestock.1,2 The GCF recognises that gender equality is a vital component of climate action.3 The SURAGAWA programme will address these disparities and provide empowerment opportunities for women to enable communities in the Sahel to sustainably adapt to the challenges posed by climate change.

The GCF recognises the importance of gender equality and women’s empowerment in addressing climate change. As a result, GCF has integrated gender perspectives into its operations, funding, and decision-making processes. The FAO also shares the same view and commitments, as indicated in FAO's Policy on Gender Equality 2020-2030, which states that, “all men and women, in particular the poor and the vulnerable, have equal rights to economic resources, as well as access to basic services.”4 This assessment is designed to provide a gender analysis for the Scaling Up Resilience in Africa’s Green Great Wall (SURAGAWA) programme.5 The GCF has developed a Gender Policy6 which mandates the integration of gender considerations into all GCF-funded programmes and projects to promote equal access to resources, benefits, and decision-making opportunities.7 This policy also requires that all GCF funds used for programmes and projects be gender responsive, acknowledging that climate change disproportionately affects women- and marginalised groups due to existing social and economic inequalities.

This Gender Analysis/Assessment for the GCF programme, “Scaling Up Resilience in Africa’s Green Great Wall (SURAGAWA)” initiative aims to identify and address gender-specific barriers and opportunities in the agriculture and rural development sectors within the target countries: Burkina Faso, Chad, Djibouti, Mali, Mauritania, Niger, Senegal, and Sudan.8 The analysis, aligned with the GCF Gender Policy components, provides the foundation for effective gender mainstreaming, providing an overview of the status of gender equality across the eight Sahelian countries covered by the SURAGAWA programme. The following chapters provide a gender analysis of the two main components of the programme: Component 1 (Resilience) and agricultural value chains, especially related to Non-Timber Forest Products (NTFP), where data are available (Component 2). The assessment concludes with recommendations for mainstreaming gender within the programme design and during implementation, via the Gender Action Plan.


---
**Page 187**
---

Plan (GAP). The GAP details specific activities to address gender inequalities in the region and empower women and ensures that all activities across the three components reach specific gender targets.

The primary objectives of this Gender Assessment are to:
- Identify gender disparities in climate resilience, agriculture, and rural livelihoods, highlighting the unique challenges faced by women, men, and vulnerable groups in the Great Green Wall region.
- Assess the extent to which current policies and support hinder or enhance gender equality in climate adaptation and mitigation efforts.
- Provide actionable recommendations to ensure the SURAGGWA programme integrates gender-responsive approaches that enhance women's empowerment and equitable participation in climate resilience initiatives.
- Align with international commitments such as the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) Gender Action Plan and the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs 5 & 13), reinforcing the role of gender equality in climate adaptation efforts.1

Methodology
This Gender Assessment is based on a multi-dimensional approach, drawing from:
(i) Desk review of:
- Relevant literature on gender, forestry, and climate change in GGW countries (e.g., recent research reports, and data sources from the United Nations, World Bank, NGOs, and other development agencies addressing gender equality and climate resilience in the GGW region), particularly eight country-specific gender profiles prepared collaboratively by the Food and Agriculture Organization (FAO) and the Economic Community of West African States (ECOWAS).
- National policies and legal frameworks on gender, agriculture, and climate adaptation in the target countries.

(ii) Primary data collected through field-level consultations. Field-level consultations supporting this analysis and the related GAP were held with a variety of stakeholders, including women in local communities; women's associations; civil society organizations; representatives from relevant government agencies at all levels of country administration (including the GGW Agency); the National Designated Authority of the GGW; ethnic minority groups (including women from those groups); and other programme teams operating in the area. These consultations (detailed in Annex 1) aimed to ensure that the analysis was based on a deep understanding of the role of women in the GGW region and the constraints they face. The consultations and review helped to identify key gender issues, vulnerabilities, and capacities within the context of land restoration and climate adaptation, as they relate to programme components and activities.

By conducting this Gender Assessment and developing the related GAP, the SURAGGWA programme will ensure the implementation of gender-transformative strategies, helping bridge gender gaps in agriculture, enhance women's participation in decision-making, and build climate-resilient communities, thus driving green transformation.

### IV. Regional overview of gender inequalities and the status of women in the Sahel

The Sahel region is a vast semi-arid strip of land that stretches across several countries in West and Central Africa. According to recent estimates the region has a total population of approximately 30 million people, with a slightly higher proportion of men than women. Despite high mortality rates of children under five years old, life expectancy for both sexes has increased from around 45 years in 1960 to doubled its population by 2050[1]. The Sahel region has a relatively young population, with 64.5% of individuals under the age of 25. However, there are some variations between countries and regions within the Sahel with implications on gender. For instance, in areas where there are more girls than boys in the younger age groups, this is often due to higher female mortality rates or high fertility rates. High birth rates coupled with low access to maternal and reproductive health care in Niger. About 76% of girls are married before the age of 18 and female genital mutilations remain recurrent with prevalence rates up to 9 out of 10 excited women[2].

Regarding the socio-economic situation, countries in the Sahel share common economic characteristics; yet their national economies vary in size. According to the World Bank classification, the programme countries range from low-income economies to lower-middle income economies, with the former being among the least developed countries in the world[3]. Agriculture represents the main sector of the regional economy, in terms of employment and value addition, contributing to an estimated 35% of the regional gross domestic product (GDP).

Women in the Sahel are more affected by their socio-economic status due to their engagements in informal and subsistence agriculture in comparison to men who are more likely to be engaged in wage labor and other formal sector activities. According to reports, women provide 80% of agricultural labour force in the Sahel[4], and at the same time, they continue to have most of the responsibilities at home and perform most of the unpaid work, such as cooking, child care, and double burden of work with women in addition are estimated to spend up to three times more than men hours on household and related activities. In addition, African women in the Sahel contribute from 60 to 80% of the food production. Nonetheless, while being a well-known issue, women face the greatest challenges in accessing productive resources (Noelle & Kaasia). Women’s access to land is limited due to cultural norms and traditions that restrict their right to own or inherit land. It is recorded by the FAO that women's restricted access to production means and inability to leverage prospects in agriculture is to blame for the sector poor performance in low and middle-income economies (Noelle & Kaasia). Restricted access in this respect, it faced more exceedingly by women than men, including a stronger gender gap. As a result, the region’s production is harmed, and women's far


---
**Page 190**
---

and profitable involvement in intra-African agrarian commerce, regional, and international agricultural value chains is hampered (Noble & Kaaria).

Despite their distinctive role in productive, generative, and communal management actions, women continue to have a minor representation in decision-making and leadership positions. Laws and policies that regulate formal institutions, as well as gender and cultural norms continue to prevent women to actively participate in the economy. Women are also more vulnerable than men not only because of lack of education and literacy but also due to gender-based violence at home including forced marriages and sexual exploitation (UNDP, 2022)

Women's participation in climate effort both at the regional and national levels is also very limited (Dankelman, 2015). Climate change has thus moved towards gender equality by exacerbating poverty, reinforcing existing inequalities of discrimination, and driving up gender-based violence worldwide. This is particularly acute in the Sahel due to its high dependence on rain-fed agriculture and therefore vulnerable to climate change. Farmers face growing challenges in maintaining subsistence agriculture due to recurring periods of droughts, desertification and floods. These extreme weather events can cause extensive crop failures that affect more severely female farmers who are usually deprived of several adaptation mechanisms (UNHCR, 2017).

While local food production available for consumption has increased, it remains insufficient in a number of countries and rates of undernourishment and malnutrition remain very high. At the end of 2022, provisional Cadre Harmonisé indicates that 28.9 million people are in need of immediate food assistance (UNHCR, 2017). In Sub-Saharan Africa, acute food insecurity exceeds the emergency threshold (15%) in Senegal, Mauritania, Niger and northeastern Nigeria. Moreover, in this region of West Africa, the obesity prevalence rates in were 5 percent among men and 15 percent among women (adults) [PFRE, 2015 cited in FAO, 2017]. In nearly all countries in the subregion, the prevalence of obesity is increasing. The same trend has been observed for hypertension, diabetes, heart disease and hypertension among men and women alike. Security tensions and violence, inflation, extreme poverty, particularly in conflict zones, are also major factors that contribute to the deterioration of nutrition and health situation in severely compromised and worsening amid colliding crisis of conflict, climate change, and COVID-19.

Despite all the difficult conditions described above, women continue to play a decisive role in household food security, food diversification and the nutritional status and health of children. In nearly all West African countries, women prepare most of the meals consumed in the household; hence their central role in ensuring a healthy and balanced diet for family members, especially in families living in coastal communities.

Key gender inequalities in the Sahel region

Like many other African countries, gender equality remains a significant issue in the countries of the Sahel region. To gain a better understanding of the situation faced by women in the region, various gender indicators are used. One such indicator is the Social Institutions Gender Index (SIGI) from OECD Stats portal, which assesses the level of discrimination against women in each country. According to the SIGI 2015 report, Burkina Faso has the lowest overall SIGI score of 3 percent, indicating a very high level of gender-based discrimination across all four areas measured by the SIGI, with particularly high scores for discrimination in the family and restricted access to productive and financial resources. Senegal has a relatively low overall SIGI score of 12 percent, which is also high compared to other countries. Chad has an overall SIGI score of 30 percent, indicating lower gender-based discrimination compared to other countries. Djibouti has no data available, but its score for discrimination in the family is high.

Another significant gender indicator is the Global Gender Gap Index, which benchmarks gender parity across four key areas: economic participation and opportunity, education attainment, health, and political empowerment. Table 1 provides rankings for seven of the eight countries in the Sahel region. Chad and Mali have the largest gender gaps in the Sahel region ranking 141st and 142nd out of 146 countries, globally (World Economic Forum 2012).###

In countries such as Mali, there are 162 births per 1,000 adolescent women, having important consequences on women's health and their ability to complete their education.

There is also a significant gender gap in terms of education. The literacy gender parity index is less than one in all cases, which shows the disadvantage women face relative to men for learning opportunities. ### Women's participation in politics and leadership

Women in West Africa remain underrepresented in the political sphere (figure 2). Women occupy only 421 seats in West African parliaments, representing 16.1% of all lawmakers. In West Africa, out of 17 countries with a parliament, women hold less than one-third of ministerial positions. In Senegal, with women making up 42.76%of its parliament, it ranks as number seven, just behind Sweden. Women occupy on average less than 20% of ministerial posts, and, of those, they are mostly clustered in the ministries covering women's affairs and social issues. Men also outnumber women within ministerial administrations. While women hold a majority of positions at the level of assistant ministers and other administrative positions, men hold most technical and managerial positions. To encourage more women to participate in politics, the region adopted the ECOASW Gender and Electoral Strategic Framework (GESF) and Action Plan (ECAEP). The GESF is a set of guidelines for ECOWAS member states to use as guidelines to update the "Supplementary Act relating to Equality of Rights between Men and Women for Sustainable Development in the ECOWAS Region". In terms of top leadership position, the Commission of the African


---
**Page 194**
---

Union is setting a good example. Its newly elected commission maintains the proper gender balance—four out of eight elected commissioners are women. The previous commission was chaired by Nkosazana Dlamini-Zuma, the first woman to lead the continent’s intergovernmental body. She also became one of the first female heads of state in Africa in 2006, the Liberian President Ellen Johnson Sirleaf, inspired many African nations to sought to achieve gender equality and increase women's participation in decision-making and expand women's economic opportunities.

As mentioned above, women play an important role in agriculture in the region. However, there are significant disparities from one country to another. For example, in Chad, women account for 74% of employment in agriculture and yet only have access to a bank account for 10%. This is because many people view women's role in agriculture as secondary. Interestingly, figure 3 shows that precisely in countries where women's participation in agriculture is higher, the percentage of women who can access a bank account is low. In Mali, for example, women account for 64% of employment in agriculture and yet have only 10% access to a bank account. This situation jeopardizes women's ability to receive and manage their income.

Women dominate employment in off-farm segments, including food-away-from-home, food processing and food marketing. Yet, they rarely have access to the resources needed to develop their activities. An analysis of the rice sector in Benin, Niger and Nigeria highlights existing gender disparities in trade networks and value chains. Women are more likely than men to be excluded from participation in trade, poorer access to information and markets, male-dominated distribution networks, time and mobility constraints, lower education levels, greater difficulties in complying with regulatory and procedural requirements, etc. Women are less likely thus to hold strategic positions and are less well-connected to central actors. However, women's employment in food value chains can offer immense opportunities for women and young people. However, more evidence, strategic thinking and integrated approaches are needed for more effective policies.

# Legal status of women

## Policy and legal frameworks that incorporate gender considerations in the design and implementation are fundamental for closing the gender gap in low- and middle-income countries. Evidence suggests that there have been improvements in gender responsiveness in national policies.

The Women, Business, and the Law database of the World Bank (WBL) is an indicator for global progress in terms of policy and legal frameworks related to women's economic opportunities, including labor force participation, property, marriage, parenthood, entrepreneurship, assets and pension.

In all the countries of the region, there has been an improvement in conditions over time (Figure 4), with the sole exception of Niger. In Niger, there are serious areas for improvement in terms of the legal status of women. For example, to improve on the property indicator, this country could consider granting spouses equal administrative authority over real property during marriage and after divorce; grant heirs rights for heritage rights to real property; equalize inheritance rights of sons and daughters; equalized in hereditary rights; recognize the value of male and female surviving spouses; grant spouses equal administrative authority over the property during marriage, and recognize the value of non-monetary contributions.

It is important to highlight the score of Burkina Faso and Senegal, who are the two economies of Sahel with better policy framework conditions for women than the mean of SSA. In Burkina Faso, when it comes to laws that affect a woman's actions to work, gender differences in property and inheritance and women's pensions, the improvements have been notable. However, some aspects such as freedom to movements are still affecting gender equality.

Despite some positive development, women in the Sahel region continue to face significant challenges, particularly in rural areas where traditional practices and beliefs often limit their freedom and opportunities. In many cases, customary law is recognized by national legal systems and can be used to resolve disputes where there is no formal law in place. However, customary laws can also perpetuate discrimination against women and girls. For example, in many countries, many customary laws contribute to continue practices such as child marriage and female genital mutilation, even though those are considered criminal offenses by the legal statutory systems in the country.
